...intensify <note>original: "asperate"</note> the heat trapped within (even in those things that are watery and putrid) so that at length it breaks out violently, as wet hay stored away (when it cannot ventilate <note>original: "transpiration"</note>) shows. When the blood is kindled within, it becomes a *fever*; when under the skin, it is *St. Anthony's Fire* <note>A historical name for Erysipelas, a bacterial skin infection characterized by a bright red rash.</note>.

The general cure is bloodletting <note>original: "the opening of a vein"</note> and cooling. But since the fever is a very common disease and of many kinds, something more must be said about it.

XII **The fever, so called from its fervency or heat, is of three kinds.** 1. The *Ephemera*. 2. The *Putrid*. 3. The *Hectic*. **The first burns the spirits; the second the humours; the third the solid parts.** The first is like a raging hot wind scorching all it meets; the second is like boiling water poured into a vessel, which it heats along with itself. The third is like a hot vessel itself, heating the water poured into it.

For the *Hectic* fever affects the bones and membranes, and eats and consumes them with an unnatural heat, by degrees almost imperceptibly, until at length it causes death. It is very similar to Consumption <note>A historical term for wasting diseases, most commonly referring to tuberculosis.</note>. But the putrid or rotten fever affects the blood and humours <term>the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) once thought to determine a person's health and temperament</term>; by which the whole body

grows hot. The *Ephemera* <note>original: "Ephemera"; from the Greek word for "lasting a day"</note> is a more subtle flame, feeding only upon the spirits. Because of this, it rarely lasts more than one or two days, until the spirit itself consumes the <term>peccant</term> <note>harmful or diseased</note> cause. Consequently, health or death usually follows within two or three days; for this reason, it is called the *Ephemera* or *diary fever* <note>original: "diary Feaver"; a fever that runs its course in a single day</note>, and also the *malignant fever*. The pestilential infection <note>likely referring to the plague or similar highly contagious diseases</note> is also of this type, for it develops in the same way.

*Putrid fevers* are the most common, but they vary greatly. When the <term>humours</term> <note>the four vital fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile, which were believed to control health</note> rot within their vessels (or "workhouses"), especially near the heart (such as in the liver or the gallbladder), the spirit rises against them and ignites them. It does not stop attacking them until it either expels the rot—which is turned into <term>soot</term> <note>Archaic medical theory held that fevers burned up "bad" humors, creating smoky or soot-like waste products called fuliginous vapors</note>—or until the spirit itself is extinguished. Therefore, this fever is often deadly; it is called the *continual fever*.

However, if the humours rot outside of their vessels—that is, in the veins or limbs—it is an *intermitting fever* <note>a fever that comes and goes in cycles, like malaria</note>. For the spirit rises up at specific times and opposes that rot with heat. But because this battle takes place further away from its "castle," the heart, once the fight is ended, it re-

...turns home. And if the *rotting* <unclear>humor</unclear> <term>The theory of the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—governed medicine for centuries. A "humor" was a bodily fluid that caused disease if it became "putrified" or imbalanced.</term> *is* *phlegm*, it still returns to oppose it the next day; this is the cause of the *Quotidian Fever* <term>A fever that recurs daily.</term>. If *it is yellow bile* <note>original: "yellow choler"</note>, then it returns every third day; this is the *Tertian*. If it is *black bile* <note>original: "black choler"</note>, it returns every fourth day; this is the *Quartan*. 

The cause of this inequality in timing is because phlegm gathers itself together the fastest and creates new work for the spirits, but it is also more quickly scattered. Therefore, the Quotidian fever does not last long. Since melancholy <term>Associated with "black bile," melancholy was considered a heavy, thick fluid.</term> is a sluggish, dreg-like humor, it does not recover itself as quickly. However, because it is thick and sticky, it is not easily overcome; this explains the long duration of Quartan fevers. 

In the Tertian fever, because the spirit opposes yellow bile—which is naturally hot—the most intense struggle occurs. Consequently, Tertian fevers are called "burning fevers." These fevers are sometimes transformed into one another or joined together, depending on whether one rotting humor or another must be opposed at any given time.

1. Why does a fever begin with a chill? It is because the vital spirit <term>The "vital spirit" was believed to be a life-giving substance that moved through the body to maintain health and heat.</term>, intending to oppose the infection <note>original: "rottennesse"</note>, gathers heat as its reinforcement from every part of the body. Meanwhile, the outer limbs become cold and numb,

(For even in too much fear, when the spirit <note>original: "spirit"; in Early Modern medicine, "spirits" were subtle fluids or gases believed to circulate through the body to maintain life and function.</note> gathers itself into the internal parts, there is usually a chilliness of the outer limbs and a quaking with cold.)

*2 From where does the heat come afterward?* Because the spirits, after they are hotter with fight and motion, return again to the limbs; which, being cold before, endure the heat much worse, as it returns now hotter than ordinary.

*3 Why does the fever leave faintness behind it?* Because the spirit, wearied with the fight, retreats to rest, leaving the limbs destitute <note>meaning: deprived of strength or vitality</note>.

*4 Why is food harmful at the beginning of a fever?* Because when the spirit is preparing itself for the battle, it has another business put upon it (to digest <term>concoct</term> the food). But seeing that it is not able to do both, it either attacks the disease more weakly, or else leaves the food undigested; or at least, if it does both, it weakens and tires itself out too much.

*5 Why is it dangerous to expel the fever too soon?* Because the fever is in itself a benefit to nature, driving away the corruption <term>rottenness</term> in time, lest it should at length prevail and oppress the heart. *Let this be the summary of what has been said: Indigestion <term>original: "crudity"; the state of undigested food in the stomach, which was believed to be the root of most illnesses</term> is the seed of all diseases. For from that, thick vapors arise and cause bloating <note>original: "inflation"</note>; when these same vapors condense in the head, they cause catarrh <note>original: "distillation"; a flow of fluid or "rheum" from the brain</note>; in the other limbs, they cause blockages <note>original: "obstruction"</note>, from which flows either putrefaction or inflammation. Therefore, let him who prevents indigestion believe this: that he takes the best course possible for his whole body. Now, the way to prevent them is a temperate diet and daily*
<margin>☛</margin> *exercises. O, the strange virtue of labor, by which we obtain both our bread and our health! If the slothful understood this mystery, they would not waste their lives in idleness.*

### Of the Diseases of the Mind.

I. The diseases of the mind are vices, procuring either *disquiet* or *grief* to it.

II. Diseases disquieting the mind are evil desires; that is, too much *intensity* <note>original: "ardency"</note>. 1. *Of Living*. 2. *Of Eating and Drinking*. 3. *Of Multiplying itself* <note>Procreation or sexual desire</note>. 4. *Of Knowing*. 5. *Of Having*. 6. *Of Excelling*.

N.W. These are thus expressed by their proper names: 1. *Self-love*. 2. *Intemperance*. 3. *Lust* <note>original: "salacity"</note>. 4. *Curiosity*. 5. *Covetousness*. 6. *Ambition*. For those who are given to these itch and are disquieted continually.

III. The diseases that cause grief to the mind are *immoderate affections*; that is, *violent alterations...*

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of human emotions and their remedies, likely following a section on the "passions" of the mind (completing the word "passions" from the previous page's catchword).</meta>

[passi]ons <gloss>or</gloss> those things which happen to us according to our desires, or contrary to them: but especially sadness, anguish <note>original: "Angor"</note>, and a weariness <note>original: "Irksomnesse"</note> of life.

IV The remedies of the mind are presented in Ethics <note>original: "the Ethicks"</note>. The summary of which comes to this: *Love the Golden Mean <term>the middle way between two extremes</term>, and avoid extremes like steep cliffs. Never desire to do more than you can. Remember that you are human. For what happens to any person can happen to every person. There is a constant change <note>original: "vicissitude"</note> in all things, and an unconquered mind overcomes all things, etc.*

### Of the Diseases of the Soul.

I The diseases of the soul are: forgetting God, the torment of conscience, and despair of mercy.

II Forgetting God is cured by the fear of God. I mean that God who sees all, judges all, and rewards everyone according to their works. It is impossible to avoid His hand (for in Him we move, live, and have our being), but to endure it is unbearable (for He is a consuming fire, etc.).

III The torment of conscience is healed by prayer and the *pursuit of innocence <note>original: "study of innocency"</note>, Psalm 26:6; Ecclesiastes 12:13, 14.* For if our heart does not condemn us, we have full assurance, etc. *1 John 3:21.*

IV Despair is healed by the blood of that only Lamb of God, who cleanses <note>original: "purgeth"</note> us from all sin (*1 John 1:7*), reconciles us to His Father (*Romans 3:25*), saves us (*Romans 5:9*), and gives us eternal life (*John 6:54*).

# Juan Luis Vives <note>original: Lud. Vives.</note>

*Nature* <note>The OCR reads "Ature" because the first letter "N" was a large decorative woodcut initial.</note> *is not to be examined by the* **lamp** *of the* **Gentiles** <note>In this context, 'Gentiles' refers to non-Christian classical philosophers, particularly those from Ancient Greece and Rome.</note>, *which yields a light that is both dim and harmful* <note>original: "maligne"</note>; *instead, it must be examined by the* **torch** *of the* **Sun**, *which* **Christ** *has brought into the darkness of the* **world**. *And for that very reason, we have found cause to write, lest we should be forced to follow the traditions of the* **Gentiles**, *between whom and us there is so vast a difference in* **religion**. *To tell the truth, just as* **Aristotle’s** *writings possess much learning and ingenuity, they also contain much obscurity. This has given some the occasion to search for—and imagine they see—things that exist nowhere, and so forth.*

# To those truly studious of wisdom, from Christ
## the fountain of wisdom, greeting.

*Jacobus Acontius <note>original: "Iacobus Acontius" (1492–1566), an Italian jurist and theologian famous for his work on religious tolerance and logic.</note>, a most excellent man, was offended by the evil disposition of our "scribbling age." He wished that it might be provided that no one should write and publish anything unless it were something new. This work should both be based on the author's own observation and contribute to the glory of God and the edification <note>the building up or spiritual improvement</note> of the Church. He hoped that so much fruit <note>beneficial results</note> might be expected from such writing that the time readers spent on it could not be better spent elsewhere. His goal was that nothing would be repeated that was already done, focusing instead only on what was yet to be accomplished. For few writers (he says) bring any*

# The Preface.

*...thing of their own: but only steal things and words, from which they make books, etc.*

Those who must read through that *medley* <note>original: "farrago," a confused mixture or hodgepodge</note> of books—with which we are almost overwhelmed every year—know this to be most truly spoken. If you look at the titles, they always seem new and very impressive. However, if you look at the actual content, it is always the same material boiled over and over more than a thousand times. It is like <term>reheated cabbage</term> <note>original: "Coleworts," a type of wild cabbage; used here as a metaphor for stale, recycled ideas</note> crammed in until it causes nausea.

Even when a new observation is offered, what is the purpose of writing entire books for it? These new discoveries are so buried among ordinary things that a reader either has no desire to search for what is new in them, or cannot do so without weariness of spirit and a loss of time.

But it is not my business to complain against this disorder at great length. I will now declare why I am publishing this work myself. And I will

### The Preface.

<sig>4</sig>
<meta>catchword: ving</meta>

To state it briefly, I bring something new and different from the standard approach to *philosophy*. I present it in such a way that I hope it will cause no one any trouble or annoyance, as it contains matters of very great importance within just a few pages. 

I also bring this work to satisfy the desires of others. For last year, I provided a proof of my philological <note>original: Philologicall; relating to the study of language and literature</note> endeavors, *The Gate of Tongues Unlocked* <note>original: Janua Linguarum referata; a famous language textbook by Jan Amos Comenius, first published in 1631</note> (or a *seminary of Arts and Languages*). That work was graciously received with applause and approved by the judgment of almost everyone, as evidenced by several letters concerning the matter sent to me or my friends. 

Consequently, some of those who are currently directing their desires, thoughts, and endeavors toward reforming the methods of study began to urge me to publish my philosophical works, or at least to share my ideas, especially regarding *physics* <note>original: Phyſicks; in this period, "physics" referred generally to "natural philosophy," the study of the physical world and the laws of nature</note>. Ha- <note>The text continues on the next page with the word "Having."</note>

# The Preface.

Having no other intention, therefore, but to contribute something on my own part that may be useful, if possible—or at least to provide an opportunity for others to produce better material—I intended to publish this same *Synopsis of Physics*, which was recently lectured <note>original: "dictated"</note> in this school. My goal is that this work, like my previous one, might be subject to public evaluation <note>original: "censure," meaning critical review or judgment</note>.

To facilitate this, it seemed appropriate to provide further information regarding the reason for and scope of our project to those who will act as our reviewers.

After the unfortunate fate of exile forced me—who was by profession a theologian <note>original: "Divine"</note>—back into the service of the school, I desired to conduct myself not carelessly, but in such a way that I might fulfill the responsibilities entrusted to me. It happened that I came across, among other things, the books of *Ludovicus Vives* <note>Juan Luis Vives (1493–1540) was a Spanish scholar and humanist often called the "father of modern psychology."</note> titled *On the Transmission of Learning* <note>original Latin: *de tradendis disciplinis*</note>. In

# The Preface.

When I had found these most wholesome counsels for the restoration of philosophy and the entire course of studies, I began to grieve deeply that a man of such sharp intellect <note>original: "piercing a wit"</note>, after he had noted so many obvious errors, had not set his hand to smoothing those rough paths. However, the judgment of one person concerning this excellent writer—namely, *that Vives saw better what was not than what was* <note>Referring to Juan Luis Vives (1493–1540), a Spanish humanist who criticized medieval scholasticism but was sometimes seen as better at critique than at providing new systems.</note>—made me consider that it is common for the wisdom of God to reveal things by degrees. 

Yet I thought to myself that others should take this as an opportunity to labor to design one certain and infallible path among the many deviations <note>original: "deviacions"</note> revealed to them. I wondered why men were so reluctant to attempt this for a full hundred years—for I did not know whether anyone else had set about it. But it happened that a certain learned man, to whom I shared these complaints of mine in a more

<summary>The author expresses frustration that despite the critiques of scholars like Vives, a definitive new method for philosophy and education has not been established in a century. He reflects on why scholars have been slow to create a certain "path" to replace the errors of the past.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy, education reform, Juan Luis Vives, methodology, intellectual history, pedagogy</keywords>

# The Preface.

...in a familiar manner, showed me a book called *Prodromus Philosophiæ Instaurandæ* <note>original: "Forerunner of the Restoration of Philosophy"</note> by Thomas Campanella, an Italian. # The Preface.

These three principles of knowledge must necessarily be joined together. Otherwise, anyone who follows the guidance of sense alone will never be wiser than the common people; nor will he be able to imagine that the Moon is smaller than a star, that the Sun is larger than the Earth, or that the Earth is spherical and habitable in every direction.

<margin>1</margin> On the contrary, if a man contemplates abstract things and consults only with reason—without the testimony of his senses—he will be carried away by mere fantasies <note>original: "phantasmes"</note> and create a new world for himself, like those of the Platonists and Aristotelians, and so on.

# The Preface.

<margin>—</margin>
<note>original: "...dation" likely "Emendation" or "Accommodation"</note> Correction is not violent, nor does it involve the destruction of the preceding principle. Instead, it is gentle, so that the very thing being corrected acknowledges and admits the correction of its own accord and with joy. It soon brings something of its own to the process, by which the same corrected truth may become more apparent. For example, Reason brings nothing to correct the senses that it does not soon confirm through various experiments; it then affirms itself to be true. (For instance, the sense of touch teaches that an oar <note>original: "Oare"</note> is not actually broken under water, as does the sense of sight itself when looking at the oar after it is pulled out of the water.)

<margin>☞</margin>
Faith presents nothing that is contrary or repugnant to Reason (although it does bring that which is beyond and above Reason). Instead, Faith presents all things in such a way that Reason not only yields to them by being overcome by authority, but also finds them to be true within the nature of things. Thus, Reason seeks and finds something of its own which may serve to <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword for the next page is "con-", likely leading to "confirm" or "contribute".</note>

<margin>☞</margin>

...and profit than anyone can tell us. If we need an interpreter anywhere, the Creator of nature—as we have said—is the best interpreter of Himself. In *Aristotle’s* age, it was barely out of its infancy. In the ages that followed (and especially in our own), it has increased so much through new observations that the Aristotelian <note>original: "Aristotelick"</note> tenets seem obscure and uncertain by comparison; indeed, they even present clear falsehoods. 

*“Suppose then,”* (writes Rudolf Goclenius <note>Rodolphus Goclenius (1547–1628), a German scholastic philosopher known for his work in logic and metaphysics.</note> to Nicolaus Taurellus <note>Nicolaus Taurellus (1547–1606), a German physician and philosopher who sought to separate philosophy from theology and criticized Aristotelianism.</note>) *“that nature has shown the full sharpness of human ingenuity in* Aristotle*; let* Aristotle *be a man who has served all human wisdom better than and above all other mortal men;”*

# The Preface.

# The Preface.

*...rudiments of the world, and not according to Christ. (Galatians 5:8, 1 Peter 2:9, Colossians 2:8.)* <note>The author cites these New Testament verses to warn against human traditions and worldly philosophies that distract from the teachings of Christ.</note>

And indeed, the most miserable state of the Church shortly afterward shows what fruit we gained by coupling *Aristotle* with *Christ*. At that time, everything was full of the noise of disputations—for slippery questions and an itching desire for controversy are the very soul of *Peripateticism* <term>original: "Peripateticisme"; the philosophy of Aristotle and his followers</term>. Heresy sprouted out of heresy, until at length the fumes of human opinions had so completely darkened the brightness of divine wisdom that all things degenerated into Antichristianism. 

In forging articles of faith and ordaining rules of life, *Aristotle* had an equal share with *Christ*—I might even say he held the sole dictatorship. <margin>☞</margin> Our *School divinity* <term>original: "School divinity"; also known as Scholasticism, a method of learning that flourished in medieval universities which relied heavily on Aristotelian logic</term> will give us a very clear view of this fact.

# The Preface.

...philosophy; and neither *Thomas Scotus* <note>original: "Thomas Scotus"; likely referring to the Scholastic philosopher Duns Scotus</note> nor anyone else had any better success. Why, then, do we tolerate it? Why do we not release our minds from those snares? Why do we not throw away those spectacles which show us fantasies instead of realities?

<margin>☛</margin>
Some are afraid that if they let go of Peripatetic Philosophy <note>original: "Peripatetick Philosophy"; the school of philosophy following the teachings of Aristotle</note>, they would have no philosophy at all. It is as if they believe that when *Hagar* was cast out, there would have been no one left to bless *Abraham* with children! Or as if the *Israelites* would have starved with hunger after leaving the *Egyptian* meat-pots; or that *Moses* must have become dull-witted <note>original: "blockish"</note> once he was away from the company of the wise men of *Memphis*! 

On the contrary, that promised Grace will come at last; at last that heavenly *Manna* will rain down; at last we shall be truly taught by God <note>original Greek: "θεοδίδακτοι" (theodidaktoi)</note>, when, turning our ears away from human opinions, we listen to God alone.

But greater care had to be taken regarding *method*: that all things should be delivered to learners in such a way that they find knowledge flowing into them, not being "stuffed" in. # The Preface.

From this it follows that you must necessarily agree with the final conclusions just as much as with the first principles. And truly, there is reason to wonder why a similar approach has not yet been attempted in *metaphysics*, *physics*, and *theology* (since *ethics* and *politics* deal with more unpredictable <note>original: "contingent"</note> matters). I am well aware that there is more visible evidence in *numbers*, *measures*, and *weights* than there is in *qualities* <note>In the science of this period, "qualities" referred to the inherent properties of matter—like heat, cold, or moisture—which were considered harder to measure than physical dimensions.</note>, through which *nature* exerts its power in a hidden way. Nevertheless, I will not say that there is greater certainty in those measurements, seeing that all things are done according to the highest reason, in a continuous order, and as if by an eternal law. Even in *mathematics*, not everything is equally clear; yet they are tested in various ways until they can be reduced to direct observation <note>original Greek: αὐτοψία (autopsia), meaning "seeing for oneself" or direct inspection</note>, as I said before, and presented scientifically. *For someone says nothing in philosophical matters if they prove nothing; and they prove nothing if they do*

# The Preface.

<margin>T</margin>...not so demonstrate it that you cannot contradict it. And now I beg you to let this be our goal: that the schools may stop trying to *persuade* and start to *demonstrate*; stop *disputing* and start *contemplating* <note>original: speculate; here meaning to observe or study the nature of things</note>; and finally, stop *believing* and start *knowing*. 

For that Aristotelian maxim, "The learner must believe" <note>original Latin: *Discentem oportet credere*</note>, is as tyrannical as it is dangerous; as is that same Pythagorean phrase <term>Greek: *autos epha*; Latin: *ipse dixit*</term> <note>meaning "he himself said it," referring to the practice of accepting an authority's word as absolute truth without proof</note>. Let no person be compelled to swear by their master's words; instead, let the things themselves convince the intellect. Nor should a teacher be given any more credit than he can actually demonstrate, in very deed, that he deserves. 

For in a free republic <note>original: Common-wealth</note> there ought to be no *kings*, but only *leaders* <note>original: Dukes, from the Latin *duces*, meaning guides or leaders</note> or *generals*; no *dictators*, but only *consuls* <note>elected officials who shared power in the Roman Republic</note>. And those who profess the art of instructing people are the fathers of humanity, not the carvers of statues. Oh, when shall we see that day when all things which ought to be known shall offer themselves so clearly to...

# The Preface.

...a person's understanding, so that there will be nothing but what may be understood for its very clarity, and nothing called into doubt because of its certainty; the truth of things makes such an impression upon the senses with its light. For he does not truly see who must still be persuaded by arguments to make him believe that he sees, as we have for the most part been treated until now. 

Because I seemed to see light in the light of God, I could not choose but attempt—calling upon God to aid me—to organize these new hypotheses of natural things <note>natural things: an early term for "natural philosophy" or what we now call science</note> into a new method and dictate them to the students of this school. # The Preface.

# The Preface.

[There] is so much light in this method of Physics <note>original: "Physicks"</note> delivered here, that very little room is left for doubts and disputes. This contributes toward resolving the controversies between authors, bringing the opinions of all into harmony—including whatever truth is found in *Aristotle*, or what *Galen*, the chemists <note>original: "Chymicks"</note>, *Campanella*, and *Verulamius* <note>Francis Bacon, Baron Verulam (1561–1626), an influential proponent of the scientific method.</note> reasonably allege against him.

This harmony may be made plain by the example of the principles they believe bodies consist of: *Aristotle* argued they were the four elements <note>Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note>, while the Spagyrists <note>original: "Syagyricks," referring to the Paracelsian school of alchemy and medicine.</note> argued for Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury <note>original symbol: ☿</note>. Furthermore, this method opens a gateway to a new kind of path—not only for understanding arts and human inventions, but also for multiplying them. This could never happen unless the foundations of truth were discovered.

# The Preface.

If he saw the *Christian Universal Wisdom* <note>original: *Pansophia Christiana*; a concept of universal knowledge integrated with Christian theology</note> that we are currently working on—which is derived from this source as well as from our *Didactics* <note>original: *Didacticks*; the science and art of teaching</note> and *Metaphysics*—he would not consider what has been said to be spoken in vain. But because those works have not yet been published, I establish this rule for the ones that are: *If anything is not sufficiently derived from sense, reason, and Scripture; if anything does not harmonize well enough with the rest; or if anything is not clear through its own obviousness, let it be regarded as if it had never been said.* 

While this rule remains in force, it is legitimate for myself and all others to always and everywhere doubt whether things are truly as they are presented. We may also investigate why things are as we find them to be. By following these two paths—constant doubt and inquiry—no one should doubt that the deepest foundations of truth will eventually be discovered.

Therefore, let none of us seek anything else but how the truth...

# The Preface.

...may best be maintained on all sides. If it happens that truth is not on our side, and that we are deceived by appearances of truth (as is very usual in human affairs), I beseech all those who are more sharp-sighted, for the love of truth, to courteously show us the way we have lost, and where our demonstrations <note>original: "demonstrations"; refers to logical proofs or step-by-step explanations</note> do not hold together. 

But if these ideas seem close to the truth, then I ask that they would not disdain to join their endeavors with ours for the illustration <note>the act of making something clear or providing examples</note> thereof. May all of us, being the children of truth, compose and sing hymns of praise together to God, the Father of Truth.

You, therefore,

> *O Christ, the Father’s bright glory,*
> *of this great world the only light;*
> *On us some beams of light bestow,*
> *Who are your servants, that we may know you.*

Amen, Lord make me see! Here in—

# PROLEGOMENA
<term>Prolegomena: Preliminary remarks or a formal introduction to a subject.</term>

*Concerning the nature, foundation, and use of Physics.*
<note>original: "Physick." In this context, "Physick" refers to Natural Philosophy or Physics—the study of the physical world and laws of nature—rather than the modern medical sense of the word.</note>

I *Physics is the scientific knowledge of natural things.*

II *That thing is natural which is produced by Nature, not by Art.*
<note>original: "Art" refers to human craft, skill, or technology.</note>

**F**or whatever this visible world has comes either from Nature or from Art. Those things are from Nature which God brought forth in the beginning, or which are produced up to this very time by a virtue <term>virtue: an inherent power or property</term> implanted in things—such as the heavens, the earth, the sea, rivers, mountains, stones, metals, herbs, living creatures, etc. Those things are from art which men have shaped by putting a new form upon natural things, such as cities, houses, ponds, channels, statues, coins, garments, books, etc.; that is, by the work of human ingenuity and hands. Physics has nothing to do with these things; these are handed over to the arts.

<vocab>Physics, Scientific Knowledge, Nature, Art, God, ingenuity</vocab>

<summary>This introductory page defines "Physics" as the scientific study of the natural world and establishes a clear distinction between things created by nature and those created by human skill or "art."</summary>
<keywords>Physics, Natural Philosophy, Nature vs Art, Creation, Human Ingenuity, Prolegomena</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>The text continues from the previous page, establishing the hierarchy of knowledge where the study of nature precedes human invention.</meta>

Now, seeing that *nature* comes before *art*: *art* imitates nothing but *nature*, because it does nothing except by the strength of *nature*. It necessarily follows that *nature* is to be laid as the foundation for the arts, and that *nature*—what things are and by what power they operate everywhere—must first be known by those who study the arts. For when this is known, the secrets of all arts open of their own accord. Without this knowledge, all things in the arts and the <term>prudential arts</term> <note>original: "prudentials"; refers to practical wisdom, ethics, and the application of judgment in human affairs.</note> will be blind, mute, and maimed. Therefore, <term>Physics</term> <note>original: "Physick"; in this period, this refers to Natural Philosophy, the general study of the physical world and its laws, rather than the specific medical profession or modern physics.</note> is so necessary to be established before the *Mathematical*, *Logical*, and also the *prudential arts*, that those who do otherwise may be thought to "build castles in the air."

### III *The nature of things is the law of being born and of dying, of operating and of ceasing, which* God *the Creator <note>original: "Workmaster"</note> has laid upon all things that exist.*

For all things are born and die; all things perform some operation, and all things cease again, in an order and manner proper to every creature. Because this order and manner is founded upon most excellent reason, it could not be arranged except by supreme wisdom, inasmuch as it is found to be constantly imposed as a law upon all things.

<summary>The author argues that the study of nature (Physics) is the essential foundation for all other arts and sciences, as nature is the divine law governing the life, death, and operation of all things.</summary>
<keywords>Nature vs Art, Natural Philosophy, Physics, God the Creator, Law of Nature, Logic, Mathematics, Foundations of Knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the definition of "nature" and transitions into the methodology for studying natural philosophy, emphasizing direct observation over the mere study of other people's theories.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>B 2</sig>

Now, it took the name of "nature" from the first degree of change <note>original: "mutation"</note> of every thing, which is to be born.

*IV. The knowledge of nature is to be obtained by searching into nature itself.*

By searching, I mean this: no one should spend his time in physics <note>original: "Phyſicks"; here referring to the study of the natural world or natural philosophy.</note> just to have his mind filled with someone else’s ideas <note>original: "conceits"</note>. Instead, he should push himself toward a thorough and intimate knowledge of things. Otherwise, the intellect will not be enlightened <note>original: "illuſtrated"</note> by the nature of things, but rather clouded <note>original: "obumbrated"</note> by the contemplation of illusions <note>original: "phantaſms"</note> in natural things. Therefore, we must seek guides who will make us students not of themselves, but of nature, and who show us not their own foolish <note>original: "fond"</note> reasons, but nature itself.

*V. To search nature is to contemplate how and why everything in nature happens.*

By "contemplate," I mean this: just as we do not see the sun except by looking at the sun, we do not learn about nature except by looking into nature. This is what the Scripture advises us: 

> *Ask the beasts, and they shall teach you, and the birds of the air, and they shall tell you; or speak to the earth, and it shall an-* <note>A quotation from Job 12:7-8.</note>

<meta>catchword: swer</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the study of nature (physics) must be based on direct observation of the natural world rather than the inherited opinions of other scholars. He uses biblical references to support the idea that the physical world is the primary teacher of natural truths.</summary>

<keywords>natural philosophy, physics, empiricism, observation, Job 12:7-8, methodology, education</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the study of the natural world, moving from biblical justification for observing nature to the scientific principle of studying simple forms to understand complex ones.</meta>

...answer you, and the fishes of the sea shall declare to you. (*Job 12:7.*) Therefore, students of natural philosophy <note>original: "naturall Philosophy"; the study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science</note> cannot be more effectively and easily instructed than if they are taught by ocular demonstration <term>visual evidence or direct observation</term> wherever it may be found. I mean that we should contemplate everything, so that we may sift out the reasons and causes of all things everywhere. 

For it is certain that nature does nothing in vain, even in matters of the least importance; indeed, sometimes much wisdom lies stored up in the very smallest things. What is more, we cannot attain knowledge of great things except through the knowledge of lesser things, as the following aphorism <term>a concise statement of a general truth or principle</term> will teach us.

<margin>☛</margin>
### VI *Nature unfolds herself in the smallest things, and conceals herself in the greatest things.*

That is, in the more "excellent" <note>higher-order or more complex</note> creatures, many things are wound up and woven together with such hidden craftsmanship <note>original: "occult artifice"</note> that neither the beginnings nor the endings of actions and properties <note>original: "accidents"; in philosophy, these are non-essential characteristics</note> can easily be discerned. But in all coarser <note>simpler or less refined</note> creatures, all things are clearly manifest; this is why the nature of compounds <term>complex structures or substances made of multiple parts</term> cannot be known unless the nature of simples <term>the basic, individual components</term> is

<summary>The author argues that scientific understanding requires direct observation of nature and asserts that the complexities of "higher" organisms are best understood by first studying simpler, more transparent forms.</summary>
<keywords>natural philosophy, ocular demonstration, Job 12:7, aphorism, occult artifice, compounds and simples, observation</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the proper order of scientific inquiry, emphasizing that foundational principles must be understood before complex ones, following the pattern of creation.</meta>

...be first known; so, consequently, we are to begin with these speculations and to proceed by degrees from simpler things to more complex ones. We shall see that the Creator himself observed this very order in producing and intertwining the nature of things.

### VII *We are to study natural philosophy <note>original: "naturall Phyloſophie"; the study of nature and the physical universe before the modern term "science" was used</note> by the guide of sense and the light of the Scripture.*

For the senses are the beginning not only of knowledge, but of certainty and wisdom. Just as there is nothing in the mind <note>original: "intellect"</note> which was not first in the senses, so if there is anything obscure or doubtful in the mind, we must return to the senses for evidence and certainty. 

But wherever sense or reason fails (as in things remote in either place or time), we are indebted to the grace of God, for he has deigned to reveal many things to us that exceed sense and reason—for example, the first creation of the world and the structure of invisible things.

He who neglects either of these principles is easily entangled in errors; for the more imagination any thing contains, the more vanity it <meta>catchword: has</meta>

<summary>The author argues that scientific study must begin with sensory observation but rely on Divine Scripture for matters beyond human reason, such as the creation of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>natural philosophy, empiricism, sensory perception, divine revelation, theology and science, epistemology</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>The previous page likely ended with a word ending in "us," potentially referring to a discussion on the limitations of human wisdom or a specific philosopher.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>

has, and is the more remote from the truth. Furthermore, the less anything participates in revealed wisdom, the less it partakes of the truth. Such is mostly the case with the philosophy of the Gentiles <note>original: "Gentiles"; here referring to non-Christian or pagan thinkers, particularly the ancient Greeks and Romans</note>, which is therefore vain and barren. We will follow the guidance of *Moses* (who described the creation of the world by the command of God), yet always carefully observing the evidence of the senses and of reason.

For *Ludovicus Vives* <note>Joannes Ludovicus Vives (1493–1540), a Spanish humanist and scholar who criticized Scholasticism</note> (as we have noted under the title of this book) wisely calls *Christians* away from the "lamp of the Gentiles"—which yields a dim and harmful light—to that "torch of the Sun" which Christ, the Light of the World, brought into the world. Vives attributed much cleverness, indeed, but little benefit to the inventions of *Aristotle*.

Moreover, *Campanella* <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639), an Italian philosopher who sought to reconcile science and scripture</note> and *Verulamius* <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626), 1st Viscount St Alban, also known as Baron Verulam</note>—most *Christian* philosophers who are familiar with that method of philosophy based on the senses and Scripture—have demonstrated that all of *Aristotle's* doctrines are nothing but a nursery for disputes. That is to say, they consist of obscurities, hesitations, contradictions, strifes, and quarrels, resembling fighting while blindfolded <note>original: "hood-winckt"</note>. They have shown that these doctrines hinder rather than advance our contemplation of things, and they have also provided...

<summary>The author argues that philosophy should be based on biblical revelation and sensory observation rather than the "barren" and "obscure" traditions of Aristotle and pagan thinkers. He cites influential figures like Vives, Campanella, and Francis Bacon to support a move toward a more Christian and empirical approach to knowledge.</summary>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues a sentence from the previous page, describing how earlier discoveries provide a light for further inquiry. The catchword "There-" suggests the next page begins with a word like "Therefore."</meta>

...us a light, by which we may kindle clearer torches for searching out the truth. Following in their footsteps (yet laying strong foundations from the Scriptures), we will prepare a small "Theatre of Nature," not for the sake of argument, but for observation <note>original: "speculation"</note>. We will travel through nature silently, yet with our eyes open, and this is also in accordance with the counsel of the great *Vives* <note>Juan Luis Vives (1493–1540) was a Spanish humanist and scholar who emphasized the importance of direct observation over abstract logic.</note>: *There is no need for arguments (he says), but for a silent contemplation of nature. Scholars shall inquire and ask rather than fight. If any are slower to understand, they will need a more complete demonstration <note>original: "commonstration," meaning a clear showing or proving</note>, not more argument.*

And a little later, he says again, *I say, here is no need for wrangling, but for looking on. Thus, this study will be the delight of the rich and a refreshing of the mind to those who deal in either public or private affairs. For when shall we easily find any other delight of the senses to be compared with this, whether in its greatness, its variety, or its permanence? For when we spend our labor on this contemplation, we do not need to seek any other recreation, nor do we desire sauce for this meat* <note>The author uses a metaphor suggesting that the study of nature is so inherently satisfying that it requires no "extra seasoning" to be enjoyable.</note>. *The walk itself and the quiet contemplation serve as both a school and a master, as it always provides something which you may admire, in which you may delight, and which may increase your knowledge.*

<summary>The author advocates for the "silent contemplation" of the natural world over academic bickering, citing the philosopher Juan Luis Vives to argue that observing nature provides unparalleled mental refreshment and knowledge.</summary>

### CHAPTER I.

#### An Idea of the World to be created and created.

**T**he eternal Deity, our God who is to be adored, after the infinite glories which he enjoys in his immense eternity, was, out of his exceeding goodness, inclined <note>original: "propense"</note> to communicate himself outside of himself. By his exceeding *Wisdom*, he saw that his invisible attributes <note>original: "invisible things"</note> might be expressed by certain visible images. To execute that, having his *Omnipotence* at hand, he decreed not to begrudge existence <note>original: "envie entitie"</note> to those things in which he might be expressed, and in which his *Power*, *Wisdom*, and *Goodness* might be revealed. 

Therefore, he produced intelligent creatures by whom he might be known and praised—*Angels* and *men*—both created in his own image. The first are pure minds; the others are clothed with bodies, for whom he built a dwelling place and, as it were, a school of wisdom: this universal World, along with other creatures of a lower <note>original: "inferiour"</note> degree, almost infinite in number. All and every one of these cry out in their own way, "*He made us, and not we ourselves*." <note>A reference to the biblical passage Psalm 100:3.</note> Now then, we proceed to reveal <note>original: "unfold"</note> in what...

<summary>This page introduces the theological motivation for Creation, arguing that God created the physical world and intelligent beings (Angels and humans) to manifest His invisible power, wisdom, and goodness.</summary>

<keywords>Creation, Deity, Divine Wisdom, Omnipotence, Angels, Humanity, Theology, Psalm 100:3</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the creation of the world as recorded in Genesis, specifically focusing on the initial stages of order and matter.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

...in what order so great a work proceeded, and with what skill all things were designed, and with what strength they are held together—yet guided by Him who alone is able to testify of Himself and His works. For thus He says, through His secretary <term>Moses</term> <note>The author refers to Moses as God's "secretary," reflecting the traditional belief that Moses transcribed the Pentateuch under divine inspiration.</note> in *Genesis 1*:

I. *In the beginning God created the heaven* (*verse 1*). That is, the highest heaven along with the Angels. Having produced them first as "morning stars," He made them spectators of the rest of His works (*Job 38:7*).

II. *And the earth*—that is, this visible world, which He did not finish at that same moment; therefore it is said:

III. *And the earth was void, and without form, and darkness was upon the face of the deep* (*verse 2*). That is, the physical substance of this world was first produced: a certain Chaos without form and dark, like a black smoke arising out of the bottomless pit of <term>nothingness</term> <note>original: "nihilitie," a term derived from the Latin *nihil*, meaning nothing.</note> at the command of the Almighty. This was matter, the first principle of this visible world.

IV. *And the Spirit of God moved upon the water.* That is, a certain strength was introduced by the spirit or breath of God into that same dark and naturally confused matter, by which it began to stir. By this, then, is understood the second principle of...

<summary>The author provides a theological commentary on the first two verses of Genesis, explaining the creation of the heavens, the angels, and the initial formless "Chaos" of the earth.</summary>
<keywords>Creation, Genesis, Moses, Angels, Chaos, Matter, Holy Spirit, Nihility</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "spirit of life" as the animating force of the world and begins an analysis of the first days of Creation as described in Genesis.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>

the *World*—that is, the *spirit of life spread throughout*, with which the *Universal World* is still full. This spirit, working its way everywhere through all parts of matter, nourishes and rules it, and produces every creature, giving each its own form. However, because this master-craftsman <note>original: "work-master"</note> needed fire to soften and prepare the matter in various ways for various uses, God produced it. For,

> V *God said, "Let there be light," and there was light (verse 3).*

This light is described as the third principle of the *World*, which is purely active. Through light, matter was made visible and capable of being divided into specific forms. Light, I say, perfects all things that exist and are created in the World; therefore, it is added:

> VI *And God saw the light, that it was good (verse 4).*

That is, He saw that all things would now proceed in an orderly way. For once that light was produced in a great mass, it immediately began to display its threefold power <note>original: "virtue"</note>—illuminating, moving itself, and heating—and by rotating around the World, it began to heat and thin <term>rarify</term> the matter, and so to divide it. From the brightness of that light, the first distinction between night and day followed.

> VII *He divided the light from the darkness, and*

<summary>This page explains the "spirit of life" as an animating force and describes light as a purely active principle that organizes matter through heat and motion. It interprets the biblical creation of light as the moment when the natural order and the division of day and night began.</summary>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues a theological and natural-philosophical commentary on the Book of Genesis, specifically detailing the first and second days of Creation. The catchword "ther" indicates the text likely continues with "there" or "therefore" on the following page.</meta>

*(...called the light day, and the darkness he called night, and the evening and morning were the first day, verse 5.)* This means that when the light had turned itself around and encircled the world, its motion created day and night.

The second effect of light came from heat. Specifically, whichever way the light passed, it thinned <note>original: "rarified"</note> and purified the matter, but condensed it on both sides—both upward and downward. This process resulted in the division of the elements, which *Moses* describes in these words:

**VIII** *"And God said, let there be a Firmament, that it may divide between the waters above and the waters below," verse 6.* "God said" means that He ordained how it should be. "Let there be a Firmament" means let that light stretch out the matter. Let the thicker part of the matter, melting and retreating from the light, create waters on both sides: above, as they mark the boundary <note>original: "term"</note> of the visible world; and below, as they are matter suitable for producing other creatures. Beneath these lower waters, the earth gathered together like thick dregs. This was completed on the second day.

**XI** *"Therefore God said, let the waters under heaven be gathered together into one place; and let the dry land appear. And it was so; and God called the dry land, Earth; and the gathering together..."*

<summary>This page explains the mechanics of the first and second days of Creation, interpreting "light" as a force that generated time through motion and separated the elements into the firmament, waters, and earth through heat.</summary>

(Chapter 2, verse 7) So man was made like the other living creatures through a blending <note>original: "contemperation"</note> of matter, spirit, and light; yet he was also made like God and the Angels through the inspiration of the mind—a most exquisite summary of the world. This was done so it might appear that God created these two only for Himself, while creating all the intermediate things for their use. 

Lastly, all things are from God and for God; they flow out from Him and flow back to Him. To ensure that all these things might continue in their essence, as they were arranged by the wisdom of God, He placed a power <note>original: "virtue," meaning an inherent medicinal or natural power</note> into everything—which people call *Nature*—to preserve themselves in their essence, and even to multiply. --- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on how God’s power operates through nature rather than through direct, miraculous intervention in every individual instance.</meta>

[Omnipo]tency <note>The text begins mid-word; likely "Omnipotency" from the previous page.</note> no longer acts directly upon individual things (as it did before), but nature itself consistently extends its power <note>original: "vertue"</note> through all things. This does not take away from God’s Providence; rather, it makes His great power, wisdom, and goodness even more evident <note>original: "illustrate"</note>. 

For His great goodness ensures that both the greatest and the smallest things are directed toward their purposes, so that nothing exists or is created in vain. His wisdom is shown in the way such diligence <note>original: "industry"</note> is placed within nature to guide everything toward its end, so that nature never fails unless it is obstructed. Finally, His power is seen in how such an unchanging durability is granted to the universe despite the constant changeability of individual parts, so that the world is, in a sense, eternal.

Therefore, we must more thoroughly examine the inner workings <note>original: "veins"</note> of nature’s strength, craftsmanship, and order. This will allow the things we have briefly suggested from the writings of *Moses* to be further clarified by the consistent testimony of Scripture, reason, and the senses, creating a path to understand how one thing follows another.

<meta>This page continues an interpretation of the Book of Genesis, specifically arguing that the "heaven" mentioned in the first verse refers to a spiritual, invisible realm created before the physical world.</meta>

...of those words of *Moses*: *In the beginning God created the heaven.* This suggests that the invisible world was the beginning of the works of God—that is, the "heaven of heavens" along with the Angels. It appears plain that by this "heaven," we should understand the heaven of heavens and the invisible or angelic world for the following reasons:

I. *From Scripture*, which (1) mentions the "heaven of heavens" everywhere, but mentions their creation <note>original: "production"</note> nowhere else, unless it is here. (2) *Moses* testifies that the visible <note>original: "invisible"—this appears to be a rhetorical point or a period typo, as the author is contrasting the "heaven of heavens" in verse 1 with the "firmament" or physical sky created later</note> heavens were stretched out on the second day and adorned with stars on the fourth day. Therefore, another heaven must necessarily be understood in this place: namely, a heaven that was finished in that same moment. This is because the particle *autem* <term>but / however</term> implies this: "He created the heavens and the earth; *terra autem* <note>Latin: "but the earth"</note>, but the earth was without form," etc.

III. This reason demonstrates the same point: those things which are made by God are made in order. An orderly process of operation is one where progress is made from simpler things to complex <note>original: "compound"</note> things. Therefore, just as man—the most complex creature—was produced last, so the most simple and immaterial creatures (*Heaven and the Angels*) were produced first of all.

III *And what more could we want?* God himself testifies expressly that when he made the earth, the Angels stood by him as spectators. For so he says to Job: *Where were you when I founded the earth, when the morning stars sang together, and all the sons of God shouted?* (Job 38:4, 7). He calls the Angels "morning stars" because they were a spiritual beam of light, and one that had newly risen; he calls them "sons of God" because they were made in the image of God. Therefore, when we hear that the earth was founded on the first day, it must necessarily follow that the Angels were produced before the earth. And if the Angels were created, then certainly the dwellings of the Angels—the "heaven of heavens"—were created as well. This was done in full perfection, with all their hosts, as if in a single moment. This is the reason why Moses speaks no more of that heaven, but descends to the forming of the earth (that is, the visible world) and describes how the Creator took six days to organize <note>original: "digest"</note> it—as we will also now descend to discuss.

## CHAPTER II.

### *Of the visible Principles of the World: matter, spirit, and light.*

**W**E have seen—God showing us—how the world arose out of the abyss of nothingness <note>original: nihilitie</note>; let us now see how it stands <note>original: standeth</note>, so that by seeing we may learn to see, and by feeling to feel, the very truth of things.

And here, three principles of visible things are presented to us: matter, spirit, and light. These were produced on the first day as three great but raw <note>original: rude</note> masses, and out of those, variously shaped <note>original: wrought</note>, came forth different kinds of creatures. *Therefore, we must inquire further whether these three principles of all physical bodies have a true being and are still existing,* lest any error be perhaps committed at the very beginning <note>original: entrance</note> by any negligence whatsoever. But now, seeing that no one doubts the existence of matter and light, this only remains to be proved: *that by that spirit which hovered upon the face of the waters <note>A reference to the creation account in Genesis 1:2.</note>, a certain universal spirit of the world is to be understood*, which puts life and vigor into all created things.

Let the following aphorisms <term>short, pithy statements of principle or truth</term> concern the nature of matter.

I. *The first matter of the world was a vapor or a fume.*

<margin>}</margin>For what else could that description by Moses <note>Referring to the prophet Moses, traditionally considered the author of the Book of Genesis.</note> mean, when he calls it *earth, waters, the deep, darkness, and a thing void and without form*? This also appears true by reason; for seeing that the smaller bodies of the world—clouds, water, stones, metals, and all things growing on the earth—are made of condensed <note>original: "coagulated"</note> vapors (as shall appear most evidently hereafter), why should the whole world not be as well? Certainly, the matter of the whole can be nothing else but that which is found to be the matter of its parts.

II. *The first matter of the world was a chaos of dispersed atoms, adhering to no other part.*

This is proved:
1. By *reason*: for if they had adhered <note>original: "cohered"</note> in any way, they would have had a form. But they did not; for the state was *Tohu vabohu* <note>Hebrew: "without form and void," from Genesis 1:2</note>, a thing without form and empty.
2. By *the senses*, which demonstrate that the elements are turned into

Atoms. For what is dust but earth reduced into atoms? What is vapor but water resolved into more subtle parts? The air itself—what is it but a very small pulverization <note>original: "comminution," meaning the action of reducing something to small particles</note> of drops of water, imperceptible to the senses? Indeed, all bodies are found to consist of extremely small parts; for example, trees, bark, flesh, skins, and membranes are made of very slender strings or threads, while bones, stones, and metals are made of small dust joined together, into which they may be dissolved <note>original: "resolved"</note> again. And this also shows that those threads or hairs are made of atoms, glued together as it were, so that when they are dried they may be powdered <note>original: "pouldred"</note>. Therefore, the whole world is nothing but dust, coagulated with various sticky <note>original: "glutinous"</note> substances into this or that form.

3. *By Scripture:* For eternal Wisdom itself testifies that *the beginning of the world was dust* (Proverbs 8:26). IV. *The matter is of itself invisible, and therefore dark.*

For darkness is seen in the same manner when the eyes are shut as when they are open; that is, they are not seen at all. <margin>☞</margin> And this is what *Moses* says: *and darkness was upon the face of the Abyss.* <note>A reference to Genesis 1:2: "And darkness was upon the face of the deep."</note>

V. *The matter is of itself without form, yet it is capable of being extended, contracted, divided, united, and of receiving every form and figure, just as wax is capable of receiving every seal.*

For we have shown that all the bodies of the world are made of these atoms, and are resolved into them; therefore, they are nothing else but matter clothed with several forms. This the chemists <note>original: "Chymicks"</note> demonstrate to the eye, reducing some dust at one time into liquor, at another time into a vapor, and at another time into a stone, etc.

VI. *The matter is eternal in its duration through all forms, so that nothing of it can perish.*

For in very deed, from the making of the world until now, not so much as one crumb of matter has perished, nor has one increased. For although bodies are generated, and do perish <note>The text ends mid-word; "perish" is supplied from the catchword on the following page.</note>

### *On the nature of the Spirit, or soul of the World.*

The spirit of the world is life itself, infused into the world to perform <note>original: "operate"</note> all things in everything. For whatever any creature does or undergoes <note>original: "suffers"</note>, it does or undergoes it by the power of this spirit, for the spirit is given to it.

*1. To inhabit the matter.*
For as in the beginning it moved itself upon the waters <note>A reference to the creation narrative in Genesis 1:2.</note>, so it still does not exist except within matter; especially in a liquid and subtle matter. For this reason, in the body of a living creature, those most subtle, blood-related vapors—which are like flames and serve as the chariot of life—are called "spirits" <note>original: "spirits." In early physiology, these were "vital spirits," the refined substances thought to carry life through the body.</note>. And <term>Chemists</term> <note>original: "Chymicks"</note>, extracting a spirit out of herbs, metals, and stones that resembles a little water, call it the *Quintessence* <term>Quintessence</term> <note>The "fifth essence" or purest element in alchemy, thought to be superior to earth, air, fire, and water.</note>. They do so because it is a more subtle substance than all the four elements. However, it is not the water itself, as water, but that "living virtue" <note>original: "virtue," meaning the inherent power or life-force.</note> of the creature from which it is extracted that lives within it. Since this spirit cannot be altogether separated from matter, it is preserved in that subtle form of matter. How firmly the spirit inheres <note>original: "inhæres"; meaning to remain fixed or attached.</note> in the matter shall be taught toward the end of this work. <margin>Chapters 9 and 10.</margin>

*2. To move or agitate itself through the whole of matter to preserve it.*
<margin>1</margin> From this, it follows that no *vacuum* <note>original: "vacuum." A reference to the traditional scientific belief that "nature abhors a vacuum" and that all space must be filled with matter.</note> can exist in the world. For all bodies, even the most subtle (such as water, air, and the sky), being endowed with this spirit, delight in contiguity and continuity <note>They "delight" in staying connected and touching one another.</note>. For just as a living creature will not allow itself to be cut, so also water, air, and indeed the world it

For one and the same spirit of the universe is afterwards deduced <note>original: "diduced"; derived or channeled down</note> into many specific forms by the command of God. <margin>3.</margin> Thus, there is one spirit of water, another spirit of earth, another of metals, another of plants, another of living creatures, and so on; and then within every kind, there are several species. This is why from the seed of wheat, there does not grow a bean—much less a walnut or a bird.

### IV *To form for itself bodies for the use of future operations.*

For example, the spirit of a dog being included in its seed, when it begins to form its young, does not form wings, or fins, or hands, etc., because it does not need those members. ### *Of the nature of light.*

<margin>Ꜫ</margin>
*I. **THE** first light was nothing other than brightness, or a massive flame, sent into the dark matter to make it visible and capable of being divided into form.*

*For in the original language, light and fire have the same name: אור <note>original Hebrew: *Or*, meaning light</note> and אור <note>original Hebrew: *Ur*, meaning fire or flame</note>, from which also comes the Latin word **URO** <note>Latin: "I burn"</note>. And truly, the light of heaven does indeed both shine and burn, or provide heat.*

*II. God put into the light a threefold power <term>original: virtue</term>: 1. of spreading itself in every direction and illuminating all things. 2. of moving the matter it takes hold of by burning and inflaming it. 3. of heating, and thereby thinning <term>rarifying</term> and weakening the density <term>attenuating</term> of the matter.*

*All these things our earthly fire also does, because it is nothing else but light kindled in the lower matter.*

*III. But since that light could not extend its motion upwards and downwards (for it would have reached a limit immediately), it moved itself, and still moves, in a circle. This was the beginning of days.*

*IV. And because the matter was thinned <term>rarified</term> above as heat was raised by the motion of light, the heavier <term>grosser</term> parts of the matter were forced to fall downward and gather into a sphere <term>conglobate</term> in the center of the Universe. This was the beginning of the earth and water.*

<summary>This page describes the divine origin and physical properties of light, explaining it as a form of fire that organizes matter through heat and motion. It details how light's circular movement created time (days) and how its heating effect caused heavier matter to settle in the center of the universe to form the Earth.</summary>
<keywords>light, fire, matter, Hebrew, Latin, Uro, motion, Universe, creation, rarifying, physics, cosmology, formation of Earth</keywords>

V Therefore, *light*—through its threefold *power* <note>original: "vertue"</note> (*light, motion, and heat*)—introduced *opposition* <note>original: "contrariety"</note> into the world.

For *darkness* was the opposite of *light*; *rest*, the opposite of *motion*; and *cold*, the opposite of *heat*. From these came other opposites as well: *moist* and *dry, thin* and *thick, heavy* and *light*, etc., which are discussed in Chapter 4.

VI Therefore, the order <note>original: "disposition"</note> and beauty <note>original: "adorning"</note> of the entire world come *from the light.*

For light is the only source <note>original: "fountain"</note> of visibility, motion, and heat. Take light out of the world, and all things will return to a *Chaos* <note>In this context, "Chaos" refers to the formless, unorganized state of matter before the biblical creation.</note>. What do you think would happen if the lights <note>original: "luminaries"</note> of heaven were completely extinguished? Therefore, *all things throughout the visible world exist and are created from matter, within the spirit, but through the power of fire or light.*

## CHAPTER III.

### Concerning the Motion of Things.

**O**nce the principles of things have been established, we must examine the common accidents <note>In traditional philosophy, "accidents" refers to properties or attributes of a thing that are not part of its essential nature but can change without the thing itself ceasing to exist.</note> of things: which are *Motion*, *Quality*, and *Change* <note>original: "Mutation"</note>.

For from the interaction <note>original: "congresse"</note> of the world’s principles, motion first arose; from motion came quality; and from quality, in turn, came the various changes of things. As will be shown, these three are present in all created things.

<margin> [decorative flourish] </margin>
*I. Motion is a property of a body by which it is moved from one place to another.*

The doctrine of natural motions—specifically how many types there are and how they occur—is the key to understanding all natural actions. Therefore, it must be studied most diligently.

*II. Motion was given to things for the sake of creation <note>original: "generation"</note>, action, and time.*

<margin> [decorative flourish] </margin>
Regarding creation: nothing could be produced without being composed of parts. Things cannot be composed without coming together, and they cannot come together without motion.

<summary>This page introduces the concept of motion as one of the three primary "accidents" of matter (alongside quality and change) and argues that motion is the essential mechanism for all natural creation and action.</summary>
<keywords>motion, quality, mutation, generation, natural philosophy, principles of nature, composition</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues a philosophical or scientific discussion on the fundamental principles of the physical world, specifically explaining why action and time require motion.</meta>

Regarding actions: because none could exist without motion.

Regarding time: so that it might serve as the measure of the duration of things. For if you were to take the sun and the stars out of the world, nothing could be known—the what, where, or when. All things would be blind, mute <note>original: "dumb"</note>, and deaf.

III **Motion is either simple or compound.**

IV **Simple motion belongs either to spirit, light, or matter.**

V **The motion of the spirit is called agitation, by which the spirit moves itself within matter, seeking to give it form <term>inform: in a philosophical sense, to give a specific shape or essence to matter</term>.**

For the living spirit would not be alive if it ceased to move itself and strive to subdue matter in any way at all. This motion is the beginning of the creation <note>original: "generation"</note> and decay <note>original: "corruption"</note> of things. For the spirit in everything (whether in flesh, an apple, a grain, wood, etc.) softens the parts by its own agitation, so that the object may either receive new life or the spirit may escape, causing the thing to rot <note>original: "putrifie"</note>.

VI **The motion of light is called diffusion, by which light and heat spread themselves into every part.**

For fire would not be fire, nor heat be heat, if it ceased to spread itself and melt things <note>original: "liquifie"</note>.

<summary>This page defines the fundamental roles of motion in the universe, explaining how "agitation" by the spirit creates or destroys life and how "diffusion" characterizes the nature of light and heat.</summary>
<keywords>motion, simple motion, compound motion, spirit, light, matter, agitation, generation, corruption, diffusion, natural philosophy</keywords>

<page-num>(40)</page-num>
<sig>VII</sig>

the matter. And *from this motion of the fire, all the motion of the matter draws its origin*: as the experience of the senses testifies. For gross <note>original: "grosse"; meaning dense or solid</note> and cold things, such as wood, a stone, ice, etc., lack motion on their own. However, when fire is applied to them, they immediately obtain motion, as can be demonstrated to the eye. Imagine a kettle full of water with wood placed underneath it; behold, all is quiet! But kindle the wood, and you shall presently see motion: first in the wood—flame, smoke, and the coals splitting apart; then shortly after in the water—first evaporating, then swirling around, and at length, boiling and galloping <note>original: "galloping"; a term for vigorous, turbulent boiling</note>. But if you remove the fire again, all the motion will gradually cease once more. Similarly, in a living body 
<margin>}</margin>(an animal), if you take away heat, not only motion but also the capacity for movement will cease immediately as the limbs grow stiff. Furthermore, although there are diverse motions in things, the origin is everywhere the same: heat or fire. When it is contained within the world, it moves circularly; when kindled in the air, it darts itself out this way or that, depending on how the matter is arranged or how the wind blows. When contained within a living creature, it moves as the strength of the <term>fantasy</term> <note>original: "phantaſie"; referring to the mental faculty of imagination or the "animal spirits" that were thought to bridge the mind and body</note> forces it this way or that way.

### VII The motion of matter is eightfold: 
### expansion, contraction, aggregation, sympathy, 
### continuity, impulsion, libration <note>original: libration (a balancing or oscillating motion)</note>, and liberty.

The first two of these motions come directly from fire; the following four come from other bodies; and the last two come from the matter itself, mediated by the spirit of the universe. <margin>3</margin> Though this may seem complex at first, it will soon be made clear with examples.

### VIII The motion of expansion is that by which 
### matter, being rarefied by heat, expands of 
<margin>3</margin>### its own accord as it seeks more space.

It is not possible for matter to be rarefied <note>original: rarified (to become less dense)</note> and still be contained in the same space. Instead, one part pushes against another so that they may stretch out and occupy a larger sphere. You can see an example of this if you put a few drops of water into a hog's bladder, tie the neck shut, and place it over a furnace. <margin>3</margin> The bladder will stretch and swell because the water, turned into vapor by the heat, seeks more room.

### IX The motion of contraction is that by which 
### matter is compressed, drawing itself into a 
### smaller space through condensation.

<summary>This page defines the eight types of motion that affect matter and provides a detailed explanation and experiment regarding the motion of expansion caused by heat.</summary>
<keywords>motion of matter, expansion, contraction, rarefaction, condensation, physics, spirit of the universe</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of physical motions and the behavior of matter under heat and cold, specifically addressing the "motion of aggregation."</meta>

For example, if you move the previously mentioned bladder from the furnace to a cold place, the vapor will return to water, and the swelling of the bladder will subside. Or, if you put a leather strap <note>original: "thong"</note> into the fire, you will see it wrinkle and shrink because the softer parts are drawn out by the heat, and the rest must necessarily contract. For the same reason, the cracks and gaps in timber and in the earth occur.

*The motion of aggregation occurs when a body is drawn toward things of its own nature <note>original: "connaturals"</note>.*

For example, a flame rises while a stone falls. The flame perceives that its "connaturals" (that is, subtle bodies <note>In early science, "subtle bodies" referred to light, refined, or gaseous matter like air or fire.</note>) are above, while a stone senses that its own kind (that is, heavy things) are here below. *Note well* that those who are ignorant of other types of motion commonly call this "natural" motion. But although it is the most visible and seems the strongest and most unchangeable, it is actually quite weak. This is because it yields to all the other motions that follow and only exerts itself when they cease. This will become clear to anyone who meditates on these things diligently. However, I will add this: a drop of ink fallen upon paper maintains its shape <note>original: "defends itself"</note> by its roundness; yet if you touch a moist pen to it, you
