<meta>This page continues a discussion on the natural motions of matter, specifically how smaller portions of a substance seek to join larger bodies of the same kind. The catchword from the previous page was "wa-," likely referring to "water."</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>

...you will see the drop run up into it. See, it does not go downward (as it should because of its weight), but upward, so that it may join itself to a greater quantity of the same substance.

### XI *The motion of sympathy and antipathy is that by which a similar body is drawn to its like and driven away by its opposite.*

Now this similarity comes from the spirit <note>original: "spirit"; in 17th-century natural philosophy, this refers to an internal animating essence or subtle matter believed to direct the behavior of physical objects</note> that inhabits it. This motion is very evident in some bodies—such as in the lodestone <note>original: "loadstone"; a naturally magnetized piece of the mineral magnetite</note>, which draws iron to it or even leaps toward the iron itself. In others, it is weak and barely noticeable; for example, in milk, where the cream separates itself bit by bit from the watery whey and gathers at the top. In some things, this motion is, as it were, bound up unless it is released in some way. This is seen in melted brass, where metals are separated from one another by the force of fire; by the power of sympathy, every element gathers itself to its like (lead to lead, silver to silver) and flows together in its own specific place.

### XII *Motion of continuity is that by which matter follows matter, avoiding any break in connection.* <note>original: "shunning discontinuity"; the idea that nature abhors a vacuum</note>

For example, when you suck up the air through a pipe after putting one end of it into water, the

<summary>This page describes the "motion of sympathy," where like substances (such as magnets and iron or different metals in a forge) are drawn together by an internal essence, and begins explaining the "motion of continuity," which prevents vacuums in nature.</summary>
<keywords>motion of sympathy, antipathy, lodestone, magnetism, metallurgy, motion of continuity, natural philosophy, physics</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural motions, following the concept that the world acts as a unified living organism to prevent a vacuum.</meta>

Water will follow the air, even if it has to move upward. As we mentioned before, the world acts like a living creature and resists being "cut" or divided, because the living spirit unites all things.

XIII *The motion of impulsion (or yielding <note>original: cession</note>) is the process by which matter yields to other matter that presses against it.*

For example, water yields to a stone falling into it so that the stone may sink; similarly, a stone yields to the hand that pushes it, and so on. A physical body will not allow itself to be penetrated; it would rather move out of the way if it can. If the whole body cannot move, its parts will give way, as we see in every break, bruise, tear <note>original: rent</note>, wearing, or cutting. In every case, the weaker material yields to the stronger.

XIV *The motion of oscillation <note>original: libration</note> is that in which parts move themselves back and forth so they can be properly positioned within the whole.*

This is like when a balance scale moves itself, first one way and then the other.

XV *The motion of liberty <note>In this context, liberty refers to what we now call elasticity or the restorative force of a material.</note> is when a body or part of it is forcibly moved from its place—but not completely torn away—and then returns to its original position.*

For instance, when a tree branch is bent by force and then released, it returns to its natural posture.

<summary>This page defines three specific types of physical motion: impulsion (yielding to pressure), libration (swinging toward balance), and liberty (returning to a natural shape after being bent).</summary>
<keywords>motion of impulsion, motion of cession, motion of libration, motion of liberty, physics, elasticity, natural philosophy, yielding matter</keywords>

### *An example of all these motions in the formation of the Macrocosm <note>original: "Macrocosme"; the great world or the universe</note> or great World.*

First, the spirit moved itself upon the waters with the motion of **Agitation**. Then, the light being sent into the matter, penetrated it in every direction with the motion of **Diffusion**. Gradually, the matter above where the light passed through, being heated and rarefied, expanded itself with the motion of **Dispansion** <note>original: "Dispansion"; the act of spreading or stretching out</note>; but below, it thickened itself with the motion of **Contraction**. 

All the more subtle parts gathered themselves upwards, and the heavier <note>original: "grosser"</note> parts downwards, with the motions of **Aggregation** and **Sympathy**—for a more hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> Sympathy and Antipathy <note>The natural attraction or repulsion between different substances</note> was placed into things later. And wherever one part of the matter went, others followed by the motion of **Continuity**; or if one part rushed against others, they gave way by the motion of **Impulsion**. But the heavier parts balanced themselves (fleeing from the heat which came upon them from above) around the center into an exact spherical shape <note>original: "Globosity"</note> with the motion of **Libration** <note>A state of balancing or oscillating toward equilibrium</note>. There was no "motion of Liberty" <note>Movement occurring when a constraint is removed</note> because there was no external violence to put anything out of order.

### *An example of the same motions in the Microcosm <note>original: "Microcosme"; the "little world," usually referring to the human body as a reflection of the universe</note>, or little World.*

In man (and in every living creature), the food that is put into the stomach <note>original: "belly"</note> grows hot through warming <note>original: "incalescency"</note>; here you have the motion of *Expansion*. Then, by the motion of *Sympathy*, every part of the body <note>original: "member"</note> attracts to itself what is good for it. But by the motion of *Antipathy*, superfluous things are driven out as unprofitable and harmful to them.

Then, the blood is distributed equally to the whole body—both upwards and downwards—by the motion of *Libration* <note>meaning: balancing or oscillating movement</note>. As the blood is assimilated into the body parts, it is condensed so that it may become flesh, a membrane, a bone, and so on, through the motion of *Contraction*.

Lastly, the air drawn in and let out during breathing shows the motion of *Continuity* and *Contiguity* <note>the state of being in direct contact</note>. (For when the lungs are expanded <note>original: "distended"</note>, the air enters in to prevent a vacuum <note>original: "least there should be a vacuum"</note>; but when the lungs contract, the air moves out.)

The motion of *Liberty* will appear if you either press down or pull up your skin; for as soon as you remove your hand, the skin will return to its original position. Finally, if you fall from any place, there will be the motion of *Aggregation*; you will move toward the earth because you are made of weight and earth yourself.

You have an example of the first type if you throw a stone toward the earth, because here the motion of aggregation <note>original: "Aggregation"; the natural tendency of matter to draw together toward a center, similar to the modern concept of gravity</note> and impulsion <note>original: "Impulsion"; the force or momentum applied to an object by an external agent</note> are joined together. You have an example of the second type if you throw a stone toward the sky: for here the motion of impulsion strives against the motion of aggregation. In this struggle, the stronger force eventually overcomes the weaker—the natural motion overcomes that which is merely temporary <note>original: "accessory"</note>.

XVII *Compound motion occurs in living creatures when they move themselves from place to place of their own accord.*

Specifically, birds move by flying, fish by swimming, and beasts by running; we shall see in Chapter 10 how each of these is performed.

Also, natural philosophers <note>the term used for scientists before the 19th century</note> call it "compound motion" when a thing is completely changed—either coming into being, ceasing to exist, or changing into another kind of being—even if it remains in the same place. However, we call these changes "mutations," and they are to be handled in a specific chapter, the third one from this.

# CHAPTER IV.

## Concerning the Qualities of Things.

**M**atter is mixed in various ways with spirit and light through these diverse motions. From this varied mixture come different qualities, so that one thing is identified as one specific thing, another as something else, and yet another as something different again. We must now consider these; and these <term>talities</term> <note>original: "talities"; likely derived from the Latin *talis* meaning "of such a kind," used here as a synonym for qualities or characteristics</note>, or qualities, are some of them general—common to all bodies—and others special, belonging only to certain creatures. The first kind will be explained here collectively; the others will be addressed later in their proper places.

> I. *A quality is an attribute <note>original: "accident"; in traditional philosophy, an "accident" is a property that a thing has but which is not essential to its nature</note> of a body, in regard to which everything is said to be "such" or "such" a kind of thing.*

> II. *There are qualities in every body, including those that are intangible, spiritual, and volatile, as well as those that are coarse <note>original: "grosse"</note>, tangible, and fixed.*

For a body is (as we saw in Chapter 2, in the description of matter, <term>Aphorism 8</term> and of the spirit, <term>Aphorism 1</term>...

The qualities, therefore, which we will discuss, shall be common to all of these. For it may be said of a stone, of water, of air, and of the spirit that is enclosed in a body, that it is fat or raw, hot or cold, moist or dry, thick or thin, etc.

### III *Qualities are the basis for all forms in bodies.* <note>original: "grounds"</note>

For these qualities cause a living creature to differ from a stone, a stone from wood, and wood from ice; the "form" <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "form" is the essential nature or identity of a thing.</note> itself consists of qualities. Therefore, the doctrine of qualities is exceedingly profitable and is, as it were, the basis of natural science. Because this subject has been poorly <note>original: "miserably"</note> handled until now, the light of physics <note>original: "physicks"; the study of the natural world.</note> has been hindered <note>original: "maimed"</note> and, by that means, made obscure.

**Answer 2:** Because the wound is immediately covered with the moisture of the plant; this moisture, being hardened by the external cold, covers the wound as if with a crust and prevents a total evaporation.

XVII *Vital spirit* (as also natural spirit) *holds so fast to its matter that it scarcely ever forsakes it.*

## CHAPTER 10

### Of Living Creatures

So much for plants; next come living creatures.

I. *A living creature is a moving plant, endowed with sense: such as a worm, a fish, a bird, or a beast.*

For if a stone or an oak could move itself freely, or had sense <note>OCR error: original "fence" likely refers to "sense"</note>, it would also be a living creature.

II. *The principal difference between a living creature and a plant is self-motion* <term>self-motion <note>original: αύτοκινησία (autokinesia)</note></term>, *which is*...

<summary>The author concludes a discussion on how the "spirit" of minerals and plants can be preserved before defining a living creature as a "moving plant" distinguished by its ability for self-motion and sensation.</summary>
<keywords>spirit, biology, self-motion, autokinesia, plants, animals, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-num>160</page-num>
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the nature of motion in living beings from the previous page.</meta>

...is a free movement of itself back and forth.

To better express the power of the spirit of life—God's deputy <note>original: "Gods Vicar"; here meaning God's representative or agent in the natural world</note> in all creatures—it was necessary for such bodies to be produced that would be obedient to all the actions of the spirit inhabiting them. Since the basis of action is motion, bodies had to be formed which could perform free movement; these are called *Animalia* or *Animantia* <note>Latin: "living beings" or "animals"</note>, living creatures, named after the soul which powerfully demonstrates life within them.

2 Therefore, mobility exists in all living creatures, but in various ways. For some move only by opening and shutting without stirring from their place, such as oysters and cockles. Others creep bit by bit, like snails, earthworms, and other worms. Some have long bodies that creep by winding themselves about, like snakes. Some are granted feet, such as lizards, beasts, and birds—though these last also have wings to fly through the air. Fish imitate this in the water, performing their movement by swimming.

### III
The moving principle in a living creature is the vital soul, which is nothing other than the spirit of life—substantial and strong—mightily filling and powerfully governing the bodies it inhabits.

IV *Now because voluntary and easy <note>original: "light"</note> motion cannot be performed except in a delicate <note>original: "subtle"</note> matter, living creatures are given bodies far more delicate than plants, but far more complex <note>original: "compound"</note>. For they consist of spirit <note>In this context, "spirit" refers to "vital spirits," a refined substance once believed to flow through the body to animate it.</note>, flesh, blood, membranes, veins, nerves, cartilage <note>original: "gristles"</note>, and lastly bones, which act as supports and pillars so that the bodily structure does not collapse.*

Understand this as applying to "perfect" <note>Historically, "perfect" living creatures referred to complex animals with internal skeletons and circulatory systems, in contrast to "imperfect" ones like insects or mollusks.</note> living creatures. For more imperfect living creatures—in which we observe only the basic beginnings <note>original: "rudiments"</note> of nature—have neither bones, nor flesh, nor blood, nor veins. Instead, they have only a white fluid <term>humour: a bodily fluid or substance</term> covered with a skin or crust, as if by a sheath, which the spirit contained within moves; as is seen in worms, snails, oysters, etc. But regarding perfect living creatures: 

1. So that they might have a more refined spirit, *blood* and brains were given. 
2. And so that these might not be dispersed <note>original: "dissipated"</note>, they were given vessels and channels: *veins, arteries, and nerves*. 
3. So that a living creature might be supported upright <note>original: "erected"</note>, *bones* were given to it. 
4. And so that the bones, as well as the veins, arteries, and nerves, would not be easily injured, everything was covered with either fat or flesh. 
5. And so that the limbs <note>original: "members"</note> might move, tendons and muscles were interwoven.

*V. The bodies of living creatures were equipped with many members, such as various organs for various actions. However, because a living creature was intended for many different activities, it also required:*

1. <term>Vivifying organs</term> <note>organs that give life and energy</note>, which supply the living creature with heat, life, and motion; specifically, the *brain and the heart*.

2. Organs of movement, such as *feet, wings, feathers, and so on.*

3. And to prevent them from running into objects or falling off cliffs, it was necessary to equip them *with sight.*

VI. Therefore, the entire treatise concerning a living creature is completed in the explanation:
I. *Of the nutritive faculty.* <term>nutritive faculty: the bodily power responsible for growth and maintenance through food</term>
II. *Of the vital.*
III. *Of the sensitive.*
IV. *Of the locomotive.* <term>locomotive: the ability to move from place to place</term>
V. *Of the enunciative.* <term>enunciative: the ability to express or communicate, often referring to voice</term>
VI. *Of the defensive.*
VII. *And lastly, of the generative.* <term>generative: the ability to reproduce</term>

For whoever understands these seven knows the entire mystery of nature in living creatures. For whatever exists in the body of a living creature serves those faculties; if it does not serve them, it is in *vain*, and creates a *monster* <note>original: "monster"—in this period, a "monster" referred to any biological anomaly or part that did not function according to the natural design</note>. It should also be observed that the first three faculties are governed by three specific spirits: *The nutritive faculty by the natural spirit; the vital by the spirit of life; and the sensitive by the animal spirit* <note>original: "animall spirit"—from the Latin 'anima' (soul/breath), this was believed to be a subtle fluid that transmitted sensation and movement through the nerves</note>. The other four faculties are governed by these three spirits working together.

When a creature is deprived of this <note>likely referring to external nourishment or moisture mentioned on the previous page</note>, it attacks the solid parts and feeds on them. ### *VIII That nourishment is suitable <note>original: "convenient"</note> for a living creature which supplies it with a spirit like its own spirit.*

Because life comes from the spirit, matter itself does not nourish life, but rather spirituous matter <note>matter containing "vital spirits," a historical concept of life-giving energy</note>. Indeed, the spirit of the nourishment must necessarily be like the spirit of the living creature. Therefore, we are not nourished by the basic elements <note>earth, water, air, and fire</note> as plants are, since they have only a natural spirit, not a vital spirit. Instead, we are nourished by plants or by the flesh of other living creatures because those provide a vital spirit. Furthermore, there is a specific proportion of spirits which causes a horse to choose oats, a pig barley, a wolf meat, and so on. Even a hog has an appetite for human waste because it still finds parts there that are suitable for it.

### *IX Nourishment turns into the substance of that which is nourished.*

It is well known from metaphysics <note>referring to the Aristotelian principle of change</note> that all action tends toward this: that the Patient <term>the object or matter being acted upon</term> may become like the Agent <term>the force or entity performing the action</term>, which is evident everywhere in natural things.

### XII *The primary transformation of nourishment occurs through the production of the four vital humors: blood, phlegm, yellow choler, and black choler.*

Since the nourishment received is composed of the four elements (as are all things in the world), it is broken down within the body of a living creature into four parts again. The richest <note>original: "fattest"</note> part of it is turned into *blood*; one part into *saliva* or *phlegm*; one part into *yellow choler* <term>bile</term>; and one part into *black choler* or *melancholy*. *Black choler, through its density <term>original "grossness": thickness or lack of refinement</term>, represents the earth; phlegm represents water; blood represents air; and yellow choler represents fire.* 

However, they differ in color and flavor. Melancholy is black and bitter; phlegm is white and tasteless; blood is red and sweet; and yellow choler is yellow and bitter. It should be noted that among these four, blood is produced in the greatest abundance because it contains the actual substance of the nourishment; to which

And hence it is that physicians, as well as common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note>, often speak of the blood as if it were the only food of life.

*XIII The production <note>original: "progeneration"</note> of vital humors is achieved through digestion <note>original: "concoction"</note>.*

For <term>concoction</term> <note>the process of "cooking" or digesting food within the body to create humors</note> alters the matter by the force of heat.

*XIV Digestion <note>original: "concoction"</note> in a living creature is done in the same manner as distillation in stills <note>original: "alembicks"</note>: namely, by the heating of the matter and its transformation <note>original: "resolution"</note> into vapors, the mixing of those vapors together, and by a new solidification <note>original: "coagulation"</note> of them again.*

For every living body is a true <term>alembick</term> <note>an early chemical apparatus used for distilling liquids</note>, full of perpetual heat and vapors. For life is heat, and heat cannot help but boil the matter that is put in, and by thinning <note>original: "attenuation"</note> it, turn it into vapors.

*XV Now in every digestion <note>original: "concoction"</note>, there is a separation of the useful <note>original: "profitable"</note> parts from the useless: the first are digested and absorbed <note>original: "assimilated"</note>,*

In the same way, in stills <term>alembics</term> <note>original: "Alembicks" — early chemical apparatus used for distillation</note>, the more refined and useful parts (that is, the more oily and spirited portions) are turned into vapor, gathered again into drops, and concentrated into a thick substance. However, the coarser and impure parts, called dregs and waste <term>excrements</term>, sink to the bottom and are later thrown away.

XVI *Every process of digestion <term>concoction</term> <note>In early modern science, "concoction" referred to the "cooking" or refining of substances within the body or by heat to make them useful</note> leaves behind useless remains, which are called waste <term>excrements</term> and dross.*

We see this same principle at work in the refining <term>decoction</term> of metals. Now, we must note that plants produce little or no waste. This is because they are nourished by a simple and uniform sap <note>original: "juice"</note> that is entirely absorbed into their nature. If any residue does remain, it oozes out as gum. 

Living creatures, however, because they are composed of many different types of parts, require a complex diet—solid and soft, dry and moist, hot and cold, and so on. This is so that the more solid parts of the body can also receive nourishment. From this, every part draws what will benefit itself through absorption <term>assimilation</term>, and the rest must necessarily be strained out. Another reason for this is that plants are sustained by a small amount of vital spirit that does not evaporate, whereas living creatures...

Creatures are full of spirit (for otherwise such a gross physical frame could not be sustained and wielded), and that spirit is continually thinned <note>original: attenuated</note> and spent. *XVII The principal digestion <note>original: concoction</note> in a living creature is threefold: Chylification, Sanguification, and Membrification.*

The first is made in the stomach; the second in the liver; and the last in all the members of the body.

*XVIII Every one of these digestion processes has three types of vessels: 1. of ingestion [intake]; 2. of digestion [processing]; 3. of egestion [expulsion].*

*XIX The vessels of Chylification <note>the process of turning food into chyle</note> were: 1. the mouth and the throat; 2. the stomach or <term>ventricle</term> <note>original: ventricle; in this context, the main cavity of the stomach</note>; 3. the intestines and the anus.*

For the food, being received at the mouth, is chewed with the teeth or jaws and passed through the throat. It is "boiled" in the stomach as if it were in a sealed <term>alembic</term> <note>an early chemical vessel used for distillation</note> for several hours. And from there, by a process of evaporation, it passes into the intestines <note>original: entrals</note> (for the opening of the stomach toward the throat is shut up) and becomes <term>chyle</term> <note>original: Chylus</note>—that is, a certain fermented substance like pap, or white broth.

As soon as the limbs <note>original: "members"</note> are hardened (after youth), the living creature ceases to grow. Yet, it continues to gain solidity and strength as long as the three stages of digestion <note>original: "concoctions"</note> are correctly performed. But when the vessels of digestion begin to dry up as well, the living creature begins to wither away, and life grows feeble until it fails and is extinguished.

### Of the Vital Faculty <note>In early modern medicine, the "vital faculty" referred to the power of the heart and lungs to sustain life through the distribution of "vital spirits."</note>

XXV **Life** in a living creature is a mixture of the spirits <note>In this context, "spirits" refers to the subtle fluids or vapors believed to circulate through the body to provide life and movement.</note> with the blood and limbs, so that they are all warm, possess sensation, and can move themselves.

Therefore, the life of living creatures consists of heat, sense, and motion. This is clear, for if any creature has neither motion, nor sense, nor heat, it is not alive.

XXVI Therefore, every living creature is full of heat, which is sometimes stronger and sometimes weaker.

For every living creature is nourished. As was shown previously, nourishment is only produced through digestion <note>original: "concoction"</note>. However, reason teaches that digestion is not achieved except by heat and fire. Therefore, it remains to be explained: from where does a living creature get heat and fire, and by what means is it kindled?

Hence, the heart is commonly said to be the first part that lives and the last that dies.

Since that inflammation of the heart produces fumes or vapors (though very thin ones), the lungs—through the same continual breathing in—exhale those vapors through the throat. This flame—or thinned <note>original: "attenuated"</note> and extremely hot blood—is called the *spirit of life*. As it spreads through the arteries (which accompany the veins in every direction), it sustains <note>original: "cherisheth"</note> the heat of both the blood in the veins and all the members throughout the whole body. 

Because it would be dangerous for this vital spirit to be destroyed, the arteries are hidden beneath the veins, except in two or three places where they stand out slightly. This is so that the beating of that spirit (as well as the heart itself when a hand is laid upon the chest) may be observed, and from that, the state of the heart may be known.

Generally speaking, there is only one sense, and that is touch. For nothing can be perceived unless it touches us, either directly at hand or from a distance. There is no sense at all for things that are absent.

XL *Therefore, in every sensation, the animal spirit <term>animal spirit: thought to be a subtle fluid in the nerves that processed sensory information and directed movement</term> is affected <note>original: "suffers"</note> by the object perceived.*

It is very evident that there is no sensation except through being acted upon <note>original: "passion"</note>. For we do not perceive heat or cold unless we ourselves become hot or cold; nor sweet and bitter unless we become sweet or bitter; nor color unless we are colored by it. Our spirit, residing in the organs, is touched and affected. Therefore, things that are like us are not perceived; for example, heat that is identical to our own heat does not affect us. But we must observe that for the organs to perceive any qualities in objects, they must lack those qualities themselves—for instance, the apple of the eye <term>apple of the eye: the pupil or the eye itself</term> lacks its own color, the tongue lacks flavor <note>original: "favour"</note>, and so on.

### XLII *The effect of sensation is pleasure or pain.*

Pleasure occurs if the sense is affected gently and easily by something agreeable to it, with a pleasant stimulation <note>original: "titillation"</note>. Pain <note>original: "griefe"</note> occurs if it is affected by something contrary to it, or suddenly by injury to the organ.

### XLIV

The sense of touch has *for its instrument the nervous skin, as well as all the nervous and membrane-like <note>original: "membranaceous"</note> parts of the body.*

Therefore hair, nails, and bones do not feel, etc., even if you cut or burn them, because they have no nerves running through them. Hence, the pain under the nails and in the membranes of the bones is most acute. Now, since the skin of the body is very sticky <note>original: "glutinous"</note> and entirely made of nerves, it is covered with a thin skin called in Latin the <term>Cuticula</term> <note>modern: the cuticle or epidermis</note>—which we sometimes see come off in limbs <note>original: "members"</note> that are scorched or bruised. This outer layer is void of feeling to restrain the intensity <note>original: "violence"</note> of the sense, so it does not put the living creature in continual pain and trouble by being too sensitive.

XLVII. *Hearing uses the ear as its organ. It now appears from this: 1. *Why sounds that are too high-pitched <note>original: "sharpe"</note> or too low-pitched <note>original: "dull"</note> offend us, while moderate ones please us?* This is because they harmonize better with our own spirit.

2. *Why a sound also travels in a slanted direction <note>original: "obliquely"</note>?* This is because the air, once moved, moves the air next to it in every direction through the process of diffusion.

3. *Why a sound that spreads out in every direction gradually fades away?* This is because it behaves just like a stone falling into a pool of water. However, there is a difference: the water continues to ripple for a long time within that entire circle, whereas the circle of moved air <note>OCR reads "rain," likely a transcription error for "air"</note> passes away all at once. For the sound of a bell does not remain in the air, but resonates <note>original: "is wheeled about"</note> within the vibrating bell itself.

4. *Why, when one person hears a sound, everyone else hears the same thing?* This is because a sound is a physical disturbance <note>original: "commotion"</note> of the air, which reaches the ears of everyone located within that circle.

5. *What an echo is?* Namely, it is a sound reflected from hollow places. It occurs in the same way that ripples of water in a vessel, after they have hit the sides, return again toward the center.

6. *Why does whiteness scatter <note>original: "disgregates"</note> the sight, and if it is excessive, dissipate and corrupt it?*
Because it is the light itself reflecting, whose nature is to penetrate, thin out, pull apart, and diffuse the object. For it was sent into the world for that very purpose.

L. <term>Vision</term> is three-fold: straight, reflected, and refracted.

*Direct* or straight vision is how light is seen—for example, the sun or a fire. In this case, the light presents itself to the eye in a single line.

*Reflected* vision is how other things are seen in the open air. In this case, the light reflected from objects comes to the eye by a second line (since, by the first line, the light falls upon the object, and by the second line, it travels from there to the eye).

*Refracted* vision is how things are seen through two different substances <note>original: "a double medium"</note>, and thus by bent <note>original: "refracted"</note> lines. This happens when an oar or a pole appears broken in the water. It also occurs when a piece of money at the bottom of a container full of water seems larger and closer to the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note>, so that one may step back and still see it.

# On the power of motion. <note>original: "motive faculty"</note>

L I. *Motion was given to a living creature:*
1. *To seek its food.*
2. *For those actions to which everyone is destined.*
3. *To preserve the vigor of life.*

For a living creature, being of a more tender constitution than a plant, would more easily rot <note>original: "putrifie"</note> and perish if it were not quickened by very frequent motion. Therefore the **Creator** has most wisely provided for our good, so that we cannot even take our food <note>original: "meat"</note> without labor and motion.

L II. *The moving principle is the animal spirit.* <note>In historical physiology, "animal spirits" were thought to be a subtle, fluid-like substance that traveled through the nerves to produce movement and sensation.</note>

Therefore a body without life, though ever so well furnished with **organs**, does not move; and when the brain—the seat of the *animal spirits*—is poorly affected (for example, with either dizziness <note>original: "giddinesse"</note> or overindulgence <note>original: "surfet"</note>), the limbs <note>original: "members"</note> presently fail, or at least stumble and totter. And when the nerve of any limb is blocked, it is immediately deprived of both motion and sense, as may be seen in *palsy* <note>original: "palsie"; paralysis or tremors</note> and *apoplexy* <note>a stroke</note>.

<meta>L III. Now</meta>

<summary>This page explains the necessity of motion for living creatures—to find food, fulfill their purpose, and maintain health—and identifies "animal spirits" originating in the brain as the primary driver of movement.</summary>
<keywords>motive faculty, animal spirits, health, physiology, paralysis, Creator, brain, motion</keywords>

LIII. *Now the **animal spirit** <note>original: "animall spirit"; in early modern physiology, this was a refined substance believed to travel through the nerves to facilitate sensation and thought.</note> moves either itself only, or the **vital spirit** <note>original: "vitall spirit"; the substance believed to reside in the heart and blood, responsible for life, heat, and emotion.</note> with it: or lastly, the members of the body as well.*

LIV. *The animal spirit moves itself perpetually, sometimes more, sometimes less: namely, running in and out of the organs of the senses: or in whatever way it stirs itself in its workshop <note>original: "work-house"; likely referring to the brain.</note>.*

For from this inward motion of it, there are perpetual fantasies or imaginations even in sleep, which we then call dreams.

LV. *It carries the vital spirit along with it when, at the sensation of something either pleasing or displeasing, it conveys itself back and forth through the body, taking that spirit with it as if to aid it; this occurs in joy and sorrow; hope and fear; gratitude <note>original: "gratulation"</note> and repentance; and last of all, in anger.*

For joy is a motion in which the spirit pours itself out at the sensation of a pleasant object, as though it would join itself with the thing that it desires. From this comes that lively color in the face of a joyful person, caused by the vital spirit flowing there with a most pure portion of the blood. And this is the reason why moderate joy purifies the blood and is helpful for prolonging life. See *Proverbs 15:13 and 17:22.*

<summary>This page explains how the "animal spirit" (nerves/thought) and "vital spirit" (circulatory/emotion) interact to create dreams and physical responses to emotions like joy, which is said to improve health.</summary>
<keywords>animal spirit, vital spirit, physiology, dreams, psychology of joy, blood, Proverbs</keywords>

Sorrow is a motion where the **vital spirit** <note>original: "vitall spirit"; the life-giving force believed to circulate through the body</note> at the perception of an object that displeases it, runs to its center; the heart, as it were, feels a hurtful thing. This causes paleness in the face of those who are frightened and a stiffness of the skin and hair <note>referring to goosebumps or hair standing on end</note>; hence there is also a danger of death if anyone is often and greatly affected with sorrow. Similar motions occur in *hope* and *fear*, *joy* and *sorrow*; that is, in the perception of good or bad, whether present or past.

But *anger* is a mixed motion, whereby the spirit flies to the center for fear of injury and then pours itself out again, as if in revenge. Hence, those who are angry are first pale and afterwards red, etc.

*N. w.* <note>Note well</note> All these motions, commonly called <term>affections</term> <note>original: "affections"; here meaning emotions or psychological states</note> or *passions* of the mind, are common to all living creatures. But they vary in degree: for *Sanguine* <note>the humor associated with blood; characterized by a cheerful temperament</note> creatures are merry; *Melancholy* <note>the humor associated with black bile; characterized by sadness</note> are sad; *Phlegmatic* <note>the humor associated with phlegm; characterized by being slow or "faint"</note> are faint; and *Choleric* <note>the humor associated with yellow bile; characterized by being "furious"</note> are furious, etc.

*LVI. The said animal spirit moves the limbs, but with the use of instruments: tendons and muscles, and the joints of the bones.*

In just the same way, for the motion of a living creature, there are required:
1. **Joints or knuckles** of bones. For bones were given to a living creature so that he might stand upright; but so that he might bend as well, his bones were not given to him as one continuous piece, but were divided by joints in the limbs.

2. **Certain ligaments** fastened around the bones, by which contraction <note>original: "attraction"</note> and relaxation might be made. They call this flesh or fleshy purse a muscle, and every limb <note>original: "member"</note> has many of them. This is not only to prevent the tendons from moving out of place when they are pulled, or to keep bones and tendons from being damaged by frequently rubbing against one another, or simply for the shape of a living creature (for what kind of body would it be if it consisted only of bones, veins, nerves, and tendons? The only possible result of this is the bending of that limb.

From this it appears: 1. That the *animal spirit* can move nothing without an *<term>organ</term>* <note>here meaning a physical tool or biological mechanism</note>. For why can no man bend his knees forward? Because he lacks a joint <note>original: "knuckle"</note> above them. Why can no man move his ear? Because that part of the body lacks muscles, and so on.

The more muscles are given to any limb <note>original: "member"</note>, the more nimble it is in motion. By how ***much the bigger they are, so much the stronger they are***. For example, in the hands and feet, they are numerous so that they might be sufficiently able to undergo a variety of labors and walking <note>original: "going"</note>. It appears also why those who are muscular or brawny are strong, while those who are thin are weak.

3. It appears also that the animal spirit <term>In early physiology, the "animal spirit" (from the Latin *spiritus animalis*, meaning "spirit of the soul") was believed to be a subtle fluid or gas that traveled through the nerves to facilitate sensation and movement.</term> is most active in motion, running to and fro at the command of the imagination <note>original: "phantasie"</note> most speedily through the nerves and arteries.

4. The motion of a living creature is composed of agitative, expansive, contractive, impulsive, and continuative motion. For the animal spirit conveys itself at the pleasure of the imagination into this or that muscle. And the muscle, giving place to the spirit flowing in, stretches itself out. Then, when the muscle is stretched in breadth, its length must of necessity be contracted. The tendon follows the muscle as it contracts and draws with it the head of the next bone by the motion of continuity—all with inexplicable quickness.

6. And because this involves effort, it cannot be done without fatigue <note>original: "wearineffe"</note>.

7. And because fatigue occurs, there is sometimes a need for rest, which is provided in three ways: 1. *Standing*, 2. *Sitting*, and 3. *Lying down*. *Standing* is a resting of the feet, but with the body inclined toward movement; therefore, it is achieved through balance <note>original: "libration"; the subtle shifting of weight to maintain an upright posture</note>. *Sitting* is rest centered in the middle of the body, which allows the other parts to be more easily kept in balance <note>original: "Æquilibrio"</note>. *Lying down* is total rest; that is, stretching out the body completely. However, just as too much motion brings fatigue, too much rest causes boredom <note>original: "tediousnesse"</note>, because the spirit <note>In early modern physiology, "spirits" were the subtle fluids or forces believed to flow through the nerves to cause motion and sensation.</note> loves to move itself. Keeping the limbs in the same position for a long time during rest is also uncomfortable, both because the lower limbs are pressed by the weight of the upper ones, and because the spirit desires to move in some direction. This is why we often turn ourselves over in our sleep.

LVII. *Every living creature (except fish) was given lungs to cool the heart, along with a gristly pipe called the rough artery. Nevertheless, this pipe also serves to produce a voice; because in its upper part, it has the form of a pipe where the air, being struck, may be divided and sent sounding forth.*

LVIII. *And so that the voice might be both raised and lowered, that pipe is composed of gristly rings. If the lowest of these opposes the air as it passes by, there is a deep vibration <note>original: "repercussion"</note>—that is, a low <note>original: "grave"</note> voice. But if the highest ring does so, there is a high vibration—that is, a shrill voice. Anyone may test this for themselves.*

LIX. *And so that the sound may be articulate (as in speech and the singing of some birds), the tongue—moving the sound back and forth—as well as the lips, the teeth, the nostrils, and the throat, perform this task.*

### Concerning the defensive faculty.

LX. *If the **animal spirit** <note>original: "animall spirit"; in early modern physiology, "animal spirits" were believed to be subtle fluids or vapors that traveled through the nerves to enable sensation and movement.</note> perceives any hostile thing approaching, it immediately turns to its weapons. This is done either to defend itself (by raising its hairs, bristles, scales, or prickles) or to attack and hurt its enemies (using its horns, nails, wings, beak, hands, etc.).*

By what strength this is achieved can already be understood from what has been said before.

### Concerning the generative faculty.

Because living creatures, as well as plants, are mortal beings, they must necessarily reproduce for the preservation of their **species**. Regarding this, note the following axioms:

LXI. *Because the **generation** <note>original: "generation"; the process of reproduction and development of offspring.</note> of living creatures—due to the large number and the tenderness of their limbs—could not be suitably carried out within the earth, they were given different sexes. And it was ordained that the new living creature should be formed within the very body of the living creature itself.*

Just as the sun by its heat begets plants in the womb of the earth, it may also create those living things whose formation is finished within a few days—such as worms, mice, and various insects. This occurs either by the seed of those same living creatures falling into suitable matter <note>original: "apt matter"</note> scattered about, or by the "spirit of the universe" <note>original: "spirit of the universe"; a vital force believed by early modern thinkers to animate matter</note> falling into suitable matter. 

However, the sun cannot beget more "perfect" living creatures <note>original: "perfect"; meaning biologically complex or fully developed</note> which consist of many solid body parts and require <note>original: "want"</note> much time for their formation (such as a man, a horse, or an elephant). Because the sun cannot stay so long in the same region of the sky <note>original: "coast of heaven"</note>, the young offspring would be ruined before it could reach full development <note>original: "perfection"</note>. 

Therefore, the most wise Creator of things appointed the place of formation to be not in the earth, but within the living creature itself. He formed two sexes so that one might play the part of the plant bearing the seed, and the other might play the part of the earth—cherishing and, as it were, hatching the seed. This alone, and no other, is the purpose of the different sexes in all living creatures. Woe to the rashness and madness of men who abuse them! No beast does so. The organs <note>original: "members"</note> by which the sexes differ are the same in number, location, and form, and differ in nothing

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physiological differences between males and females and begins a new section on the development and classification of living creatures.</meta>

...almost except in regard to *the exterior* and *the interior* <note>original: *exterius* and *interius*</note>: specifically, the greater force of heat in the male pushes the genitals outward, while in the female, due to weaker heat, these members remain inside, as anatomists know.

LXII *The spirit is the director of all generation <term>the process of biological creation or reproduction</term>, just as it is in plants. Once this spirit is heated within the seed, it first forms a place to reside—that is, the brain and the head. From there, it ventures out to form the rest of the body's parts gradually and gently. By returning to its seat, it rests and operates in turns, which is the origin of waking and sleeping.*

Therefore, the formation of a living creature does not begin from the heart, as *Aristotle* thought, but from the head. The head is, in a sense, the entire living creature; the rest of the body is merely a structure of organs for various operations. This is clearly seen because some living creatures (such as fish <note>Historically, some thinkers incorrectly believed that "lower" animals or certain fish lacked a true heart because their circulatory systems differed from mammals.</note>) have no heart, but none are without a head and brain.

LXIII *A living creature, according to the differences in its motion, is:* 1. *Reptile* <term>creatures that crawl or creep</term>. 2. *Gressile* <term>creatures that walk or step</term>. 3. *Natatile* <term>creatures that swim</term>. 4. *Volatile* <term>creatures that fly</term>.

LXIV A *Reptile, or a creeping thing*, is a living creature with a *long body* that lacks feet, yet is made of *joints* (or *gristly rings*). By the *contraction and extension* of these joints, it *winds itself up and reaches out*: such as *worms and serpents*. <note>original: "wanting feet" meaning lacking feet.</note>

LXV *Gressile* <note>original: *Gressile*, from the Latin *gressus*, meaning "walking" or "stepping."</note> refers to that which has feet (two or more) and walks; such as a *lizard*, a *mouse*, a *dog*, etc.

LXVI *Natatile* <note>original: *Natatile*, from the Latin *natatilis*, meaning "able to swim."</note> refers to that which passes through the water by the help of *fins*: it is called a *fish*. Among these, *crabs* and various *sea-monsters* are also included.

LXVII *Volatile* <note>original: *Volatile*, from the Latin *volatilis*, meaning "flying" or "winged."</note> refers to that which moves through the air by the flapping of its *wings*; and it is called a *bird*.

The *lightness* of birds that allows them to *fly* comes from their *plumosity* <note>The state of being feathered.</note>. For every *plume* or *feather*—not only in the *stalk* but through all its parts and the smallest particles of those parts—is *hollow* and full of *spirit* and *vapor*. <note>In early modern science, "spirit" and "vapor" were thought to be light, warm substances that provided buoyancy and life to the body.</note> For this reason, *no birds urinate*, because all their *moisture* perpetually *evaporates into their feathers*. It is *impossible*, therefore, for a *man to fly*, even if he *fits himself with wings*, because he *lacks the feathers to lift him*; and those feathers which he might attach to himself are *dead* and *devoid of heat and spirit*.

### LXVIII *Small living things are called by the special name "insects," such as flies, worms, etc.*

They are called insects because of the "incisions" <note>original: "incisions"; from the Latin *insectum*, meaning "cut into," referring to the segmented bodies of these creatures</note> or segments by which their bodies appear to be divided. These may be classified in the same way as other animals. For example, worms are *creeping* <note>original: "Reptile"</note> creatures; lice, fleas, bedbugs <note>original: "Punies," likely from the French *punaise*</note>, spiders, and so on are *walking* <note>original: "Gressile"</note> creatures; the water-spider and the horse-leech are *swimming* <note>original: "Natatile"</note> creatures; and flies and gnats are *flying* <note>original: "Volatile"</note> creatures. There are infinite variations among these, so that here too we find a very clear mirror <note>original: "glasse"</note> of the Creator's wonderful wisdom. This variety serves as a school for mankind to learn virtues and abandon vices, as an image of both is clearly seen in these living creatures—a point the Bible <note>original: "Scripture"</note> often emphasizes.

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the preservation and restoration of life, providing examples of animals and humans who appear dead but are later revived.</meta>

<page-num>205</page-num>

...is found in flies, spiders, frogs, swallows, etc., which in winter lie as though they were dead in the cracks of walls, or crevices <note>original: "chaps"</note> of the earth, or under the water; yet when the spring arrives, they are alive again. Similarly, flies choked in water come to life again in warm ashes <note>original: "cinders"</note>. It is also certain that men who have been strangled have been brought back to life after several hours. Furthermore, there is a well-known example of a boy killed by the cold and found four days later, who was revived with warm compresses <note>original: "foments," meaning medicinal applications of heat and moisture used to stimulate the body</note>. Trances lasting for several days are commonly known from this: some people ready to be buried as though they were truly dead—and indeed, some even buried—have lived again. Some geographers have written that in the farthest parts of *Moscovia* <note>modern: Russia</note>, men are frozen every year by extreme cold and yet come back to life like swallows. However, since this is an uncertain claim, we will set it aside for now.

III. The third and most strange point is this: that the spirit flows out with the blood that is shed, yet it does not cease to maintain its connection <note>original: "consent," implying a sympathetic or harmonious link</note> with the spirit remaining within the body (whether the greater part remains or only the remnants <note>original: "relicks"</note>).

[They] do not cure the wound itself, but the instrument with which the wound was given, or the garment, wood, or earth sprinkled with the blood from the wound, is the only thing anointed; and then the wound closes and heals naturally <note>original: "kindly," meaning according to its natural state</note>. Some deny that this is done by natural means, but they do not sufficiently consider the secret strength of nature. Yet examples show that this kind of cure—using an ointment made with entirely natural things (even with nothing but the grease of the axle <note>original: "axeltree"</note> scraped off from a cart)—has certain success, without using any superstition.

Therefore, it is believable that the spirit <note>In early modern physiology, "spirits" were subtle fluids or vapors that carried life and sensation through the body.</note> poured out of the body with the blood that is shed remains partly in the blood and partly on the instrument itself (for it cannot exist without matter). Being forced from there by the fat that is applied, it returns to the whole body and replenishes it. Perhaps the observation concerning the venom of a snake, viper, or scorpion delivered into a person through a bite relates to this as well. For if the same beast, or even just its blood or fat, is immediately applied to the wound, it sucks the venom out again because the venom returns to its own like substance <note>original: "connaturall," meaning something of the same nature</note>. More instances of this kind might be observed through proven experiments.

<meta>This page concludes a discussion on the "animal spirit" and its role in spontaneous generation before beginning a new chapter on the nature of humanity.</meta>

6 Finally, it is worthy of our observation that the **animal spirit** <term>animal spirit: In early modern science, these were believed to be subtle fluids or essences that facilitated sensation and movement in living beings.</term> forms living creatures of another kind rather than completely forsaking putrefying <note>original: "putrifying"; decaying</note> matter—namely, worms and similar creatures. Now, it is certain by experience that from dead and decayed living creatures, those specific living creatures are bred upon which they were accustomed to feed when they were alive. For example, serpents are bred from the flesh of storks, spiders from hens, frogs from ducks, and so on. *Johann Poppus*, a distiller from *Coburg*, has taught—following the work of others—that this will occur if they are buried in dung. It appears, then, that the animal spirit is everywhere and is very diligently busy with the animating <note>original: "animating"; the process of giving life or movement to</note> of bodies.

I **A** *Man is a living creature, endowed with an immortal soul.*

<margin>a</margin>II. Therefore, he is composed of three things: a body, a spirit, and a soul.

So the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul</note> testifies in *1 Thessalonians 5:23*: *May your whole spirit, soul, and body be kept blameless.* And so in *1 Corinthians 14:14*, he distinguishes between the *spirit and the mind*. And indeed, it is so: we have a body composed of the elements just like the animals <note>original: "bruits," meaning brute beasts</note>; we have a spirit from the spirit of the world, just as they do; but the soul or mind is from God. The first (the body) we carry about us as mortal; the second (the spirit) is dissolvable <note>original: "dissipable," meaning able to be scattered or evaporated</note>; but the last (the soul) endures forever even without the body, as we are assured by faith. Therefore, when you see a man, think that you see a king, royally clothed and sitting on his royal throne. For the *mind* is a king, his robe is the *spirit*, and his throne is the *body*.

*III. The body is the organ and habitation of the spirit; but the spirit is the habitation and mansion of the soul.*

For just as the spirit dwells in the body and guides it, as a pilot does a ship, so the soul dwells in the spirit and rules it. And as a body without a spirit neither moves itself nor has any sense of anything (as is seen in a dead carcass), so the spirit without the mind has no reason, nor

*IV. Just as the spirit is affected by the body, so is the mind affected by the spirit.*

For just as when the body is diseased, the spirit is immediately saddened or hindered from its action; so when the spirit is ill-disposed, the mind cannot perform its functions skillfully—as we may see in those who are drunk, melancholy, or insane, etc. Because of this, the gifts of the mind follow the temperament <note>original: "temperature"; referring to the "tempering" or balance of the four bodily humors which were believed to determine personality.</note> of the body. This explains why one person is more talented, courteous, chaste, or courageous than another. Since the body and the soul are extremes—the one earthly, the other heavenly; the one animalistic, the other rational; the one mortal, the other immortal—they are always contrary to one another in their inclinations. Now, the *spirit*, which is placed between them, truly ought to obey the superior part and keep the lower part in order at its command. Nevertheless, it often happens that the spirit is carried away by the flesh and becomes brutish.

V. *Such a body was given to man as might fitly serve all the uses of his rational soul <note>original: "reasonable soule"</note>. Therefore, it is: 1. Furnished with many organs. 2. Erect. 3. He alone has obtained an upright stature, lest he should live unmindful of his true home, Heaven. Furthermore, he alone was made naked and unarmed; both of these were granted through the unique favors of God. For while other living creatures always carry their garments (hair, feathers, shells) and their weapons (sharp prickles, horns) with them, what are these but burdens and hindrances to various actions? The liberty granted to man, along with his industry in providing, fitting, and storing all things for his use and pleasure, is something more divine.

*Observe this also:* When the animal spirit looks forward and takes in new images of things through the senses, it is said *to learn*. If the senses perceive something from far away, or in a weak and obscure manner, it is a *general conception*. For example, when I see something moving far away, I conclude it is a living creature; when I come closer, I know it is a man; and at length, I recognize him as this or that specific man, and so on.

XII **There are three faculties of the human mind: the Understanding, the Will, and the Conscience.**

These correspond to the three functions of the animal spirit <note>original: "animall spirit"; the "animal" spirit was believed to be the most refined spirit, located in the brain and responsible for sensation and movement</note>, or to the *inward senses*, from which they also result. Therefore, the three inward senses—**Attention, Judgment, and Memory**—are instruments by which the soul uses the Understanding, Will, and Conscience. For by diligent attention, it creates understanding of things; by imagination or judging, it makes a choice (that is, to will or to refuse <note>original: "nill"</note>); and by remembrance, it forms the conscience.

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded by discussing the faculties of the soul; this page defines "understanding" and "reasoning," illustrating how humans use logic to predict outcomes while animals rely solely on past experience.</meta>

XIII *The understanding is a faculty of the rational soul <term>original: reasonable soule</term>, gathering things unknown from things known and, by comparing uncertain things together, drawing out certain truths through reasoning.*

XIV *To reason is to investigate the reasons and causes of why anything exists or does not exist, by thinking about it.*

For the mind or reason takes the experiences <term>original: experiments</term> gathered from the senses and first forms certain general concepts <term>original: notions</term> for itself. For example, when it sees that fire scorches all things, it forms a rule for itself: *All fire burns, etc.* These kinds of experimental concepts are called "principles," from which the understanding frames a logical argument <term>original: discourse</term> as the occasion arises. 

For example, if gold melts in fire, then it is also hot and burns while it is melted. From this follows this conclusion: therefore, if a craftsman pours gold into his hand, he will be burned by it. Notice how this is understanding—and of a thing never before seen! An animal <term>original: bruite</term> cannot achieve this, for they do not reason but rely simply on their experiences. For instance, if a dog is beaten with a staff, he runs away later at the sight of a staff because the memory of his recent suffering returns to him:

memory: but that he should reason (for example: a staff is hard; pain was caused to me by a staff; therefore every hard thing struck against the body causes pain), this he cannot do. Therefore, *to understand* <note>original: *intelligere*</note> is *to read between* <note>original: *inter legere*</note>; that is, to choose and determine among many things what is true and what is not.

XV. *When reasoning* <term>original: ratiocination</term> *aligns with itself in every way, it produces truth* <term>original: verity</term>; *if there is a gap anywhere, it produces error.*

XVI. *Quickness of reasoning is called ingenuity; solidity is called judgment; and their absence is called dullness.*

For he is *ingenious* who perceives and speaks readily; he is *judicious* who, with a certain natural quickness, pays attention to whether the reasoning aligns sufficiently in every way. He is *dull* who has neither of these qualities. The first two come from the *mixture* <note>original: *temperature*</note> *of blood and melancholy* <note>In the ancient theory of the "four humors," melancholy was thought to be a cold, dry fluid that influenced a person's temperament and intellect.</note>; the last comes from an abundance of phlegm. For melancholy (meaning not the thick and dreg-filled kind, but the pure kind) tempered with much blood, gives a nimble wit; but when mixed with less blood, it gives a piercing and steady judgment. This is made clear by this comparison <note>original: *similitude*</note>: A mirror <note>original: *glasse*</note> receiving and reflecting shapes excel—

If iron or steel could be used, it is certain that the images would be brighter due to the blackness.)

In the same way, the animal spirits <term>In early modern medicine, "animal spirits" were believed to be a subtle fluid that transmitted thoughts and sensations through the nervous system.</term>, receiving agility from pure **blood** and strength and constancy from **melancholy** <note>original: "Melancholy"; here referring to the humor of black bile, associated with depth of thought.</note>, make men **ingenious**. When the prevailing melancholy clears the imagination, it makes them **judicious**; however, an excess of **phlegm** <note>original: "flegme"</note> overflowing both makes men **stupid**. **Yellow bile** <note>original: "Yellow choler"</note> contributes nothing but mobility to the emotions <note>original: "affections"</note>; for this reason, it is not without cause called the whetstone of wits.

For example, when a Christian chooses drunkenness rather than sobriety (even though he is taught otherwise), he does it because the intellect—deceived by the senses—judges it better to please the palate than to be tormented with thirst.

XXI *It appears from what has been said that man is rightly called a* <term>microcosm <note>original Greek: μικρόκοσμος (mikrokosmos)</note></term>, *a little world. This is because:*

2. *He resembles the universe in the arrangement of his members. Just as the universe is divided into three parts—the elementary <note>The physical realm of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note>, the celestial <note>The realm of the stars and planets.</note>, and the super-celestial <note>The highest spiritual or divine realm.</note>—so a man has three "ventres" or cavities. The lowest serves for nutrition; the middle (the chest) contains the workhouse of life and the fountain of heat; and the highest (the head) is where the animal spirits <note>In historical medicine, "animal spirits" were thought to be the refined substances that allowed the mind and soul to communicate with the body.</note> reside, and within them, reason—the image of God.*

3. *There is an analogy <note>A structural or functional similarity.</note> between the parts of the world and the parts of the body.* For example: flesh represents the earth; bones represent stones; blood and other humours <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to govern health and temperament.</note> represent the waters; the vapors which fill the body represent the air; and the vital spirit represents the heavens.

2. *Man is "all" because he has the capacity to be anything; that is, either most excellent or very base.* For if he gives himself over to earthly things, he becomes brutish and falls back again to nothingness; but if he gives himself to heavenly things, he is in a manner <term>deified</term> <note>Made god-like or elevated to a divine state through spiritual union</note> and rises above all other creatures.

## CHAPTER XII.

### *Of Angels.*

**W**e join this treatise concerning Angels with the study of Physics <note>original: "Phyſicks"; in this context, it refers to Natural Philosophy or the study of the physical, created world</note> because they are also a part of the created world and are next to humanity in the hierarchy of creatures. By understanding human nature, the nature of *Angels* is more easily explained. Therefore, we will conclude this subject in a few *Aphorisms* <note>original: "Aphoriſmes"; concise statements of principles or truths</note>.

1. *There are Angels.*

Divine testimonies and appearances <note>original: "apparitions"</note> testify to this, as does a threefold reason:

1. Vapors, solid objects <note>original: "concretes"</note>, plants, and living creatures are a mixture of "water" (matter) and spirit. Since there is matter without spirit (the pure elements), there must also be spirit without matter.

2. Just as the matter of the world is divided into four kinds (the four elements), so we see the spirit of the world is distinguished into the natural, vital, animal, and mental spirit. Now, the lowest degree of spirit is found alone (as in solid objects). Therefore, the highest degree may also be found alone—specifically, in the Angels.

3. *Every creature is*

<summary>This page introduces the study of angels as a branch of natural philosophy, arguing for their existence based on a logical hierarchy of matter and spirit.</summary>
<keywords>Angels, Physics, Natural Philosophy, Elements, Spirit, Matter, Aphorisms, hierarchy of creatures</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues a philosophical argument regarding the hierarchy of existence, moving from the nature of physical matter toward the nature of spiritual beings.</meta>

...compounded of Being <note>original: "Entitie"</note> and nothingness <note>original: "Nihility"</note>. (For they were nothing before the creation, but now they are something because the Creator has bestowed on them a portion of His Being, in varying degrees. The more Being any thing has, the further it is from nothingness, and vice versa.) 

Since there is a "first degree" away from nothingness—which is *Chaos* <note>original: "Chaos," referring here to the formless, rudimentary state of matter at the start of creation</note>, the very beginning of Being—there must also be a "last degree" that comes closest to pure Being. Human beings are not this last degree because, by having physical matter mixed into our nature, we still share much with nothingness. Therefore, by necessity, there must be a creature in which all other perfections remain once materiality is taken away. *And that creature is an angel.*

### II *An angel is an incorporeal man.*

*An angel* <note>incorporeal: having no physical body or material form</note> may be called a "man" in the same sense that a man is called an "animal," an animal is called a "plant," and a plant is called a "physical composite" <note>original: "a concrete"</note>, and so on (as we have established in their definitions). This is because the form of the preceding category is included, with only a new perfection added to it. For a human is a rational creature made after the *Image of God* and is immortal; an *angel* is the same, but for the sake of even greater perfection, it is free from a body.

<keywords>angelology, hierarchy of being, ontology, incorporeality, creation, rational soul, metaphysics</keywords>

But that Angels are incorporeal <note>original: "incorporous"</note> is evident because:
1. Although they are present, they are not perceived by sight or any other sense. 2. They take on earthly, watery, airy, fiery, or mixed bodies as the need requires and discard them again; they could not do this if they had bodies of their own as we do. Yet they usually appear in a human form because of the similarity of their natures, as we have already mentioned.

### III Angels were created before all visible things.

That was shown in the Appendix of the first Chapter; you may refer to it again if necessary. And Moses' words are clear:
> *In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth: and the earth was void.*

Notice that the earth was empty and void in 그 first production! Therefore, heaven was not void; it was filled with its host <term>host: a great multitude or army, referring here to the "heavenly host"</term>, the Angels.

As Moses seems to include the production of *Angels* under the name of *Heaven,*

We conclude, therefore, that the Angels were formed out of that Spirit; so that part of that spirit was left in the invisible heaven and shaped into purely spiritual substances [*Angels*], and part was sent down into the material world below. This occurred in the same way that fire was later partly left in the sky and fashioned into shining globes <note>The stars and planets</note>, and partly sunk into the depths of the earth for the formation of minerals and other purposes. Therefore, although they perceive without organs <note>bodily organs like eyes or ears</note>, we must conclude that they are not unlike our own spirits, which perceive through organs.

### V. *The Angels were created perfect.*

That is, they were finished in the same moment, so that nothing is added to their essence by external increase <note>original: "adventitious encrease"</note>. Since they are immaterial, they are also free from the law of materiality—the law that says when a thing tends toward perfection, it must be condensed, fixed, increased, and so augmented and made solid by certain additions.

### VI. *Angels are not begotten.*

Humans, animals, and plants are generated <note>produced through reproduction</note> because the spirit included in the matter spreads itself through the matter and attempts <note>original: "essayes"</note> to create new entities. But an angel

<summary>This page discusses the metaphysical nature of angels, asserting that they possess senses and emotions like human spirits but exist as immaterial, instantly perfected beings who do not undergo physical growth or reproduction.</summary>

Since it is without matter, and its essence cannot be broken apart <note>original: "dissipated"</note>, it has nowhere to transfer itself. Therefore Christ says that in Heaven we shall be like the Angels, without reproduction <note>original: "generation"</note>, or the desire for reproduction, Matthew 22:30.

### VII Angels do not die.

The spirit of animals and of plants perishes because when the matter (which is its vehicle <note>original: "chariot"</note>) is broken apart, the spirit is also broken apart. But an **Angel**, having an essence composed within itself without matter, cannot be broken apart and therefore endures.

### VIII The number of Angels is virtually infinite.

See Job 25:2–3. Yet Daniel names thousands upon thousands and myriads upon myriads, Daniel 7:10, as does John in Revelation 5:11 <note>original: "Apoc." refers to the Apocalypse, now commonly called the Book of Revelation</note>.

### IX The home of the Angels is the Heaven of Heavens, Matthew 18:10 and 6:10.

Therefore they are called the **Angels** of Heaven (Galatians 1:8) and the Host <note>In this context, "host" refers to a great multitude or a celestial army</note> of Heaven (1 Kings 22:19). For it was fitting <note>original: "meet"</note> that just as the earth, sea, air, and sky have their inhabitants, the Heaven of Heavens should not be left empty. Yet they are sent out from there for the following duties <note>original: "Ministeries"</note>.

### XI *Angels can act upon bodies, but they cannot be affected by bodies.*

Both of these are apparent by their effects. For angels carry about, move, and govern the bodies which they assume. But as for those objects that are separate from them, they can overthrow, stop, and move them from place to place with external force at their pleasure; yet they themselves, in the meantime, can be hindered or stopped by no physical body.

### XII *The power of angels exceeds the strength of any physical creature.*

For it operates:
1. Without resistance from objects, by penetrating through them. 
2. Without effort or strain, because they are not detained or hindered by their own body as our spirit is. Our spirit, being tied to the body, must of necessity drag it along laboriously, just as a snail carries her shell. Hence the angels are called *mighty in power* (Psalm 103:20) and *powers, principalities, and dominions* (Colossians 1:16).

## *XIII The agility of the angels is greater than that of any corporeal substance.*

Therefore, they are compared to wind, to fire, and to lightning (Psalm 104:4, Ezekiel 1:13, Luke 10:18), and they are called *Seraphim*, which means "flaming" (Isaiah 6:2). Yet it is certain that they move swifter than wind or lightning when they travel anywhere. For while wind and lightning move through the air with some resistance, an *angel*—being a pure spirit—does so without any resistance at all. It appears, then, that although an *angel* is not in many places at once (Daniel 10:13, 20), they can move themselves wherever they wish in a moment. This is why a single *angel* was able to slay a whole army in one night, and also to strike the firstborn of the *Egyptians* throughout the entire kingdom (Isaiah 37:36, Exodus 12:23, and 2 Samuel 24:16 <note>original: 24. 6.</note>).

## *XIV The knowledge of angels is far more sublime than human knowledge.*

This is for three reasons: 1. ->*I.*<-

***A*** *disease is the corruption of a thing <term>Entity: a distinct thing or being</term> in some part of it, and a tendency <note>original: "disposition"</note> of it toward total destruction (that is, death).*

Therefore, the Body, Mind, and Soul each have their diseases <note>original: "hath its diseases"</note>.

->*II.*<-

*The diseases of the body are various, numbering almost too many to count; and they are often mixed.*

A disease added to another disease is called a symptom <note>original: "symptome"</note> of a disease.

<term>Physics</term>: The study of nature and the physical world.
<term>Disease</term>: Defined here as a state leading toward the death or destruction of a thing.
<term>Body, Mind, and Soul</term>: The three parts of a human being believed to be subject to illness.
<term>Symptom</term>: In this context, a secondary condition that accompanies a primary disease.

<meta>The text continues a discussion on the nature of diseases, transitioning from physical injuries to internal imbalances of the humors. The catchword "VI Cru-" indicates that the next page will begin section six, providing a detailed explanation of "Crudity" (indigestion).</meta>

3. A disease of the body is caused either by a break in tissue <note>original: "solution of that which is continued"; a medical term for a disruption in the physical continuity of skin, muscle, or bone</note> or by an imbalance of the humors <term>humors: the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile—once believed to govern health and temperament</term>.

4. A break in tissue is caused by either a rupture or a wound. A rupture is prevented by being careful of falls and violent motion. A wound is avoided by shunning those things which can cleave, cut, prick, rend, tear, bruise, or hurt in any way; both are to be cured by the surgeon <note>original: "Chirurgion"</note>.

N.B. <note>Latin "nota bene," meaning "note well"</note> 1. The cure of a wound is hopeless if any vital organ <note>original: "member"</note> is hurt, such as the heart, the brain, the liver, the entrails, etc. In such cases, the vital functions are hindered and soon after cease. 2. If any limb <note>original: "member"</note> is completely lost, it cannot be reattached, because the vital spirit <note>the "spirit" was thought to be an invisible substance that carried life and sensation through the body</note> has no way to pass into the part that has been severed.

5. The imbalances <note>original: "distempers"</note> of the humors and the diseases that result from them always proceed from one of these six causes: namely, either from:

->1. Indigestion <term>original: "Crudity"; refers to undigested or "raw" matter in the stomach</term><-
->2. Bloating <term>original: "Inflation"; the swelling of the body with gas or wind</term><-
->3. Flowing Humors <term>original: "Distillation"; the "dropping" or flowing of excess fluids, such as mucus or catarrh</term><-
->4. Blockage <term>original: "Obstruction"</term><-
->5. Decay <term>original: "Putrefaction"</term><-
->6. Inflammation<-
