VI *Crudity in the body is nourishment that has not been sufficiently digested <note>original: "concocted"</note>: specifically either chyle <note>chyle: a milky fluid of digested food found in the small intestine</note> or blood. This arises:* 1 *from the quality of food and drink; when they are consumed too raw, phlegmatic <note>phlegmatic: here meaning producing excess phlegm, or being cold and moist in nature</note>, or unwholesome, so that the digestive power <note>original: "concoctive faculty"</note> cannot properly break them down.* 2 *from the quantity: when more food and drink is consumed than the body is able to transform and absorb <note>original: "assimilate"</note>. Consequently, undigested and unabsorbed humors <note>humors: the bodily fluids once thought to determine health</note> burden the body like intruders that do not belong there.* 3 *from a lack of exercise: when the natural heat is not stimulated or strengthened to vigorously perform its duty in the digestion of food.*

*Various problems <note>original: "inconveniences"</note> result from such crudities.* 1. If the crudity is in the stomach, it causes a loathing of food; for as long as the previous food is not digested, there can be no appetite for more. Furthermore, children may have an appetite to eat earth, chalk, coals, and so on, depending on whether the crudities have taken on the likeness of such matter—for "like desires like." 2. If there is a viscous <note>viscous: thick and sticky</note> crudity sticking to the stomach <note>original: "ventricle"</note> or the intestines, it becomes energized by warmth and turns into worms. ### V I I I *Distillation is the condensation of raw vapors into rheum, which is the cause of many evils.*

When raw <term>vapors</term> <note>original: "crude vapours"; in early modern medicine, these were gases produced by digestion that were thought to rise to the head</note> rise to the head—and because of their great abundance and thickness they cannot be expelled through the usual channels—they become <term>rheum</term> <note>original: "rheume"; a watery discharge or mucus</note> flowing in various directions and causing several diseases.

1. If they flow abundantly and run from the nose, they cause the *cold* or *head-congestion* <note>original: "Murre or Pose"; archaic terms for a common cold or a stuffed nose</note>.
2. If the discharge falls into the throat <note>original: "jawes"</note>, it causes *catarrh*.
3. If it falls into the glands of the throat, it causes *quinsy* <note>an abscess of the throat or tonsils</note>.
4. If it enters the lungs, it causes difficulty of breathing and *asthma*.
5. If the discharge is salty and sharp, ulcerating the lungs, it causes a *cough*.
6. If this happens often and the lungs become filled with <term>abscesses</term> <note>original: "apostemes"</note>, it causes <term>consumption</term> <note>tuberculosis</note>.

For when the ulcerated lungs cannot properly perform their function of cooling the heart, the <term>vital spirit</term> <note>a substance then believed to be produced in the heart to provide life and heat</note> is generated hotter than it should be. This spirit does not nourish the body, but instead feeds upon the flesh and blood, and eventually burns out the very factory of the blood itself, which is the liver; whence...

IX Obstruction is a blockage of the internal organs <note>original: "bowels"; here referring generally to internal organs or the digestive tract</note> by thickened phlegm, which results in them being unable to perform their functions. For example, when the intestines are blocked so that they cannot empty, it is called *volvulus* <note>original: "Volvuli"; a painful twisting of the intestine that causes bowel obstruction</note>, or a "wringing of the guts." When the liver is blocked, it leads to dropsy <term>dropsy: an old term for edema, or the painful swelling of soft tissues due to fluid accumulation</term>. (This happens because the chyle <note>original: "Chylus"; a milky fluid produced during digestion</note> is not turned into blood, but flows through the veins and limbs without being absorbed into the body's tissues.) When the gallbladder is blocked, it causes yellow jaundice; when the spleen is blocked, it causes black jaundice <note>original: "Black Jaundice"; a historical term likely referring to a severe form of jaundice or a condition related to an excess of "black bile"</note>. X *Putrefaction* is the decay of some humor in the body: namely, either of <term>phlegm</term>, choler, or melancholy. When these decay either inside or outside their vessels, they produce fevers or ulcers.

*The cure is:* 
1. Cleansing of the affected area. 
2. A healthy diet. 
3. Physical activity.

XI *Inflammation* is a burning of the <term>vital spirit</term><note>In early medicine, a refined substance in the blood thought to sustain life and heat.</note> or of the blood. It is caused by excessive motion (either of the body through exhaustion, or of the mind through brooding and anger), or else by decay, or by a blockage.

# VI. God produced these qualities intrinsically so that the substance of every body might be formed. For:

| Principle | | Qualities | | Resulting Property |
|-----------|-----------|-----------|-----------|-----------|
| Mercury (☿) | | fluidity, union <note>original: "coition"</note>, rawness <term>crudity: an unrefined or raw state</term>. | | incombustibility. |
| **Sulphur** | **gives to things** | softness, adhesion <note>original: "cleaving together"</note>, oiliness <note>original: "fatness"</note>. | **and from there** | inflammability |
| **Salt** | | consistency, hardness, brittleness <note>original: "aptness to break"</note>. | | incorruptibility. |

*That Mercury gives fluidity and the easy union of matter is shown by quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, which, because Mercury is predominant, is extremely fluid; it refuses to be stopped or fixed <term>fixed: in alchemy, the process of making a volatile substance solid or stable</term>. It is also in its most raw state, so it can neither be ignited nor burned; instead, if you apply fire to it, it escapes into the air.*

*Now, the fact that the binding <note>original: "coagulation"</note> of bodies comes from sulphur, acting as a glue, is evident because there is more oil in dry, solid, and dense bodies than in moist ones. This is also seen because ashes (after the sulphur has been consumed by fire) do not stick together in a lump if you pour water on them; however, they do stick together with oil or fat. Chemists <note>original: "Chymicks"</note> extract oil out of every stone, leaving nothing but ashes, with no part sticking to another any longer.*

<sig>E 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical theory of the Tria Prima (Mercury, Sulphur, and Salt), detailing how each of these three principles imparts specific physical properties—such as fluidity, inflammability, and hardness—to all material substances.</summary>
<keywords>Tria Prima, Mercury, Sulphur, Salt, alchemy, chemistry, quicksilver, physical properties, incombustibility, coagulation</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<page-num>( 54 )</page-num>

That *salt* provides consistency is shown by the bones of living creatures, from which <term>chemists</term> <note>original: "Chymicks"; referring to early modern alchemists or natural philosophers</note> extract pure salt; likewise, all dense things leave behind many ashes (which are salt) after they are burned.

God, therefore, with great wisdom blended these three qualities together in physical bodies. For if *mercury* were absent, matter would not flow together to create things; if *salt* were absent, nothing would hold together or remain stable; and if *sulfur* were absent, the consistency would be forced and yet easily scattered. Finally, if there were no sulfur in wood and other materials, we could have no fire on earth except for solar heat (for nothing could be ignited), and then what great hardships would human life endure?

### *Of the accidental or extrinsic qualities of bodies.*

So much for the substantial qualities; the accidental ones follow.

VII *An accidental quality is either manifest or hidden.* <note>original: "occult"</note>

VIII *A manifest quality is one that can be perceived by the senses, and is therefore called sensible.*

Examples include heat, cold, softness, and roughness.

<meta>This page continues a philosophical or scientific discussion on the properties of matter, moving from general definitions of qualities to a specific categorization of the senses.</meta>

<page-num>(55)</page-num>
<sig>E 4</sig>

**IX** An "occult" <term>hidden or secret</term> quality is one that is known only through experience—that is, by its effects (such as the attraction of iron to a magnet <note>original: "loadstone"</note>, etc.). Therefore, it is called "insensible" <term>beyond the reach of the physical senses</term>.

**N.** Manifest <term>obvious or observable</term> qualities arise from the various temperatures of the elements and from substantial <note>original: "substantificall"</note> qualities; occult qualities arise directly from the unique spirit of each thing.

**X** Sensible qualities <term>qualities perceived by the senses</term> are five-fold, corresponding to the number of the senses: visible, audible, olfactile <term>relating to smell</term>, gustatile <term>relating to taste</term>, and tangible. These are color, sound, odor, flavor <note>original: "savour"</note>, and "tangor" <note>an archaic term coined by the author for the quality of being touchable</note>.

Do not let the unusual word *tangor* offend anyone; it is invented for the sake of teaching, and analogy supports it. For if we derive *calor* (heat) from *caleo* (to be hot); *color* (color) from *colo* (to color); *sapor* (flavor) from *sapio* (to taste); *amor* (love) from *amo* (to love); *fluor* (flow) from *fluo* (to flow); *liquor* (liquid) from *liquo* (to melt); *clangor* (noise) from *clango* (to resound); and *angor* (anguish) from *ango* (to distress)—why not also *tangor* (touch-quality) from *tango* (to touch)?

### Regarding the quality of touch

**XI** The quality of touch (or *tangor*) is the specific arrangement <note>original: "positure"</note> of the parts of matter within a body.

**XII** There are twelve pairings <note>Note: While the author mentions twelve pairings, only seven pairs—totaling fourteen characteristics—are listed here.</note> of these; for every object, in relation to touch, is: 
1. rare <term>thin or porous</term> or dense 
2. moist or dry 
3. soft or hard 
4. flexible or stiff 
5. smooth or rough 
6. light or heavy 
7. hot or cold.

<summary>This page defines the difference between "occult" and "manifest" qualities and categorizes the five senses, specifically defending the creation of the term "tangor" to describe the sense of touch. It begins a detailed list of physical properties that can be perceived through touch, such as density, moisture, and temperature.</summary>

<keywords>occult quality, manifest quality, senses, tangor, tangible quality, loadstone, elements, physics, linguistics</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the properties of matter, likely following a list of physical characteristics or elements.</meta>

Of each of these, we must accurately consider what they are and how they function.

XIII *<term>Rarity</term> is the extension of thinned <note>original: "attenuated"</note> matter through larger spaces; <term>density</term>, on the contrary, is the tighter pressing together of matter into one space.*

For all earth, water, air, and spirit are sometimes more rare and sometimes more dense. We must note that there is no body so dense that it does not nevertheless have pores, even if they are imperceptible <note>original: "insensible"</note>. This is evident in wooden and earthenware vessels, which leak liquids in the form of sweat; it is also seen in a lead bottle filled with water, which, if crushed with hammers or a press, sweats out water like a most delicate dew.

XIV *<term>Humidity</term> (or moisture <note>original: "humour"</note>) is the liquidity of the parts of a body and their tendency to be penetrated by one another; <term>dryness</term> <note>original: "ficcity"</note>, on the contrary, is a firmness <note>original: "consistency"</note> and an impenetrability of the parts of the body.*

So a clod hardened by either heat or cold is dry earth, but mud <note>original: "mire"</note> is moist earth; water is a humid liquid, but ice is dry water, etc.

XV *<term>Softness</term> is a condition <note>original: "constitution"</note> of matter that is somewhat moist and easily yields to the touch:*

<meta>Catchword: hard-</meta>

<summary>This page defines and explains the physical properties of rarity, density, humidity (moisture), dryness, and softness. The author argues that even the densest materials, like lead, possess imperceptible pores that allow for the passage of fluids under pressure.</summary>

<keywords>physics, properties of matter, density, rarity, porosity, humidity, dryness, softness, lead bottle experiment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's definitions of the physical properties of matter, following previous discussions of density and composition.</meta>

*Hardness is a dryness of the matter that does not yield to the touch.*

So a stone is either hard or soft; likewise water, spirit, air, and so on.

XVI *Flexibility is a compaction of the matter with a moist glue <note>The author uses "glue" to refer to the internal binding forces or substances that hold matter together.</note>, so that it allows itself to be bent. Stiffness is a coagulation of the matter with dry glue, so that it will not bend but break.*

Thus, iron is stiff and steel is flexible. Likewise, some wood is flexible and other wood is stiff; but note that the flexible is also called tough, and the stiff is called brittle.

XVII *Smoothness is that which, through the equality <note>original: "aquality"</note> of its parts, pleasantly affects the sense of touch. Roughness is that which, through the inequality of its parts, distracts and draws apart the sense of touch.*

Note that in liquid things, the smooth is called mild and the rough is called tart. For example, unpolished marble is rough, but polished marble is smooth. Water is rough, while oil is mild. A violent <note>original: "vehement"</note> and cold wind is rough and sharp, while a warm air is mild. Similarly, in our bodies, humors, vapors, and spirits are said to be mild or sharp.

XVIII *Lightness is the rushing upward of a body because of its rarity <note>term: rarity—low density or thinness of matter</note> and spirituosity <note>term: spirituosity—the quality of being composed of fine, active "spirits" or gases</note>. Heaviness is the downward tendency of a dense body. The former appears in flame and every exhalation <note>term: exhalation—vapors or gases rising from the earth or water</note>; the latter appears in water and earth.*

->N.W.<-

<margin>N. W. 1</margin> How this motion is made upwards and downwards by a "love of fellowship" <note>This refers to the archaic concept that things of the same nature are naturally attracted to one another.</note> (or the attraction of things of the same nature) has been discussed in Chapter 3.

2. The inequality of heaviness or weight <note>original: ponderosity</note> comes from the unequal density <note>original: condensation</note> of the matter. For observe, the more matter there is in a body, the heavier it is: for instance, a stone is heavier than wood, metals are heavier than stones, and among these, gold, mercury <note>original: quicksilver</note>, and lead are the heaviest of all, because they are the most compact bodies.

3. Among all heavy things, gold is found to have the greatest weight; alcohol <note>original: spirit of wine, or sublimated wine</note> has the least. The proportion of weight between these two is found not to exceed a ratio of 21 parts; so that one drop of gold is no heavier than twenty-one drops of alcohol.

### XII *The principal <term>transmutation</term> <note>The process of changing one substance into another; in this context, the conversion of food into bodily fluids.</note> of the nourishment is by the production of the four vital humors: blood, phlegm, yellow choler, and black.*

The fattest part of it is turned into **blood**; a part into **spittle** or **phlegm**; a part into **yellow choler** (or bile); and a part into **black choler** (or melancholy). 

**Melancholy, by its thickness, represents the earth; phlegm represents water; blood represents air; and choler represents fire.** 

But they differ in color and in flavor; for melancholy is black and bitter; phlegm is white and without taste; blood is red and sweet; and choler is yellow and bitter. Now it is to be noted that among these four, blood is most abundantly generated, because it contains the very substance of the nourishment: to which

Now, among all things that are known to us, *fire is the most hot*; we have nothing that is *most cold* except ice; which, nonetheless, is far from being as cold as fire is hot in its respective degree.

*Calefactivum* <note>original: Latin for "heat-inducing" or a substance that produces heat</note> is that which may stir up heat, such as motion and whatever may produce motion—namely fire, pepper, and all sharp and bitter things taken into the body. For motion comes from fire, and fire comes from motion, and heat comes from both. For just as fire must be in motion (otherwise it immediately goes out), so motion inevitably catches fire, as is seen by striking a flint or rubbing wood together for a long time. Therefore, both are heat-inducing, but fire is further said to be actually hot, while heat-inducing things are commonly called *hot in potentia* <note>original: Latin for "potentially hot," referring to things that are not hot to the touch but produce heat through chemical or mechanical action</note>.

*Calefactile* <note>original: Latin for "heat-receptive" or capable of being warmed</note> is that which may easily be heated, such as air, and after air, fatty things (oil, butter), then wood, and then water. In these substances, because the parts are somewhat rare <term>meaning thin or less dense</term>, they are more easily moved to agitation <note>The author attributes heat to the "agitation" or movement of internal particles.</note>. Stones and metals, because they have their matter compacted, do not easily admit heat; however, they retain it longer after it is admitted because the heat cannot easily exhale <term>meaning escape or dissipate</term> due to their narrow pores. And this is the

### *XVI Every <term>concoction</term> <note>From the Latin "concoctio," meaning a "cooking" or digestion. It was the process of refining food into blood using internal heat.</note> leaves behind it unprofitable dregs; which are called excrements and dross.*

Thus we see it come to pass in the **decoction** <note>The process of boiling a substance to extract its essence, common in both herbal medicine and metallurgy.</note> of metals. Now we must note that plants make little or no excrement, because they are nourished with a simple and uniform juice which goes entirely into their nature; or, if anything remains, it sweats forth in gum. But living creatures—because they consist of very dissimilar parts—have need of a compound **nutriment** <note>Nourishment or food.</note> that is solid and soft, dry and moist, hot and cold, etc. In this way, the more solid parts may have nourishment as well; whereby through **assimilation** <note>The biological process where nutrients are converted into the actual substance of the body's organs and tissues.</note>, every part draws that which will profit itself, and the rest must of necessity be strained out. Another reason is because plants are sustained with a little spirit, and that which does not evaporate; but living creatures

The perpetual effect of heat is **attenuation** <note>original: "attenuatiō"; the process of making a substance thinner, less dense, or more fluid</note>: but it acts in different ways depending on whether the matter is:

<note>The following text is arranged in the original as a branching logic diagram with large brackets.</note>

*   **Liquid** <note>Written vertically within a bracket in the original.</note>
    *   **Sulfurous** <note>original: "Sulphury"</note>, which it kindles, turns to flame, and snatches upward.
    *   **Mercurial**, which it rarefies and stretches forth, as may be seen in the evaporation of water; also in the desiccation <note>drying out</note> of earth, wood, etc., in which all the humor <note>original: "humour"; referring to the internal moisture or fluid</note> that is present turns and evaporates into air.
*   **Consistent** <note>In this context, meaning solid or firm matter.</note>, which if it has parts that are:
    *   **Glutinous** <note>original: "Glutiness"; sticky or oily</note> or **Sulfurous**, it forces them to melt, as may be seen in suet, wax, and metals.
    *   **Ashy** or **salty**, it forces them to be condensed by the drying up of the moisture; and also to break if you apply more force, such as wood, a clod of earth, a tile, etc. (And so hardening is an effect of heat by accident <note>In Early Modern science, an "effect by accident" meant a secondary or indirect result rather than the primary purpose or direct action of the force.</note>.)

## Of tastes.

It appears, therefore, where the flavors of herbs, fruits, parts of living creatures, and minerals come from: namely, from *salt* and *sulfur* <note>In early modern chemistry/alchemy, Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury were known as the *tria prima*, the three primary elements thought to compose all material things.</note>, which are diffused everywhere. Every creature absorbs more or less of these according to its nature. *Mercury* is by itself without taste (as we observe in phlegm <note>original: "flegme," referring to one of the four bodily humors, thought to be cold and moist.</note>), but the others are saturated by it, as well as by the various degrees of cold or heat, so that they are more or less sweet, bitter, salty, and so on.

### *Of smells.*

XXI *The olfactory quality is called odor, which is an extremely thin vapor emanating from the taste.* <note>The author suggests that smell is essentially a diluted, airborne version of a substance's flavor.</note>

Yet sulfurous things produce more smell than salty things; and hot things more than others, because heat thins <note>original: "attenuates"</note> and spreads the substance into the air. Hence, gardens and ointment boxes are so much the sweeter the hotter the air is. Yet, the sweeter they are, the sooner they lose their smell, as that fragrant <note>original: "odiferous"</note> sulfurous quality is exhaled little by little.

### *Of sounds.*

XXII *The audible quality is called sound, which is a splitting of the air when sharply struck, flowing in every direction.*

Not every motion of the air produces a sound, but only that motion by which the air is

suddenly divided and parted. Now a sound is either high-pitched <note>original: "acute"</note> or low-pitched <note>original: "obtuse"</note>; pleasing or displeasing; depending on whether the body that strikes the air is sharp or blunt, smooth or rough.

The natural kinds of sound are: *tinkling*, when the air blows through some sharp thing; the *murmur of running water*; the *rattling of thunder*; the *rustling of leaves*; the *bellowing or lowing of oxen*; the *roaring of lions*; the *hissing of serpents*; and the voices of other living creatures.

### Of colors.

XXIII *The visible quality is called color, which is light received in different ways on the surfaces <note>original: "superficies"</note> of bodies, and tempered with its opposite, darkness—such as whiteness, blackness, greenness, etc.*

Observation 1. That color is nothing in itself, but is actually light reflected in various ways from objects, is apparent for two reasons: 1. Because just as it cannot be seen without light, it is not found to be anything by any other sense or by reason; 2. Because colors, as well as light, spread themselves through the air and are present in the eyes of all beholders. Since we saw before that this spreading <note>original: "diffusive"</note> motion was a property of light, color is therefore in-

<summary>This page categorizes various natural sounds and introduces a scientific theory of color, arguing that color is not an inherent property of objects but rather a manifestation of reflected light.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of light and optics, following the previous page's mention of glass.</meta>

( 66 )

Indeed, color is nothing but light colored in different ways by the different surfaces <note>original: "superficies"</note> of things. This is because light, being reflected in several manners within the same matter, produces several colors.

We see this, for example, in a cloud, which in itself is consistent, yet it appears to us sometimes whitish, sometimes blackish, sometimes reddish <note>original: "ruddy"</note>, according to how it is positioned against the light. In like manner, we see in the Rainbow (which is nothing else but the breaking up <note>original: "resolution"</note> of a cloud into very small drops of water) yellow, green, flame-red, and sky-blue. This also appears in crystal dust turned towards the light, which shows plainly that color is nothing else but a different tinting of light resulting from the different way it hits a surface <note>original: "incidencie"</note>. 

But there (in the Rainbow and glass), the colors pass through because the matter itself is fluid and transparent. In solid <note>original: "fixed"</note> bodies, colors are also fixed, but in a way known to God rather than to us.

Observation 2. That from the capacity to receive colors, a body is called *Pellucid* <note>from the Latin *pellucidus*, meaning translucent or clear</note> or *Opaque* <note>original: "Opacous"</note>. *Pellucid* (*Transparent* and *Diaphanous*) is that which gives light a passage through it, and is therefore neither colored nor seen, such as air and, in part, water.

<summary>The author explains that color is an effect of light reflecting off surfaces at different angles, using clouds, rainbows, and crystals as evidence. The text also defines the difference between transparent (pellucid) and opaque bodies based on how they interact with light.</summary>

glass, crystal, a diamond, etc. (That air is not colored, but only tinted <note>original: "tinct"</note> with light, is evident in a room that is tightly shut on every side: if you let in a beam of the sun through a hole, the light will pass through the entire room and yet will appear nowhere except on the opposite wall or floor—unless you place your hand or some other solid object in its path, or unless dust is stirred up so that its particles <note>original: "atomes"</note> fly in such quantity as to reflect the light.) *Opaque* <note>original: "Opacous"</note> describes that which does not allow light to pass through but reflects it, and therefore it is colored and visible, such as earth, wood, stone, gems, and colored water. This *light reflected from an opaque body is properly called color.* Of color, there are six kinds: *white, yellow, green, red, sky-blue, and black.*

*White* is light reflected in its own true form.

*Yellow* is light tinted with a little darkness.

*Green* is light in a middle state, a most pleasing balance <note>original: "temperature"</note> of light and darkness.

*Red* is light leaning more toward darkness.

*Sky-blue* <note>original: "skie-colour"</note> is light that is more than half dark.

*Black*, finally, is the lack of reflection <note>original: "non-repercussion"</note>

## CHAPTER V.
### *Concerning the changes of things: generation, corruption, etc.*

**F**rom the opposition of qualities—especially cold and heat, for these two qualities are the most active—arise those changes <note>original: "mutations"</note> to which all things in the world are subject, as we shall now see.

1. *Change is an accidental property <term>accident: in Aristotelian philosophy, a property that does not change the essential nature of a thing, though here the author notes it affects the "essence."</term> of a body, by which its essence is altered.*

Specifically, this occurs whether a thing passes from non-being to being, from being to non-being, or from being one way to being another.

<summary>This page concludes a section on how the universal spirit creates diverse physical forms through hidden qualities and begins a new chapter on "mutation," the process of change driven by the conflict between heat and cold.</summary>
<keywords>spirit of the world, occult qualities, sympathy and antipathy, mutation, generation, corruption, heat and cold, Aristotelian physics</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the fundamental principles of physical bodies; this page continues by explaining how those bodies change and decay over time.</meta>

### II *All bodies are subject to change.*
The reason for this is that they are all composed of matter, spirit, and fire, which are perpetually mixed together in various ways. Because matter is a fluid and slippery thing, and the spirit is restless—constantly agitating itself—the heat generated everywhere by light and motion eats into, tears, and pulls apart the substance of things. Because of this, I say, nothing can remain in the same state for long. Everything grows, increases, decreases, and perishes. Thus the Scriptures also affirm that the heavens grow old like a garment (*Psalm 103:26* <note>original: Psalm 103:27; in modern Bibles, the passage "they shall wax old like a garment" is found in Psalm 102:26</note>).

### III *The change of a thing is either essential or accidental.*

### IV *Essential change occurs when a thing begins to exist or ceases to exist: the first is called <term>generation</term>, and the other <term>corruption</term>.*
For example, when snow is formed from water, it is said to be generated; when it is dissolved back into water, it is said to be corrupted <note>original: "corruption"; in this context, it refers to the breaking down or decomposition of a specific form rather than moral rot</note>.

### V *An accidental change in a thing occurs when it increases or decreases, or when its qualities change: the first is called <term>augmentation</term>, the next <term>diminution</term>, and the last <term>alteration</term> (each of which we will now examine individually to see how they occur).*

## *On the Generation of Things*

VI *Generation is the production of a thing, so that what did not exist begins to be.*

Thus every year—indeed, every day—countless things are generated throughout all of nature.

VII *For generation to occur, three things are required: Seed, a Matrix, and Moderate Heat.*

These three things are necessary for the generation of living creatures, plants, metals, stones, and finally <term>meteors</term> <note>In the early modern period, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and lightning, as well as falling stars.</note>, as will be seen in their respective sections.

VIII *Seed is a small portion of matter that contains the spirit of life within it.*

For seed is physical and visible, and therefore <term>composed of matter</term> <note>original: "materiate"</note>. It is not truly seed unless it contains the spirit of the <term>species</term> <note>Here, "species" refers to the specific form or type of creature the seed is meant to become.</note> it belongs to. For by what else would it be formed? Therefore, seeds from which the spirit has evaporated are useless for generation.

IX *The Matrix is a suitable place to deposit the seed so that it may exercise its power.* <note>original: "vertue"; in this context, it refers to an inherent natural power or biological potency.</note>

Nothing exists without a place, nor is anything generated without a suitable location, because otherwise the actions of nature are hindered. A place is suitable for generation when it provides the seed with: 1. A soft environment. 2. <term>Enclosure</term> <note>original: "circumclusion"</note>, lest the spirit should evaporate.

<summary>This page defines the process of "generation" (the creation of new things) and identifies its three essential requirements: seed, a matrix (womb or environment), and moderate heat.</summary>
<keywords>generation, seed, matrix, moderate heat, spirit of life, biology, natural philosophy, meteors</keywords>

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of sensory perception from page 179, specifically concluding the definition of "tangors" (objects of touch) and moving into the mechanics of how the senses operate through physical organs and mediums.</meta>

For nothing is seen, touched, etc. of itself, but by <term>accidents</term> <note>In historical philosophy, "accidents" are the external qualities of a thing—like its color, smell, or texture—rather than its essential "substance."</note> wherewith it is clothed. And if we would be accurate Philosophers, <note>The text uses "N. W.", which is likely a variation of "Nota Well" or "Note Well," equivalent to the modern N.B.</note> of the three principles of things, only light or fire is perceptible. For matter and spirit are of themselves insensible: *the light then tempered with darkness, makes the matter visible. Motion (which is from light) makes a sound; but heat (which is from motion) stirs up and balances the rest of the qualities: odors, savors, and <term>tangors</term> <note>The qualities or objects perceived through the sense of touch.</note>.*

### XXXVII The organs of the senses are parts of the body, in which the <term>animal spirit</term> <note>Thought to be a refined fluid or "spirit" that traveled through the nerves to carry sensory information to the brain.</note> receives the objects that present themselves; namely, the eye, the ear, the nostrils, the tongue, and all that is <term>nervy</term> <note>The "nervy" parts refer to the nervous system and tissues connected to nerves.</note>.

Nothing in all nature acts without organs: therefore the animal spirit cannot do it neither.

### XXXVIII The <term>medium</term> <note>The substance or element that carries information from an object to our sense organs.</note> of conjoining them, is that which brings the object into the organ: in light, the light; in hearing, the air moved with breaking <note>This refers to the vibration or "breaking" of air that produces sound.</note>; in smells, the air vaporing; in taste, the water melting; in touch, the quality itself inherent in the matter.

### XXXIX The manner of sensation is the contact of the Organ with the object, <term>passion</term> <note>In the philosophical language of the time, "passion" meant being "passive"—the state of the sensory organ being acted upon by an external object.</note>, and action.

(whether it be from the sun, or from fire, or the internal heat of a living creature, it does not matter, as long as it is temperate), there can be no <term>generation</term> <note>original: "generation"; the process of coming into being or being produced</note> because matter cannot be prepared, softened, or expanded without heat.

### *On the Growth of Things*

XI. Everything that is generated increases and <term>augments</term> <note>original: "augmenteth"; grows or increases in size</note> itself as much as possible; it does this by the attraction of matter and the <term>assimilation</term> <note>original: "assimilation"; the process of absorbing and incorporating new matter into a body</note> of that matter into itself.

For wherever there is generation, there is heat; and where there is heat, there is fire; and where there is fire, there is a need for and attraction of fuel. Because heat always <term>thins</term> <note>original: "attenuateth"; to make thin or less dense</note> the parts of the matter which evaporate, it seeks and attracts other matter to sustain itself (as we see in a burning candle). When a portion of matter is attracted and applied to a body, it gradually takes on that body's form, becomes like it, and is made the same. Through the power of heat, <term>dissimilar</term> <note>original: "heterogeneous"</note> things become <term>similar</term> <note>original: "homogeneous"</note>. In the meantime, the spirit of that body also attracts to itself something of the <term>spirit of the universe</term> <note>original: "spirit of the universe"; likely referring to the "Anima Mundi" or a vital universal energy believed to animate all matter</note>, thereby multiplying itself as well. This is how stones, minerals, plants, living creatures, and so on, grow.

<summary>This page explains that heat is essential for the creation and growth of all things, as it allows a body to attract and assimilate external matter and universal spirit into its own form.</summary>
<keywords>generation, growth, augmentation, assimilation, heat, fuel, heterogeneous, homogeneous, spirit of the universe, natural philosophy</keywords>

### *Of diminution*

XII *Whatever has increased, at some time or another ceases to increase and begins to decrease; this happens because the matter dries out <term>arefaction</term>.*

Namely, because the heat that increased along with the body gradually consumes its fluid and fatty parts and dries up the solid parts. At last, these parts are not able to incorporate <term>assimilation</term> <note>original: "assimulation"; the biological process of absorbing new matter into a system</note> the matter flowing in, because they lack a binding substance <term>gluten</term> <note>original: "gluten"; here meaning a natural glue or moisture that holds the body's structure together</note>. Therefore, the body fades and withers, and at length perishes.

### *Of the alteration of things*

XIII *No body always retains the same qualities, but changes them in various ways.*

For example, wood is thin and soft when it grows; afterward, it is condensed and hardened, especially once it is dried. Fruit, on the contrary, becomes less dense <note>original: "rare"</note> and soft as it ripens, changing its color, flavor, and smell. For it is the law of the universe to be subject to constant change <term>vicissitudes</term>, as well as to decay <term>corruption</term>, which follows here.

### *Of the corruption of things*

XIV *Every body is liable to decay <term>corruption</term>.*

Because it is composed of a decaying mat-

And for this reason, perhaps every material thing is called ***CORPUS*** <note>Latin: "body"</note>, as if it were *corrupus*, because it is subject to corruption.

XV ***All corruption occurs either by arefaction or putrefaction.***

For we are not speaking here of violent corruption, which is caused by the breaking apart of a solid object (as when anything is broken, torn, bruised, burnt, etc.), but of natural corruption, which brings destruction to things from within. It is manifest that this can happen in no other way except by <term>arefaction</term> <note>original: "arefaction"; the process of drying out</note> or <term>putrefaction</term> <note>original: "putrefaction"; the process of decay or rotting</note>.

XVI ***Arefaction is when the inflow of matter is denied to a body, and the internal heat—having consumed its own moisture—dries and hardens the remaining parts and eventually leaves them.***

In this way, herbs, trees, and living creatures wither.

XVII ***Putrefaction is when the spirit is released from a body, and the components of the matter are dissolved, returning to their diverse and separate parts.*** <note>original: "heterogeneous parts"</note>

For then the watery parts are gathered together (therefore decayed things give an evil scent); the oily parts gather to themselves (which is why decayed things always have some <term>unctuosity</term> <note>oiliness or greasiness</note>); the dregs gather to themselves (causing that confusion in decayed things and unpleasant taste, etc.). From this, it is easy to find the reason *why cold, salt, and drying hinder putrefaction* <note>decay</note>. Namely, because cold stops the pores of a body so that the <term>spiritual parts</term> <note>original: "spirituall parts"; in early science, these were the volatile vapors or essences of a substance</note> cannot go out and exhale. Dried things, however, are emptied of those thin parts which might decay. Salt, last of all, binds the parts of the matter within and, as it were, holds them with bands so that they cannot escape and release the spirit.

Again, it may easily be gathered from this *why hard and oily things are durable.* Specifically, it is because hard things have much salt, which hinders decay, but they lack <term>humidity</term> <note>moisture</note>, which is the provocation of decay. Oily things are durable because they do not easily let go of their spirit, due to their nourishing nature and gentle way of containing it. (Suet <note>hard animal fat</note> and fat decay because they have loose pores and some <term>aquosity</term> <note>wateriness</note>.)

### XVIII *From what has been said, it may be concluded that the world is potentially eternal.*

Since not a single crumb <note>original: "crum"</note> of matter can perish, nor the spirit be stifled, nor the light be extinguished, and none of these can escape from the world, they must necessarily exist together. As they pass through and act upon one another, it is inevitable that one thing should be born from another without end. For that old *axiom* of the *philosophers* is most true: *the decay of one is the birth of another* <note>original: "the corruption of one is the generation of another," a reference to the Aristotelian principle *corruptio unius est generatio alterius*</note>. In this way, the *Architect* <note>a title for God common in natural philosophy</note> of the world expresses his own eternity.

## CHAPTER VI.

### Of the Elements: Sky, Air, Water, Earth.

**W**e have so far contemplated the general parts of the world, namely, the principles and their common <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "accidents"; in 17th-century philosophy, "accidents" are properties or characteristics that are not part of a thing's essential nature, such as its color or location.</note>. Now follow the types of things which are derived from these principles by various degrees. Here, the elements must first be considered; having been formed from the first gathering together of the principles, they are, as it were, the bases and hinges of the entire order of the world.

**I.** *An element is the first and greatest body in the world, having a simple nature.*

A body, or a substance—for although we also called matter, spirit, and light "substances" (in Chapter 2) because they are not <term>accidents</term>, yet because none of them exists by itself or apart from others, but rather they join together to make up other substances, the elements and the creatures that follow may more rightly be called substances. Now, an element is said to be of a simple nature in respect to the sub-

<meta>This page continues the numbered explanation of the physics of sound and hearing from page 186, then introduces the next sense, sight.</meta>

Now it is clear from this: 1. *Why sounds that are too high-pitched or too low offend the ear, while moderate ones please us?* It is because they harmonize better with our **spirit** <note>The "animal spirit," a subtle fluid thought to carry sensory information to the brain.</note>.

2. *Why does sound also travel indirectly?* Because when the air is moved, it moves the air next to it in all directions through the motion of **diffusion** <note>The outward spreading of waves.</note>.

3. *Why does a sound spreading in all directions gradually fade away?* It is just like when the water of a pool is moved by a stone falling into it. However, while the water vibrates for a long time in that whole circle, the circle of the air <note>OCR reads "rain," but the context of "air" vs "water" indicates "air" is intended here.</note> moved by sound passes away all at once; for the sound of a bell does not remain in the air, but is spun around within the ringing bell itself.

4. *Why is it that when one person hears, everyone hears the same thing?* Because a sound is a physical **commotion** <note>A disturbance or vibration.</note> of the air, which reaches the ears of all those who are within that circle.

5. *What is an Echo?* It is a sound reflected from hollow places, in the same way that ripples of water in a container, after hitting the sides, return again toward the center.

### XLVIII. *Sight has the eye as its* **Organ**; *which is nothing more than a living...*

nature of heaven, etc., and he will acknowledge the vanity of this Aristotelian fiction <note>original: "Aristotelicall figment"</note>.

IV *The sky is the purest part of the matter of the world, spread over the highest spaces of the world.*

It is commonly called the visible and starry heaven, and by an error of the Greeks (who, thinking that it was of a solid substance like crystal, called it <term>stereoma</term> <note>original: στερέωμα; a solid base or firmament</note>) the Firmament; but this is little in agreement with the truth. More appropriately, it is called <term>ouranos</term> <note>original: οὐρανὸς; the Greek word for heaven</note> from the Hebrew <term>or</term> and <term>ur</term> <note>original: אור & אור; light and fire/heat</note>, that is, light and fire, as if it were <term>pyr ano</term> <note>original: πῦρ ἄνω</note> (that is, fire above), and so named from its *burning*; just as <term>aither</term> <note>original: αἰθὴρ; ether</note> is derived from <term>aitho</term> <note>original: αἴθω</note>, meaning to burn. For it burns with an unquenchable light of the stars, by which it is also purified. The etymology <note>original: "notation"</note> of the Hebrew word supports this: <insert><term>shamayim</term> <note>original: שמים; the Hebrew word for heaven</note></insert> from <insert><term>esh</term> <note>original: אש; fire</note></insert> and <insert><term>mayim</term> <note>original: מים; water</note></insert>, meaning fire and water. The nature of the heaven is to *be liquid in the highest degree, volatile, and hot.*

V *Air is a part of matter that is moderately pure, spread over the lower spaces of the world.*

The nature of it is to *be breathable and penetrable in every direction.*

VI *Water is a denser part of the matter of the world, reduced into fluidity.*

The nature of it is to be *fluid and moistening.*

VII *The earth is the most coarse <note>original: "grosse"; meaning dense, thick, or heavy</note> part of matter, acting as the dregs and sediment gathered together at the bottom.*
The nature of it is to be dry and *immovable*.

VIII *The elements, therefore, are all one single matter of the world, distinguished only by their degrees of density and rarity.*

For where the light is spun around, there the matter is most refined and pure; below that it is coarser; then coarse and fluid; and finally, at the bottom, there are dregs and a thick sediment. Therefore, this is a mere gradation. For earth is nothing else but thickened and hardened water; water is nothing but thickened air; air is refined <note>original: "subtilized"; meaning made thin or less dense</note> water; and water is liquefied earth.

But from this difference of density and rarity, there arises another difference among these same elements: namely, *in regard to motion and rest, heat and cold*. Water is movable (for it flows); air is even more so (for it spreads <note>original: "transfuseth"</note> itself here and there); the sky <note>The author uses "skie" and "heaven" to refer to the celestial regions beyond the atmosphere.</note> does nothing but whirl around most swiftly and perpetually. Also, the heaven, by reason of its perpetual motion, is perpetually hot (even burning); the earth, by reason of its perpetual rest, is perpetually cold—except where it is warmed by the fire of heaven coming upon it, or enclosed within it.

<meta>The text continues from the previous page, detailing the properties of the four elements—earth, water, air, and fire—and how they interact through heat and cold.</meta>

### IX *The elements are transmutable into one another.*

That is because the heat raised in the matter may expand and condense it. In water and air, we see that happen daily. For who does not know that water evaporates and is turned into air? Rain teaches us that water is formed again from vapor. But we may also achieve the same transformation manually, or in common <term>alembics</term> <note>original: "vulgar Alembicks," referring to a distilling apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</note> in which waters or wines are distilled. 

Suppose there is an <term>alembic</term> empty of all matter, filled only with air. To the long pipe that hangs out from it, apply a narrow-mouthed glass and seal the pipe’s mouth carefully so that no air can escape. You will see that when the air cannot expand <note>original: "dilate it selfe"</note> locally, it will be condensed <note>original: "coagulated"</note> into water in the furthest and coldest corner of the apparatus (that is, in the glass). You will see that glass "sweat" and distill drops, into which the air—heated and thinned <note>original: "rarified"</note> in the <term>alembic</term>—contracted itself. But remove the fire, and you will see those drops vanish little by little and return into air.

### X *Aristotle thought that the Elements were in a tenfold proportion to one another;*

<page-num>83</page-num>
<sig>G 2</sig>

...but later men have found them to be nearly a hundredfold. 

That is, that from one drop of earth, ten drops of water are made by rarefaction <term>rarefaction: the process by which a substance becomes less dense, such as a liquid turning into a gas or vapor</term>; and from one drop of water, ten drops of air are made. The truth of this latter claim is easy to demonstrate in the following way: 

Let someone take a bladder (from an ox or a hog) and, after cleaning it, coat it with oil to seal the pores so that the air cannot escape. To the neck of this bladder—after first squeezing out all the internal air—let them tie the neck of a small glass vial containing about one-hundredth of the volume of water that the bladder is capable of holding. Let this apparatus be placed in the hot sun or in a very hot stove. When the water is turned into air <note>original: "air"; the author refers to what we would now call water vapor or steam</note> by the heat, it will be seen that the bladder becomes full. However, if you bring that same air-filled bladder into the cold, you will see it collapse as the vapor turns back into water. 

*Note.* The same hundredfold proportion, or something very close to it, is also observed in colors. One drop of ink or red dye will color a hundred drops of water, but the reverse is not true. This is because blackness represents the earth in its density, while whiteness represents the heavens in their rarity <term>rarity: in this context, the state of being thin, porous, or less dense</term>. But this very proportion varies, because the air is in...

## XI *The matter of all the elements, just as it is made up of atoms, is also turned back into atoms; it does so more subtly the more subtle its mass is.*

For example, the earth and every dry and hard thing is reduced to a dust that is almost indivisible <note>original: "brought into a dust almost indivisible"</note>. This dust may be sifted through a sieve, but it cannot penetrate <term>penetrate</term><note>In this context, the author refers to the ability of a substance to pass through the pores or solid walls of a container.</note> solid materials. Water, however, can both be strained and can penetrate. For example, it can pass through vessels of clay <note>original: "earth"</note> and wood—even lead—as we have shown in the example in chapter 4, aphorism 13. Air and fire also penetrate through thicker bodies, just as heat passes through the walls of furnaces.

## XII *The elements are the four greatest bodies of the world, from which all others are created.*

Scientific analysis <term>resolution</term><note>original: "resolution"; the process of breaking a complex substance down into its basic constituent parts.</note> shows that the smaller bodies of the world—which are infinite in number and variety—are actually composed of the elements. For when these bodies decay <term>corrupted</term><note>original: "corrupted"; in early modern science, "corruption" refers to the natural decomposition or transformation of a substance.</note>, they return to their original elements. Our senses also teach us this: all things possess some solidity <note>original: "grossenesse"</note> from the earth, some liquid from the water, some airy quality <note>original: "spirituosity"</note> from the air, and some heat from the heavens. And because

All living things are nourished by these; they are therefore called *Elementa quasi Alimenta* <note>Latin: "Elements, as if nourishment"</note>, as if you should say "nourishment," as in the Bohemian <note>Czech</note> words *ziwel* or *ziwent*.

### XIII. Elementary matter occupies a place in the world according to its degree of density and rarity.

For the earth rests at the bottom; the water swims upon it; the air floats above the water; and lastly, the sky <note>In this context, "sky" refers to the fourth element, often identified as fire or ether</note> is in the highest place. You shall see a similar spectacle if you pour clay, water, wine (especially distilled <note>original: "sublimated"</note>), and oil into a glass; for every one of these will occupy a place according to its nature.

### XIV. Therefore, the Elements form the four visible regions or spheres of the world.

For the earth is a globe which the water naturally encompasses; the air encompasses the water, and the sky encompasses the air. This is in the same manner as in an egg, where the yolk is surrounded by the white, and that by the skin and shell.

### XV. Of the Elements, there are two extremes—the sky and the earth—and two intermediates <note>original: "mean"</note>—air and water.

They are called "extreme" and "intermediate" both in regard to their locations <note>original: "sites"</note> and their properties <note>original: "accidents," a philosophical term referring to the characteristics or qualities of a substance</note>.

XVI *But because the elements were prepared not merely as an idle spectacle, but for strong operation upon one another, the Creator somewhat changed that order and commanded two sorts of water and two sorts of fire to be made.*

<meta>This page continues a theological argument regarding the nature of the "waters above the firmament" described in the Book of Genesis, distinguishing them from ordinary rainwater or clouds.</meta>

...are signified by the name of the water in heaven; and therefore [they are signified] here also. But I answer:

1. That the *waters in heaven* are one thing, and the *waters above heaven* another. *Rain* might be called *water in heaven* because the air was called the "first heaven" by the *Hebrews*; but it cannot be called the *waters above heaven*, such as these of which *Moses* speaks.

2. That the waters of the clouds are not actual liquid water <note>original: "waters in act"</note>, but vapors; but *Moses* speaks of waters. For he says expressly that in the first seven days there *was no rain* (Genesis 2:5 <note>original: "cap. 2. ver. 5."</note>); but he says that those waters above the *Expanse* <note>original: "Expan-sum"; refers to the firmament or "Raqia" mentioned in Genesis</note> were immediately made on the second day; therefore, they are something other than rainwater.

3. He says that the waters were separated from the waters, but the waters of the clouds are not separated from the waters of the sea and of rivers. For they are perpetually mingled: vapors ascending and rain descending.

4. He says that the *Expanse* was in the middle <note>original: "middest"</note> between the waters and the waters; but how can that be said of the clouds, which are below the *Expanse* and do not reach even a thousandth part of its height <note>original: "altitude"</note>?

Lastly, *Psalm 148* places the waters above the heaven next of all to the Heaven...

...of the Heavens, verse 4, but he reckons up clouds and rain afterward among the creatures of the earth in verse 8. What need do we have for any other interpretation?

Reason most strongly persuades us of the same thing. For when we establish the principles of the world in the same order that *Moses* did, it was necessary that matter—being scattered by the light rolling around—should fly here and there and coagulate <note>original: "coagulate it selfe"; meaning to thicken or solidify into a mass</note> at the boundaries of the world on both sides. Thus, in the middle where the light moved (and still moves), there would be a pure sky; but on both sides, above and below, the matter, hardening itself, would grow thick. We see this happening here below; why should it not happen above as well, especially since God himself suggests it? Let it be so, because naturally it cannot be otherwise.

But that fire is contained within the earth is shown by:
1. The eruptions <note>original: "eructations"</note> of fire in *Etna*, *Vesuvius*, *Hecla*, etc.
2. The hot water springs found everywhere.
3. The formation <note>original: "progeneration"</note> of metals, even in cold countries, and other things which can originate from nothing else but fire, which shall be examined in the sections that follow.
4. Lastly, there is evidence found in the book of *Job*, chapter 28, verse 5.

**XVIII** *The waters above the heavens are placed there for purposes known to God, but the use of fire underground is well enough known to us also.*

Yet we may say something of these waters by conjecture. Specifically, it was fitting that there should be visible boundaries <note>original: "termes"</note> of the visible world, and that the heat of the universe's ever-revolving frame needed cooling from the other side as well—and similar reasons. But regarding the fire underground: by it, mountains, valleys, and caves of the earth are produced; also stones, metals, and <term>juices</term> <note>original: "juyces"; in early modern science, these were mineral-rich fluids or "succus" thought to solidify into minerals and metals.</note> are generated, along with many other things we shall see in what follows. <meta>This page continues a discussion on the properties of the heavens, specifically how the sun's heat affects the celestial medium.</meta>

For by the motion and heat of the Sun, which is always present, it is perpetually thinned <note>original: attenuated</note> to an extreme delicacy <note>original: subtlety</note>.

XXI *The entire sky is moved around because that burning and ever-flying light of the stars hurries the sky along with it.*

This is shown, 1. by reason: for if the stars were moved within an immovable heaven (in the same way that birds are carried through the air and fish through the water), that piercing <note>original: penetration</note> of the heaven would not happen without force. Neither could it be performed with such great speed <note>original: celerity</note> nor with such an even <note>original: æquable</note> course, because of the resistance. Therefore, the stars are carried through heaven in all respects like clouds in the air—that is, along with their vehicle <note>original: charriot; referring here to the medium that carries them</note>. 2. by the senses: for we see that our earthly fire carries away with it the matter which it has caught and thinned—namely, vapors, smokes, and flames. Why should the heavenly fire not do the same? Comets also show this to the eye, about which we shall see more in Chapter 8. 3. The same conclusion is to be gathered from the words of *Moses*, if accurately considered (*Genesis* 1:14 & 17).

<meta>The previous page concludes a discussion on the heavens or the atmosphere. This page continues by defining the nature of air and water within the natural hierarchy.</meta>

In Scripture, air is referred to by the name of the "first heaven." Yet it penetrates water and earth to fill up their cavities because there is no *vacuum* <note>The belief that a "vacuum" or empty space could not exist in nature was a central tenet of Aristotelian physics, often summarized as "nature abhors a vacuum."</note>.

XXIII *Air occupies a middle position between the heavens and the water, both in its location and its characteristics.*

However, it is denser where it joins the earth and water, and thinner toward the heavens. Therefore, on the highest tops of some mountains, neither humans can live nor trees grow because of the thinness of the air. Because of this thinness, the air is insufficient for the breathing of living creatures or for the growth of plants.

XXIV *Near the earth, the air is hot in the summer (due to the intense reflection <term>repercussion</term> of the sun's vertical rays). In winter, it cannot be heated because of the slant <term>obliquity</term> and indirect reflection of the rays. Higher up, the air is always cold, especially in summer when it is hemmed in <note>original: "pend in"</note> on both sides by the heat of the heavens and the heat of the earth.*

### Of the Water

XXV *Water is condensed <note>original: "thickned"</note> air. It washes and moistens the earth and is the home of fish.*

XXVI *By its own nature, water is only moist and fluid; it is neutral <note>original: "indifferent"</note> toward all other qualities.*

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of the physical properties of the elements, specifically focusing on the fluidity, density, and thermal qualities of water.</meta>

Observation 1. The fluidity of water is such that if you give it even the slightest slope <note>original: "declivity"</note>, it flows. However, its liquid consistency <note>original: "humidity"</note> is unequal according to its degree of thinness <note>original: "rarity"</note> and density. For a ship does not sink as deep in the sea as it does in a river, because seawater is thicker and "drier" <note>In the science of the time, "dryness" often referred to the presence of salt or minerals that added density to the liquid.</note>.

Observation 2. People commonly add that water is naturally cold, using a two-part argument: 1. because it cools things, and 2. because it extinguishes fire. But I answer that it cools not by its own "coolness," but by its raw nature <term>original: "crudity"; a state of being unrefined or lacking the "digestive" heat of life or fire</term>. It quenches fire in the same way that hot water and wine do—even though they are hot—not because they are the opposite of fire, but because fire is nourished by the thinner parts of the wood. If an abundance of water is cast on (or any fluid thing, even oil), the "pores" <note>The author suggests that fire needs to access the small openings in fuel to burn.</note> are blocked and the fire is extinguished. Otherwise, there are fires made of bitumen <note>a naturally occurring petroleum or tar-like substance</note> (which is not a porous material) that burn even in the water, which we also see happening with lime <note>Quicklime reacts chemically with water to produce intense heat.</note>. Lastly, great fires are sometimes "nourished" with water. We also see that there is sometimes hot and sometimes cold water, not only in rivers but also breaking out of springs <note>original: "fountains"</note>, depending on how it is influenced. Yet, it may not be hidden <note>original: "dissembled"</note> in the meantime that air is more...

<summary>This page explores the physical properties of water, arguing that its density changes based on salt content and that its ability to extinguish fire comes from smothering the fuel rather than its inherent coldness.</summary>
<keywords>fluidity, density, seawater, buoyancy, properties of fire, bitumen, lime, physics of water</keywords>

XXVII *The water at first covered the earth all around; but (on the third day of the creation) it was gathered into certain channels (which are called seas, lakes, pools, rivers, etc.).*

Moses testifies in these words that this was done at the command of God: "Let the waters be gathered together into one place, that the dry land may appear" (*Genesis 1:9*). But David (relating the process of the creation) also describes the manner in which it happened in *Psalm 104:6–9*: *That thunders were raised, by which the mountains ascended and the valleys descended, while the waters were carried steeply down into their channels; and in this way, a boundary was set for them, so that they might not return to cover the earth.*

From this, it is very likely that the uncovering <note>original: "discovery"</note> of the surface of the earth was caused by an earthquake. This earthquake was produced by fire buried deep within the earth; as it battled the cold gathered <note>original: "conglobated," meaning collected into a mass or ball</note> there, it shook the earth and either caused it to swell in various places or tore it apart. This created the rises and falls in the earth's surface (that is, mountains and valleys). But

The sea is lower than the earth, for rivers run down into it, not up away from it.

XXX *The sea is one body in itself; because it flows into the continents here and there, as if with strong arms, it has acquired several names in different places.*

That great sea surrounding the earth is called the Ocean; those arms dividing the continents are called bays or gulfs. For all those gulfs are joined to the Ocean, except the *Caspian* or *Hyrcanian Sea* in *Asia*. Yet even that is thought to have channels within the earth through which it is joined to the Ocean.

XXXI *The sea is of unequal depth, commonly from a hundred to a thousand paces <note>A "pace" usually refers to a Roman pace, roughly five feet; thus, a thousand paces is approximately one mile.</note>; yet in some places, they say that the bottom cannot be found. It is probable that the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the earth covered by water is as uneven as the part of the earth we live on. Namely, in some places there are very wide plains, in other places valleys and depths, and in other places mountains and hills. If these mountains stand above the water, they are called islands; but if they are hidden under the water, they are called <term>shelves</term> <note>In a nautical context, "shelves" refers to dangerous shallow areas, such as sandbanks or reefs.</note>.

## Of the Earth

XXXIII *The earth is the densest body in the world, serving as the dregs and sediment of all matter.*

Consequently, it is coarse, opaque, cold, and heavy.

XXXIV *It hangs in the middle of the universe, surrounded by air on every side.*

Since it is surrounded on all sides by the heavens and is pressed by their heat from every direction, it has nowhere else to go or rest except in the equilibrium <note>original: "aquilibrium"; a state of physical balance</note> of the universe.

XXXV *The earth is round in every direction.*

It still retains the form it first received from the light of heaven revolving around it—except that in some sides it is raised into mountains and hills by the thunder which was sent into its depths <note>original: "bowels"</note> on the third day <note>A reference to the Biblical account of Creation</note>. In other places, it is pressed down into valleys and plains to allow for the flow of rivers; however, this does not significantly hinder its overall spherical shape <note>original: "globosity"</note>.

XXXVI *The greater part of the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the earth is still covered with water; the*

The lesser part stands out of the water, where it is called *dry land* or a *continent*; or, if it is a *small portion*, an *island*.

There are seven continents of the earth: *Europe*, *Asia*, *Africa*, *Peruvian America* <note>original: America Peruviana; refers to South America</note>, *Mexican America* <note>original: America Mexicana; refers to North and Central America</note>, *Magellanica* (or *Terra Australis* <note>original: Terra Australis; "The Southern Land," a hypothetical continent often depicted on early maps before the discovery of Antarctica</note>), and *Terra Borealis* <note>original: Terra Borealis; "The Northern Land"</note>; but there are islands beyond number.

XXXVII. *The earth’s outward face is flat in some places and mountainous in others; but inside, it is solid in some places and hollow in others.*

This is visible in mountains and metal mines, where one can see stones or clay very tightly packed in some areas, while in others there are dens, deep caves, and endless passages. These must be thought to have been the work of the thunder sent into the earth on the third day of the creation (which, penetrating and piercing its bowels, tore them apart). Now, there are not only spacious caves and holes in the earth, but an infinite number of narrower <term>veins</term> <note>In this context, small channels or cracks rather than just ore deposits</note> and pores, as is plain enough by experience.

XXXVIII. *The cavities of the earth are full of water, air, and fire.*

Because there are caverns, passages, and pores, they must necessarily be filled;

## Chapter VII.
### *Of Vapours.*

**If** the Light of Heaven had achieved nothing else upon matter except melting it together into the forms of the Elements—as it was variously thinned <note>original: "rarified"</note> or thickened <note>original: "densified"</note>—the world would have remained empty of other living creatures. But it does not stop passing through the Elements themselves to scorch

...scorch them, and by scorching them to thin them out <note>original: "attenuate"</note>, and by thinning them, to break them down <note>original: "resolve"</note> into *vapors*: from which, once condensed again, many different kinds of things are produced. Now, then, the nature of vapors shall be laid open in the following aphorisms <term>Aphorisms: short, pithy statements of truth or principle</term>.

*I Vapour is an element thinned out <note>original: "rarified"</note>, mixed with another element.*

For example: the vapor of water—what is it but water thinned out and scattered in the air? Smoke—what is it but an exhalation <note>original: "exhalation": a release of gas or vapor</note> of wood or other matter broken down?

*II Vapour is generated from the denser <note>original: "groffer"</note> elements—earth, water, and air—as well as from all mixed bodies.*

In the case of water, the proof is evident. For being set to the fire, it evaporates visibly; set in the sun, it evaporates noticeably, because even whole pools, rivers, and lakes are dried up little by little by the heat of the sun. That the earth exhales, you may know by your senses if you put a clump <note>original: "clot"</note> of soil into a dish (of earthenware or pewter) and pour water onto it repeatedly, and let it evaporate with heat until there is nothing left—neither the water nor the clay. For what has become of the clump? It has surely turned

The vapor of air is invisible; yet it appears that there is some. 

1. In a living body, where all acknowledge that there are evaporations through the skin and the hair. For then, what are the vapors that go out but the vapors of the inward vapors—far more subtle than the vapors of water? 

2. Fruits, herbs, spices, etc., when dried—even very dry—spread an odor from them. Now, what is an odor but an exhalation? <note>original: "exhalation" here refers to a substance given off as a vapor</note> But in this case, it is not a watery exhalation (since there is not anything watery left in them); therefore, it is an airy one.

That mixed bodies <term>mixed bodies: substances composed of a combination of the four classical elements—earth, air, fire, and water</term> do vaporize is without doubt, inasmuch as the elements of which they consist do vaporize. This includes not only soft bodies (sulfur, salt, herbs, flesh, etc.) but even the very hardest. For how could a thunderbolt be generated in the clouds if stony vapors did not ascend into the cloud? <note>Early modern meteorology often held that "stony" or "sulfurous" vapors rose from the earth to create lightning and "thunderstones."</note> And it is certain that stones exposed to the air for many years (as in high towers) grow porous. How could this happen, but by evaporation? And what is the melting of metals but a kind of vaporization? III *Heat is the efficient cause <note>In traditional philosophy, the "efficient cause" is the primary agent or force that brings something into being.</note> of vapor*; which, wherever it spreads, thins <note>original: attenuating</note> the matter of bodies and turns it into vapor.

For this is the constant power <note>original: virtue</note> of heat: to rarify, thin, and spread.

IV *The whole world is full of vapor.*

For heat, the creator of vapors, is never absent; so that the world is nothing else but a great <term>vaporary</term> <note>original: Vaporarie; a room or vessel where steam is produced, similar to a sweat-bath or hothouse.</note>, or stove. For the earth always nourishes an infinite store of vapors in its bowels; the sea boils daily with internal vapors, and the air is stuffed full of them everywhere. We shall see hereafter that the sky is not altogether free from them. Furthermore, the living bodies of animals and plants are nothing but workshops of vapors, acting as a kind of <term>alembic</term> <note>original: Alembecks; a chemical apparatus used in distillation to cool and condense vapors.</note> perpetually steaming as long as they have life or heat.

V *Vapors are generated for the production of other things.*

### VI *Vapors are the substance of all bodies.*

For who does not know that waters and oils are collected from the vapors of <term>alembics</term>? <note>An alembic is an early chemical apparatus used for distillation, consisting of two vessels connected by a tube.</note> Who does not also see that smoke in a chimney turns into soot, which is black dust? Indeed, that soot gathers on the walls of chimneys and turns into a stony hardness. In the same manner, then, it will appear clearer than the light at noon-day that clouds, rain, hail, stones, and herbs are made of the condensed vapors of the elements. Even living creatures themselves—and within them the blood, flesh, bones, and hair—are nothing but solidified vapors.

### VII *Vapors, then, are coagulated—some into liquid matter (such as water, saliva, flesh, or pulp) and some into solid <note>original: "consistent"</note> matter (such as stones, bones, wood, etc.).*

VIII *The motion of vapors in our world is upward because, among the denser <note>original: "thicker"</note> elements, they take on the nature of thinner ones.*

For certainly the vapor of water is thinner than water itself—indeed, thinner than the very air. Although air consists of smaller parts, they are more tightly packed together. Therefore, vapor does not allow itself to be compressed <note>original: "prest"</note> by either water or air, but instead frees itself, continually rising. It is for this reason that plants grow upward: because the vapor contained <note>original: "included"</note> within them expands and moves toward the sky.

IX *One vapor is moist, another dry; one thin, another thick; one mild, another sharp, and so on.*

For those qualities that later appear in solid bodies are present initially in their basic components <term>rudiments</term>—that is, in vapors—as we know from experience. For example, dry smoke causes pain to the eyes, while a moist <note>original: "humid"</note> vapor does not; this demonstrates the quality of "sharpness." Likewise, do not smells (which are nothing but the exhalations of things) suffi-

X *Vapors gathered together, and not coagulated <term>coagulated: solidified or thickened into a mass</term>, cause wind in the air, disturbances in the sea, and earthquakes in the earth.*

### Of Winds

XI *Wind is a flow <term>flux: a continuous moving or flowing</term> of the air, ordained in nature for most profitable ends.*

For winds are: 1. the brooms <note>original: "besomes"</note> of the world, cleansing the elements and keeping them from rotting; 2. the fan of the spirit of life, causing it to grow <note>original: "vegetate"</note> in plants and all living things; 3. the chariots of clouds, rain, smells, and even of heat and cold, wherever they need to be conveyed; 4. lastly, they provide strong motions for human use (such as grinding and sailing).

XII *The ordinary cause of wind is an abundance <note>original: "store"</note> of exhalations in one place, forcing the air to flow elsewhere.*

We may produce <note>original: "raise"</note> a kind of wind with our own hands in four ways: namely, by forcing or compressing, and by thinning <term>rarifying: making less dense, such as heating air so it expands</term> and thickening <term>densifying: making more dense</term> the air (which shall be shown by examples...

### XIII *Winds in some countries are predictable, coming at a specific time of the year and from a specific direction; others are free, coming from any location.*

Note that they call these <term>Etesian</term> <note>original Greek: ἐτησίας, meaning "annual"</note> winds. These are caused either by the mountainous nature of the nearby region, where the snows melt at that time, or certainly by other causes that produce an abundance of vapors there. You should note, however, that these Etesian winds are mostly weak and gentle, and they give way to the "free" <note>unpredictable</note> winds.

Note 2: There is also another kind of set wind common to the entire world: a perpetual flow of the atmosphere from east to west. There are several proofs that such a wind exists:
1. Those who sail near the <term>equator</term> testify to it.
2. In the seas of Europe, when a specific wind stops, it is said that a gentle breeze is still felt from the east.
3. Consequently, mariners are constantly of the opinion that navigation from east to west is performed most quickly.

...us in a clear and still sky, the highest clouds are mostly seen to be carried from east to west. Therefore, we need not doubt this general wind, if anyone chooses to call it a wind. For it does not come from exhalations <term>vapors or gases thought to rise from the earth to form weather</term>, but from the heavens. By its revolving motion, the heavens carry the air around perpetually—moving swiftly above, but here near the earth (where the clouds are) almost unnoticeably. However, under the equator (as it is a larger circle), this movement is very significant. From this, a scientific question <note>original: "Probleme"</note> may be profitably noted: why does the *east wind dry, but the west wind moisten?* Namely, because the east wind, being carried along with the air, thins it out <term>original: "attenuates"</term> more; but the west wind, pushing against the air, condenses it.
