It is clear that various winds may arise in various places at the same time, depending on where the material for exhalations is available and what causes them to turn in one direction or another. ->*Of the Sea Tide*<-

XV *The sea tide is the daily flux of the sea toward the shore, and its reflux back again.*

The sea has its fluxes <term>flux: the flowing in of the tide</term>, which are less inconsistent than those of the air, for it flows only toward the shores and back again the same way. Twice a day it flows up, and twice it ebbs again. The purpose of this, without doubt, is to keep the waters of the sea from putrefying <note>original: "putrefying"; meaning to rot or decay, which was a common concern regarding stagnant water</note> through that continual motion. But the efficient cause <term>efficient cause: the primary force or agent that produces an effect</term> of the tide—previously considered among the secrets of nature—is now being sought through the truest grounds of natural philosophy <note>natural philosophy: the study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science</note> and more accurate observations.

*...within, through which the sea, as it swells, spreads itself out, and as it falls, settles down again.*

For this tide is like boiling water seething over a fire; it is nothing more than the stirring of the vapors raised within the water by the force of heat. For it is impossible for water not to be transformed <note>original: "resolved"</note> into vapors by heat, and it is impossible for those vapors not to seek an upward passage toward their natural elements <note>original: "connaturals," referring to the Aristotelian idea that elements like air and steam naturally seek to rise toward the heavens</note>. 

Yet, it is also impossible for them to have an easy passage out of the water. This is because the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the water—indeed, its whole mass—is a flowing liquid like molten glass and has fewer pores than earth, wood, or stone. Therefore, the water must inevitably swell, rise up, and dash itself against the sides of the kettle. At length, it breaks into a thousand openings to allow the dancing and evaporating heat a way out, driven by the vapors that are raised and multiplied within, striving to move upward. We see all of this happen in a boiling pot. 

In the same manner, the sea swells because of the vapor that multiplies at the bottom of its depths <note>original: "gulfs"</note>. It lifts itself up into a swelling <note>original: "tumor"</note> and, of necessity, spreads itself toward the sides. It does not contradict this theory that the water...

...of the sea boiling is not as hot as the water of a boiling pot. For in this case, the vast quantity does not allow for such great heat over such deep gulfs. XVII *Vapors within the sea are chiefly generated by the fire underground.*

Commonly, people attribute this to celestial <note>original: cælestiall</note> fire, the Sun, and the Moon. But that is likely to be as true as saying we see a pot of water boil when set in the sun, no matter how hot it is. For who has ever seen that? The Sun may lick the surface of the water and so consume it little by little, turning it into vapor; but nothing can make it boil at the bottom except for fire placed underneath it. Therefore, the cause of the vapors within the sea must of necessity be placed underneath—namely, that underground fire which the whole nature of inferior things <note>This refers to earthly or material substances in the sublunary sphere, as opposed to celestial or "superior" things.</note> demonstrates is shut up there.

XVIII *The vapors and tides of the sea are provoked by the heat of heaven (the Sun).*

A laboring man, or a traveler, sweats easily enough from his internal heat (stirred up)

This harmony is also seen in how water is given to us from the clouds and springs <note>original: "fountains"</note>. For in rainy weather, springs flow more abundantly; in dry weather, they dry up somewhat. God indicated both of these facts in Genesis 7:11 and Deuteronomy 28:23. Now, the cause is "the harmony of fire to fire"—of the celestial to the subterranean, and so on—as will appear elsewhere.

### XIX The Sea Flows Twice a Day, According to the Sun's Path

For when the Sun ascends to the meridian <term>meridian</term> <note>the highest point reached by the sun at noon</note>, it attracts the vapors of the sea and causes the waters to be elevated and spread out. As the Sun descends toward the West, it allows them to fall again. Now, the reason the waters swell again at sunset and fall as the Sun hastens toward the East is the same as that seen in boiling pots. In a boiling pot, the hot water is seen to boil and be lifted up, not only in that part which is toward...

So the sea is a cauldron, which the sun (the world’s fire) surrounding, makes to swell up on both the opposite parts, but to fall in the intermediate parts; so that this sea-tide following the sun, goes circularly after a perpetual law.

**XX The flux and reflux <note>original: "fluxe and refluxe," referring to the rising and falling (high and low) of the tides</note> of the sea is varied according to the motion of the Sun and Moon, and the geography of places.**

1. In winter it is almost imperceptible, the Sun but weakly raising the subterranean <note>original: "subterrane"</note> vapors.
2. Also, as the Moon increases, the flowings are somewhat retarded; as it decreases, they are anticipated <note>meaning they occur earlier than expected</note>: which gave occasion to the ancients to think that it was caused by the Moon alone.
3. ...places sooner, in others later. But enough of the sea tide; the earthquake follows.

XXI *An earthquake is the shaking of the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the earth in any country. It arises from underground vapors <note>original: "subterrane exhalations"</note> gathered together in great abundance and seeking a way out.*

Therefore, it does not cease until those vapors are either scattered through the cavities of the earth or else break forth.

XXII *Earthquakes are sometimes so horrible that they overturn <note>original: "subvert"</note> cities, mountains, and islands with a hideous bellowing, howling, and crashing.*

These terrifying <note>original: "formidable"</note> effects lead us to suspect that those vapors are then mixed, much like the ones that cause thunder in a cloud. This occurs not simply by the blast of the vapors, but by their burning, so that they are a kind of underground lightning. Still, I thought it best to mention them here together.

# CHAP. VIII.
## Of solidified substances <note>original: "concrete substances"</note>: namely, Stars, Meteors, and Minerals.

I **A** *solidified thing <note>original: "concrete thing"</note> is a solidified vapor <note>original: "vapour coagulated"</note> endowed with a particular form.*

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the causes of earthquakes, attributing them to the movement and combustion of underground vapors, before beginning a new chapter on "concrete substances" like stars and minerals.</summary>
<keywords>earthquake, geology, subterranean vapors, natural philosophy, concrete substances, minerals, meteorology</keywords>

--- Page 273 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "concretes" or solidified substances, transitioning from a list of examples to the physical causes and categories of their formation.</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>

For example: soot, clouds, snow, and so on. Note that these names, *concrete* <term>In this context, a "concrete" refers to a composite substance formed by the gathering of parts into a mass</term> and *concreture*, are new. Yet, they are suitable to express this level of created things, which contributes nothing but coagulation and shape <note>original: "figure"</note>.

### II
The primary cause of the solidification <note>original: "concretion"</note> of vapors is cold, which, wherever it finds a vapor, condenses and coagulates it. This is visible in alembics <term>A vessel used in distillation, consisting of a gourd-shaped container and a cooling cap</term>, where the vapor raised by heat is carried into the highest region of the vessel where it is cold; there, it resolves itself back into water. To achieve this, distillers occasionally wash the uppermost cap of the alembic with cold water and make the pipes, through which the solidified liquid distills, pass through a vessel of water. However, heat also helps the solidification of things by consuming the thinner part of the substance and forcing the rest to harden, as we see happening in the formation <note>original: "generation"</note> of metals.

### III
Some solidified substances are *ethereal*, others *aerial*, others *watery*, and others *earthly*. This is because some are formed in the sky, such as stars; others in the air, such as clouds; others in water, such as a bubble; and others in the earth, such as stones. Each of these will be considered separately.

<meta>catchword: IV</meta>

IV *Ethereal composite bodies <note>original: "concretes"; in 17th-century natural philosophy, a "concrete" refers to a physical body formed by the union of matter and form.</note> are stars and comets.*

V *Stars are fiery globes, full of light and heat, with which the sky glitters on every side.*

Both the beauty of the world required this—that hanging lamps should not be missing in such a lofty palace—as did the necessity of the inferior world <note>The "inferior world" refers to the Earth, which in this cosmological model sits below the celestial spheres.</note>, concerning which is the following aphorism. Now we classify stars in the rank of composite bodies because it is certain that they are made of matter and light.

*Stars were produced in such great numbers out of a very great necessity.*

Namely: 
1. To heat the Earth with varying temperatures. 
2. To create the various harmonies of time <note>This refers to the cycles of days, months, and seasons determined by celestial movement.</note>. 
3. To instill various forms into living creatures. 
For such great variety could not be introduced into the lower world without such variety in celestial things.

VII *God placed the greatest number of stars in the highest heaven all around, so that they might shine upon <note>original: "irradiate"</note> the Earth on every side and carry their sphere about with a rapid motion of heat.*

Regarding this starry sphere, consider these following aphorisms:

1. That the motion of this sphere is completed in the span of twenty-four hours.

VIII. *A very great portion of most intense light is gathered into a ball in the sun, so that it may seem to be the only fountain of light and heat.*

If it were not for the *sun*, we would have perpetual night despite all the rest of the stars; for even at high noon, we are immediately in darkness if the *sun* is merely covered.

Now, concerning the *sun*, the following axioms are to be noted:

->**Axioms**<-

1. It was made large enough to suffice both to illuminate the whole world and to heat and <term>vaporate</term><note>To cause evaporation or provide the heat necessary for the water cycle.</note> the whole earth; it is 160 times larger than the earth.

4. And so that it may serve all sides of the earth with its light and heat (namely, by turns), that retardation <note>original: "retardation"; here referring to the sun's gradual daily "lag" behind the rotation of the fixed stars</note> is not made simply through the middle of the world under the *Equator*, but under the *Zodiac*, bending toward the North on one side and toward the South on the other. From this comes the division of the year into four parts (*Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter*) and the inequality of days for those who inhabit areas outside the equinoctial <term>the equator</term>. For when the sun declines toward the North, it creates summer for those in that region and the longest days, and so on the contrary. And the more vertical the sun is to any part of the earth, the more it heats it, by reason of the direct incidence <term>the angle at which light hits a surface</term> and the repercussion <note>original: "repercussion"; the reflection or bouncing back of the sun's rays from the earth's surface</note> of the rays.

X *The planets, therefore, are the Sun's assistants <term>coadjutors</term> in governing the world; they differ in position <note>original: "site"</note>, path <note>original: "course"</note>, size <note>original: "magnitude"</note>, and light.*

XI *Three of the planets—Saturn* (♄), *Jupiter* (♃), and *Mars* (♂)—*are above the Sun. Venus* (♀), *Mercury* (☿), and the *Moon* (☽) *are below it. They are arranged in a most orderly <note>original: "decent"</note> manner, as if surrounding the sides of their King.*

It is probable that the stars are carried higher or lower in the heavens <note>original: "heavē"</note> for the same reason as clouds in the air or wood in water: that is, according to their different degrees of density or thinness <note>original: "rarity"</note>. For just as heavy <note>original: "thick"</note> wood swims under the water with either all or half of its body submerged, while light wood swims on the surface; and just as watery clouds do not ascend far from the earth, while dry and barren clouds go very high; so it is with the globes of the stars...

Jupiter completes this in twelve years; Mars in almost two; the Sun (as was previously said) in a year. Venus circles <note>original: encompasseth</note> the Sun in five hundred eighty-three days; Mercury in one hundred and fifteen days. The Moon, because she is the slowest of all, remaining behind every...

every day 13 degrees, measuring the *Zodiac* in 27 1/3 days.

XIV *The higher planets observe the Sun in such a way that as they approach near to it, they move into their highest position; as they move away from the Sun, they sink lower toward the Earth.*

For this reason, both their size <note>original: "magnitude"</note> and their movement vary in our eyes. When they are nearer to the Earth, they seem larger; but when they are more distant, they seem smaller. Furthermore, the higher they are, the slower they move, and in this state they are called *direct*; the lower they descend, the faster they move, so that they seem either *stationary* (keeping pace for some weeks with the same fixed stars) or else *retrograde*, sometimes appearing to move backward <note>original: "outstripping them in their course," referring to the apparent backward motion of a planet against the backdrop of stars</note> past them.

XV *Venus and Mercury do not move away from the Sun, except to the sides in either direction: Venus by 47 degrees, and Mercury by 23 degrees. Thus, sometimes they go before the Sun, sometimes they follow it, and sometimes they lie hidden under its rays.*

Note: *Venus*, when she is the morning star and precedes the Sun, is called the "Light-bringer" <note>original Greek: φωσφόρος (*phosphoros*)</note> or *Lucifer*; when she is the evening star, she is called *Hesperus*.

XVI *As for their light, Mars is very fiery*

...fiery and heat-producing <note>original: "calefactive"</note>. <term>Saturn</term> is pale and very cold-producing <note>original: "frigifactive"</note>. <term>Jupiter</term> and <term>Venus</term> possess a benign light. <term>Mercury</term> sparkles changeably, and the <term>Moon</term> shines only with a borrowed light—of which more will be said shortly.

**Note.** The stars and planets do not sparkle because of their great distance (for if they did, <term>Saturn</term> would sparkle more than *Mercury*, whereas we find the opposite to be true), but rather because of their flaming nature. Because fire or light cannot remain still, the polar stars twinkle the most because they are moved the least by the common motion <note>The apparent daily rotation of the heavens.</note>.

XVII. *Because the Moon is near to the Earth and situated in a dense <note>original: "grosse"</note> atmosphere, she moves most slowly; her body is also dense and dark, like a spherical <note>original: "globous"</note> cloud.*

For it is not distant from the Earth by more than 60 semidiameters <note>The radius of the Earth; a standard unit of astronomical distance in this period.</note> of the Earth.

Because of her opacity, the Moon does not shine by her own power, or does so only very weakly. Instead, on the side that is illuminated by the Sun, she shines like a looking glass, while the other half remains dark.

**Note.** *Because the Moon was intended to rule the night, a weak and borrowed light was given to her. Because she was appointed to show shorter periods of time (months), her motion...*

To make this more evident, she was placed below the Sun so that she might appear to us with her enlightened face in various ways. For when she travels with the Sun in the same sign of the Zodiac, she does not appear to us because her enlightened face is turned toward the Sun, while her dark face is turned toward us. But when she is opposite the Sun, we—beholding her from the same side that looks toward the Sun—see her entire luminous face. Lastly, in the intermediate positions, we see her increasing or decreasing in light, according to whether she turns her enlightened face toward us or away from us, because of the variety of her position in relation to the Sun and us.

### XIX 

>*When the Moon, at the change <note>original: "at the change"; referring to the New Moon phase</note>, comes directly under the Sun, she obscures him from our view; when at the full, she is directly opposite to the Sun, she enters into the shadow of the earth and is herself obscured. This is what they call the Eclipses of the Luminaries <note>Luminaries: A traditional astronomical term for the Sun and Moon, the two brightest objects in the sky.</note>.*

From this, it appears that the Sun is not obscured in the same way as the Moon...

<summary>This page explains the phases of the moon based on its position relative to the Sun and Earth, and introduces the astronomical causes of solar and lunar eclipses.</summary>
<keywords>Moon phases, Zodiac, New Moon, Full Moon, Solar Eclipse, Lunar Eclipse, Luminaries, Astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which discussed the nature of celestial bodies and light.</meta>

The Moon is truly darkened—that is, deprived of light—because it has fallen into the shadow. But the Sun is not deprived of light; it is only covered from our view so that it cannot enlighten the Earth with its rays at that time. Therefore, the Earth is more truly eclipsed than the Sun.

God ordained eclipses for three reasons: 
1. That we might understand that all our light comes from the Sun. 
2. So that the size of the celestial bodies <note>original: "Luminaries"</note> and of the Earth might be determined. 
3. To find the true longitude of countries—though that is a matter for astronomers, and this last point for geographers.

->*Concerning Comets*<-

XX *Comets are additional stars which sometimes shine and then fade away; they usually appear with tails or bushy appearances like hair.*

We classify them among the heavens and stars, not with the air and meteors, because they are not created in sublunary <term>sublunary: the region between the Earth and the Moon, traditionally thought to be the only place where change and decay occurred</term> places (as Aristotle thought), but in the highest heavens, even above the Sun. This is shown by: 
1. Their motion, which is always swifter than the Moon itself. 
2. Their parallax <term>parallax: the difference in the apparent position of an object when viewed from two different points; a smaller parallax indicates a greater distance from Earth</term>, which is less than the Moon's, and sometimes non-existent.

XXI *Comets are not burning vapors, but a reflection of the Sun's light on vapors elevated to a great height.*

<summary>This page explains the mechanics of eclipses, arguing that a solar eclipse is actually an eclipse of the Earth, and discusses the nature of comets as celestial rather than atmospheric phenomena.</summary>
<keywords>Eclipses, Sun, Moon, Earth, Longitude, Comets, Aristotle, Parallax, Astronomy, Sublunary</keywords>

--- Page 285 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the nature and composition of comets, specifically refuting the idea that they are made of self-consuming fire.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>

The first point is easily proved. If a comet were a vapor set on fire, it could not last for even half an hour. (For nothing can be set on fire except for sulfurous material, which is consumed in a moment—as is seen with gunpowder, lightning, a chasm <note>original: "Chasme"; a term used in early meteorology to describe an atmospheric light phenomenon that looked like a gap or opening in the sky</note>, a falling star, etc.) However, historical records state that comets have lasted as long as three years. 

The second point is demonstrated by two facts: 
1. Comets cast a tail away from the Sun, just as the Moon casts a shadow (for those dry vapors are not an opaque <note>original: "opacous"</note> body like the Moon, but are semi-transparent <note>original: "semidiaphanous"</note>). 
2. They are eclipsed by the shadow of the Earth, just as the Moon is (as *Campanella* <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639), an Italian philosopher and astronomer</note> testifies). This would not happen if they burned with their own internal fire.

*N. W.* As for the reports of sulfurous material or a stone falling from a burning comet—if true, it should be considered that these were produced by other fiery meteors, and not from a comet itself.

### XXII
*The purposes <term>original: "ends"; here referring to the scientific conclusions or final causes</term> of comets are to demonstrate: 
1. That the entire heavens move, not just the stars. 
2. That the heavens are liquid and permeable <note>original: "transmeable"</note>, not hard like crystal. 
3. That vapors ascend to such great heights, and that change <note>original: "mutations"</note> occurs everywhere in this visible world.*

Vapors, I say, whether they exhale from

<summary>This page argues that comets are not self-combusting vapors because they last too long and are affected by shadows, suggesting instead that the heavens are fluid and subject to constant change.</summary>
<keywords>comet composition, sulfurous matter, Tommaso Campanella, celestial motion, liquid heavens, atmospheric vapors</keywords>

### *Of Aerial Concretes, that is, Meteors.*

XXIII *By reason of the perpetual flux <note>original: "confluxe"; a flowing together or continuous moving</note> of exhalations in the air from all the elements, many things are daily formed <note>original: "concreted"; grown together or solidified</note> there, but they are of short duration.*

For the air is full of exhalations, even when it seems clear. For it cannot be so pure here near the earth, but it will always have something watery, oily, or salty mixed with it. Things formed <note>original: "concrete"</note> from these were anciently called Meteors because they are made high up: for <term>meteōros</term> <note>original: μετέωρος</note> signifies "high" or "lofty."

XXIV *From humid exhalations, watery meteors are made; from dry ones, fiery meteors.*

XXV *Watery meteors are mists, clouds, rain, hail, snow, dew, and frost.*

We must examine each one of these individually to see how they are made.

XXVI *A mist is a watery exhalation that is partially formed <note>original: "half concrete"</note>; because of its density, it cannot lift itself up, and so it creeps along the ground.*

XXVII *A cloud is a gathering together of*

<meta>catchword: thin</meta>

<summary>This page defines "meteors" as atmospheric phenomena formed from earthly exhalations and begins a systematic classification of watery weather events like mists and clouds.</summary>
<keywords>meteorology, exhalations, atmospheric science, mists, clouds, natural philosophy, etymology</keywords>

thin vapors, lifted upward into the highest parts of the air.

They gather mostly over the sea and stagnant waters because that is where most <term>exhalations</term> <note>original: "exhalations"; refers to vapors or gases rising from the earth or water, thought to be the material cause of weather phenomena</note> are produced. From there, they are driven through various parts of the world by the winds and increased by more <term>exhalations</term> arising elsewhere. Therefore, in every region, rain most often comes from the direction nearest the sea—just as it comes from the West for us.

*XXVIII. Rain is the <term>resolution</term> <note>original: "resolution"; the process of a substance breaking down or changing state, here from cloud to water</note> of a cloud into water and its falling in drops.*

Note 1: That <term>resolution</term> is always caused by the <term>condensation</term> of the <term>vapor</term>, but the primary cause of its <term>condensation</term> is not always the same. Sometimes cold condenses a <term>vapor</term>, as we see in the head and pipe of an <term>alembic</term> <note>an early chemical apparatus used for distillation, consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</note> (which must be kept cool). Other times, the condensation is caused by compression itself, or <term>conspissation</term> <note>original: "conspissation"; the thickening or densifying of a substance</note>, as is evident on the ceilings of bathhouses and the lid of a boiling pot. Neither of these causes is lacking when it comes to creating rain. Since the <term>middle region</term> <note>In early modern meteorology, the atmosphere was divided into three layers; the "middle region" was thought to be perpetually cold and the place where clouds and rain formed</note> of the air is cold, and the cloud is squeezed by the vapors constantly rising from below, it must necessarily be dissolved into rain. It is very likely that these are suddenly generated within the cloud from vapours of the same nature gathered together, by virtue of a living spirit mixed with them—just as in the beginning, at the command of God, the waters brought forth creeping things and fish in a moment.

### XXIX *Hail is congealed rain.*

When the sun's beams in the greatest heat of summer have driven all cold away from the earth and into the middle region of the air, it happens that this intense cold violently hardens the drops of rain passing through it, forcing them to turn into ice. Therefore, hail cannot be produced <note>original: "procreated"</note> in winter, as the cold remains near the earth then, rather than high up in the atmosphere.

**Note 1:** It falls only in winter because the vapors are not raised by the sun's weak rays as far as the middle region (which is the cold region). Therefore, here near the earth, the dissolution <note>original: resolution</note> occurs in a milder cold, and furthermore, the freezing <note>original: congelation</note> is very gentle. 

**Note 2:** The whiteness of the snow comes from the joining together of the parts of the water; the same thing happens in crushed ice and in the froth of water.

X X X I *Dew is a thin vapor (or else the air itself) attracted by the leaves of plants and condensed into water by their coldness.*

For it is found nowhere except upon plants, and specifically in the heat of summer when the plants are colder than the air itself. This results in a great benefit for the plants; for by that means they are moistened during the very driest time of the year. Therefore, dew is also produced in those countries that experience no rain.

X X X II *Frost is frozen <note>original: congealed</note> dew.*

Therefore, there is none except in winter, when cold reigns due to the sun's absence.

### *Of Fiery Meteors*

Fiery meteors are those which arise from fatty fumes kindled in the air. There are seven principal kinds: a falling star, a flying dragon, lightning, flying sparks, *ignis fatuus* <note>Latin: "foolish fire"; a light seen over marshy ground, often called a will-o'-the-wisp</note>, a torch, and *ignis lambens* <note>Latin: "licking fire"; a harmless glow sometimes seen on people or animals</note>.

XXXIII A falling star is a fatty and sticky <note>original: "viscous"</note> fume, kindled at its upper end by <term>antiperistasis</term> <note>original: "antiperistafis"; a term in natural philosophy describing the intensification of a quality when surrounded by its opposite, such as heat being compressed and ignited by surrounding cold</note>. The flame follows its fuel and is carried downward until the fuel runs out and the fire is extinguished.

These can be seen every clear night, and more often in winter than in summer. You may see a similar effect if you relight the fatty smoke of an extinguished candle by holding another flame above it. This falling star is made of a thick <note>original: "grosse"</note> vapor; because of its thickness, it holds together like a cord. Therefore, it burns so intensely that if it falls upon a person, it burns through their clothing. Whichever direction it moves, it predicts that wind will come from that part of the sky.

XXXIV A flying dragon is a long, thick, fatty fume that is elevated in all its parts. Because of this, when it is kindled, it does not shoot downward but moves sideways like a dragon or a sparkling beam.

<summary>This page defines fiery meteors as ignited fatty vapors and provides detailed descriptions of "falling stars" and "flying dragons," explaining their physical composition and behavior.</summary>
<keywords>fiery meteors, falling star, flying dragon, antiperistasis, meteorology, vapors, ignis fatuus, ignis lambens</keywords>

This meteor is not seen very often; therefore, those who are ignorant of its natural causes think that the Devil is flying.

XXXV ***Lightning is fire ignited within a cloud. In escaping from the opposing cold, it breaks out with a horrible noise and usually casts its flame as far as the earth.***

The world is nature’s <term>alembic</term> <note>original: "alembick"; an apparatus used in distilling, consisting of a gourd-like container and a cap</note>, and the air is the cap of this still. The sun provides the fire; the earth, water, minerals, plants, and so on are the materials which, being heated by this fire, perpetually exhale vapors upward. 

Thus, vapors of salt, sulfur, nitre, and other substances ascend. When these are wrapped up in clouds, they produce various effects. For example, *when sulfurous exhalations are mixed with nitrous ones* (the first being of an extremely hot nature, the second extremely cold), they tolerate each other only until the sulfur catches fire. 

But as soon as that happens, the same effect immediately follows as in gunpowder (which is composed of the same mixture of sulfur and nitre): a conflict, a bursting <note>original: "rapture"; here meaning a sudden breaking or eruption</note>, a noise, and a violent casting forth of the matter. It is from this that a sticky, flaming substance is thrown out, which immediately ignites anything flammable it touches. Striking into the earth, it turns

**XXXVI Flying sparks** are a sulfurous <note>original: "sulphury"</note> fume scattered into many small parts and ignited.

These are seldom seen, as are those that follow.

**XXXVII *Ignis fatuus*** <note>Latin: "foolish fire," commonly known as a will-o'-the-wisp</note> is a fatty and viscous fume which, because of its thickness <note>original: "grosseness"</note>, does not rise far from the earth. Once ignited, it wanders here and there, sometimes leading travelers off their path and into danger.

**XXXVIII A torch** is a similar fume, but thin, and therefore rises upward. Once ignited, it burns for a while like a candle or lamp.

**XXXIX *Ignis lambens*** <note>Latin: "licking fire," a harmless atmospheric glow sometimes observed around people or animals</note> is a fatty exhalation coming from a living body that has been heated through movement, which ignites at the head or nearby.

It sometimes happens to men and horses who are breathing heavily after running, that the hot <note>original: "ardent"</note> vapors they exhale are turned into flames.

XL *Chasma* <note>Greek: "opening" or "abyss"</note> (a pit) is the hollowness of a cloud, making a show of a great hole.

It is caused by a shadow in the midst of a cloud, the extremities of which are enlightened. You may see something similar at night by placing a candle against a wall that has any hollowness in it, even if the wall is whitish.

XLI *Halo* <note>Greek: "threshing floor," referring to its circular shape</note> (a floor) is a luminous circle, formed when the vapors underneath the sun or moon are illustrated <note>original: "illustrated"; meaning illuminated or made bright</note> with the rays of the luminary.

You may see the same at night in a bath, or any other vaporous place, around a burning candle. It is most often seen under the moon because the sun, with his stronger rays, either penetrates or dissipates the cloud.

XLII *Parhelion* <note>original: "Parelius"; from the Greek for "beside the sun"</note> (a false sun) is the representation of the sun upon a bright cloud placed by its side.

In the same manner, if you stand upon the opposite bank of a river, you shall see two suns: the one, the true one in the sky <note>original: "heaven"</note>, and the other reflected in the water. There are sometimes three suns seen, if two of

**XLIII** *Paraselene (a false moon) is the image of the moon reflected in the same way upon a neighboring <note>original: collateral</note> cloud.*

**XLIV** *Rods are sunbeams covered by a cloud, yet shining through the thin parts of the cloud and stretching toward the earth like rods.* <note>These are likely what we now call crepuscular rays or "Jacob's ladders."</note>

**XLV** *Colours are the various hues that appear in a cloud, depending on how it is positioned relative to the sun and to us; thus, the cloud sometimes appears yellow, and sometimes red and fiery.*

**XLVI** *Finally, the Rainbow is a halo appearing opposite the sun or moon in a dewy cloud, representing a bow of various colors.*

For there are lunar rainbows also. It is clear that the rainbow is an optical phenomenon <term>appearing meteor</term> <note>In the Early Modern period, a "meteor" referred to any atmospheric phenomenon (weather, light, or orbs), not just falling rocks from space.</note>, simply because it moves backward and forward along with the eye of the observer. *Lunar Rainbows* are only pale, like a *Halo*. These colors are arranged <note>original: "coordinate"</note> just as can be seen in a *crystal prism*. Certainly, the rainbow *was given for this very purpose: so that we might learn to observe and think about the nature of colors*. ### *Of Watery Concretions.* <note>original: "Concretes"; here referring to discrete or solidified forms of water.</note>

XLVII *Watery concretions are: a bubble, foam, ice, and several appearances in the water; also the saltiness of the sea, spring waters, and medicinal waters.*

XLVIII *A bubble is a thin watery skin filled with air.*

It is created when a small portion of air, pushed down below the water, is carried upward. Because the water is somewhat thicker <note>original: "fatter"</note> on its surface <note>original: "superficies"</note>, it does not allow the air to escape immediately, but instead covers it with a thin skin like a little bladder. The more oily the water is, the longer the bubbles last. This can be seen in those playful <note>original: "ludicrous"; from the Latin 'ludus' meaning play</note> round bubbles that boys often blow from water and soap, which fly through the air for a long time without breaking. From the bubble, we learn to what a degree of thinness <note>original: "subtilty"</note> water can be reduced. For the skin of a bubble is a thousand times thinner than the thinnest paper.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural phenomena, following descriptions of various states of water and leading into a discussion on the composition of the sea.</meta>

(139)

XLIX *Foam is a collection of very small bubbles, raised by the sudden falling of water into water.*

The beating of the water into small parts causes whiteness in the foam; just as ice, wax, pitch, and other things appear whitish when they are beaten. The durability of foam is also greater in an oily liquor, such as in beer, etc.

L *Ice is water hardened together by cold.*

LI *Watery impressions are images of clouds, of birds flying over, of men, of trees, and of any objects placed before them.*

It is known that water is the first mirror, receiving the images of all things: this is due to the evenness of its surface <note>original: "superficies"</note>. For light, colored by objects and falling upon the water, cannot be dispersed (as happens in another body with a rough surface); instead, because of the water's exceeding evenness, the light is entirely reflected and presents itself whole as an image to the eye of the beholder. This is the foundation of all mirrors. But let us come to the actual solid formations <note>original: "reall concretions"</note> in the water.

LII *The saltiness of the sea comes from subterranean fire, which, by heating a bituminous matter <note>original: "bituminous matter"; likely referring to mineral pitch or asphalt-like substances believed to exist beneath the seabed</note>, spreads salt exhalations through the sea.*

--- Page 298 ---
<page-num>(140)</page-num>
<meta>The previous page likely concluded a discussion on the properties of water or the initial formation of the earth's features, leading into the specific characteristics of the sea.</meta>

Saltiness, which is somewhat bitter and possesses a kind of oiliness <note>original: "oleosity"</note>, was given to the sea for three reasons: 1. So that the waters might not putrefy <term>putrefy</term>: rot or decay and produce a foul smell. 2. For the more suitable nourishment of fish. 3. For the strength to carry the heavy burdens of ships. 

Now, the sea is salty, but not (as Aristotle thought) because of the sun's beams extracting the thinner parts of the water and scorching the rest. (If that were true, our own fires would do the same, as would the sun when shining on lakes and pools, yet neither happens. Indeed, the more salt water is heated by our fire, the saltier it becomes; but fresh water remains fresh). Instead, it is salty because of the heat trapped within the depths <note>original: "bowels"</note> of the earth and the deep. When this heat cannot escape <note>original: "exhale"</note>, it sharply scorches the moisture <note>original: "humour"</note> that is there, so that it turns into a state like urine. We see this very same thing happening in our own bodies (and in all living creatures), for urine and sweat are similarly salty.

### LIII *Spring waters are made of vapors condensed in the caverns of the earth, in the same manner as drops gather on the lids of pots.*

It is certain that a great deep lies beneath the earth, according to *Genesis 7:11* <note>The biblical reference to the "fountains of the great deep" breaking open during the Flood.</note>. That is, a mighty mass of waters spread through the hollows...

<summary>This page discusses the theological and physical reasons for the sea's saltiness, rejecting Aristotle's theories in favor of a theory involving internal terrestrial heat. It also begins to explain the origin of springs as condensed vapor from the "great deep" inside the earth.</summary>
<keywords>saltiness, Aristotle, salinity, Genesis 7:11, condensation, spring water, putrefaction, sea navigation</keywords>

--- Page 299 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the hydrological cycle, specifically how seawater moves through the earth to become freshwater springs.</meta>

...hollows of the earth. The fact that these hollows join with certain gulfs of the ocean is evidence that the depth of the sea in some places is completely unsearchable <note>original: "insearchable," meaning bottomless or impossible to measure</note>. Therefore, just as vapors rise out of the open sea into the air and are transformed into drops that fall as rain, so the subterranean <note>original: "subterrane"</note> waters, being thinned by underground heat, send forth vapors. These vapors, gathered together in the hollows of the earth and collected into drops, flow out wherever a passage is provided for them. And this is what the Scripture says:

> *All rivers enter into the sea, yet the sea does not overflow; unto the place from which the rivers come, they return, so that they may flow again.*
— Ecclesiastes 1:7

From this, we can understand why springs yield fresh water, even though they come from the bitter and salt waters of the sea. Specifically, they reach the spring-head through distillation. For they say that seawater, when distilled (that is, turned first into vapors and then into drops in an <term>alembic</term> <note>An early chemical apparatus used for distillation, typically consisting of two vessels connected by a tube.</note>), loses its saltiness. By the same logic, the deep underground evaporates salt waters and nevertheless sends them out fresh from fountains. And what need is there for more words? For clouds gathered from the vapors...

<summary>This page explains the early scientific theory that rivers and springs are formed by seawater being distilled into freshwater through subterranean heat and evaporation, citing Ecclesiastes as biblical evidence.</summary>
<keywords>hydrology, distillation, subterranean water, Ecclesiastes 1:7, alembic, water cycle, desalination</keywords>

**LIV** *Medicinal waters are made from the various infusions <note>original: "tinctures"</note> of the metals and juices of the earth, from which they receive their healing power <note>original: "virtue"</note> and flavor.*

For example, hot springs or baths are made from bitumen <note>a naturally occurring petroleum substance like asphalt</note> burning within the earth. Therefore, they clearly exhale sulfur. In contrast, acidic <note>original: "sharpish"</note> waters taste of iron, copper, vitriol <note>a sulfate of a metal, often iron or copper</note>, alum <note>a mineral salt used in medicine and dyeing</note>, etc. It is now time to speak of these earthly compounds <note>original: "concretes"</note>.

->*Of earthly compounds, which are called*<-
->*Minerals.*<-

**LV** *Minerals are earthly compounds born from subterranean vapors, such as clods <note>lumps of earth or ore</note>, solidified juices, metals, and stones.*

These are called minerals from the Hebrew words *min* <note>original: מן</note> and *eretz* <note>original: ארץ</note>, as if you were to say "from the earth." They are also called *fossils* <note>at this time, "fossil" referred to anything dug out of the ground</note> because they are dug up. The fact that all these are produced from subterranean vapors and subterranean fire is shown by the example of our own bodies: where blood, yellow bile <note>original: "choler"</note>, phlegm, black bile <note>original: "melancholy"</note>, urine, saliva, fat, flesh, veins, nerves, membranes, cartilage <note>original: "gristles"</note>, bone, etc.—and even kidney stones and gravel—are made from the vapors of food.

<summary>This page explains the origin of medicinal waters and mineral substances, arguing that they are formed by subterranean heat and vapors in a process similar to how the human body produces different tissues and fluids from food.</summary>
<keywords>mineralogy, medicinal waters, hot springs, bitumen, humors, fossils, subterranean vapors, early chemistry, physiology analogy</keywords>

--- Page 301 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the formation of the body and transitions into the generation of minerals within the earth.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>

food processed <note>original: "concocted"</note> and digested, as will be seen hereafter. Just as these parts of our bodies are formed within the body by internal heat, so minerals are generated in the depths of the earth, and nowhere else. For the earth—with its deepest passages and veins winding in every direction, where infinite vapors are generated and perpetually distilled in a thousand ways—is the great workshop of God. Within it, throughout so many ages, such things are created that neither art can imitate nor human intellect <note>original: "wit"</note> fully understand.

LVI *Clods are dug earths, permeated <note>original: "infected"</note> only with oiliness <note>original: "fatnesse"</note> or some color, and prone to being soaked, such as:* 1. Clay. 2. Marl <note>a soil consisting of clay and lime</note>. 3. Chalk. 4. Red earth. 5. Paints or painters' colors (*such as lake, vermilion, ochre, azure or blue, and verdigris <note>a green pigment formed on copper</note>*). 6. Fuller's earth <note>a clay used for cleaning wool and absorbing fats</note>, in Greek, <term>smektis</term> <note>original: σμῆκτις, meaning cleansing-earth</note>. 7. Medicinal earth, such as sealed earth <note>Terra sigillata, medicinal clay pressed into cakes and stamped with a seal</note>, *Lemnian, Armenian, Samian, etc.*

These colors seem to be nothing other than the soot of subterranean fumes, distilled in various ways; and those earths are nothing but various mixtures of liquids, also distilled in various ways and brought to a specific quality.

LVII *Solidified <note>original: "concrete"</note> juices are minerals <note>original: "fossiles"</note> endowed with*

with a flavor, or some *sharp quality* <note>original: "virtue"</note>, easily dissolved or ignited; such as *sulfur*, *nitre* <note>potassium nitrate, used in gunpowder</note>, *salt*, *alum*, *vitriol* <note>sulfuric acid or its related sulfate minerals</note>, *arsenic* (which painters call orpiment), *antimony* or *stibium*, and *similar substances*.

  N. These juices seem to be nothing else but *the essence* <note>original: "cream"</note> *of subterranean liquids* distilled in various ways.

  LVIII *Metals* are *watery fossils* <note>In the 17th century, "fossil" referred to anything dug out of the earth, not just organic remains</note>, easily melted, cast, and hammered: such as *gold*, *silver*, *brass* (or copper), *iron*, *tin*, *lead*, and *mercury* <note>original: "quick-silver"</note>.

  N. 1. That they are generated from fire is proven by the fact that they are often taken hot out of the veins <note>mineral lodes or deposits</note>, so hot that they cannot be touched. For in winter, when all herbs are white with frost, those which grow over the veins do not frost over because the hot vapors within prevent freezing <note>original: "concretion"</note>. Similarly, trees show the location of metal veins by the blue tint of their leaves.

  2. Now, regarding the idea that metals are made of vapors, there is an argument that they are also commonly created right in the clouds. Indeed, even in our own time, there are known examples of brass or iron bodies of significant weight falling from the heavens <note>likely a reference to meteorites</note>.

  3. That metals are made of watery vapors,

...pours. Their liquidity shows [this]; they are now solidified by the power of salt. Therefore, the slag <note>original: "drosse"</note> of iron is salty and bitter.

4. Mercury <note>original: "Quicksilver"</note> alone is always liquid and never solid <note>original: "consistent"</note>, serving as a perpetual witness to the watery nature of metals. Other metals float upon it because it has the most densely compacted substance of all, except for gold; therefore, gold is the only metal it absorbs into itself.

5. Whether metals differ in their actual species, or only in their degree of purity, hardness, and heat, we will leave undecided for now.

*59. Stones are earthly minerals <note>In the 17th century, "fossils" referred to anything dug out of the earth.</note> that are hard and compacted, and are only capable of being broken into pieces.*

Bricks and pottery teach us that stones are earth solidified with water and fire, for in this case, art imitates nature. Yet the various forms of stones show that they are not simply hardened earth, but a mass solidified from various very thick <note>original: "grosse"</note> earthly vapors, combined with a varying balance of fluids <note>original: "humours"</note>.

*60. Stones are either common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> or precious.*

*61. A common stone is earth that is very hard and compacted. There are seven principal kinds: the gravel stone, the millstone, the pumice stone, and the flint (to which I include <term>emery</term> <note>original: "Smiris," a mineral used as an abrasive</note>, which is used to cut glass),*

and iron polished the whetstone, and the touchstone <term>a stone used to test the purity of gold and silver</term> (or *Lydian stone* <note>original: Lapis lydius</note>), marble, and the loadstone <term>a naturally magnetized mineral</term>.

N. Every kind has its own differences as well.

2. A great stone is called *saxum* <note>Latin for stone or rock</note> or *a rock*; a small one is called *gravel and sand*.

3. Most mountains are stony (and yield metals) because the subterranean fire—on the third day of Creation—swelled the earth in those places and made for itself many channels and passages. Breathing through these, the fire variously exhales, melts, mixes, and boils the matter; this process does not happen as abundantly beneath the plains.

LXII. *Precious stones are called gems because they are the "gums" of stones, sweating out from the depths <note>original: "bowels"</note> of the earth.*

Their clarity and brightness come from this—that is to say, from their extremely fine and precise filtration <note>original: "straining"</note>, even more so than in the saps <note>original: "gums"</note> of trees; for wood has looser pores than stones.

LXIII. *All gems are transparent and translucent, but some are only transparent, such as these three: the diamond, the crystal, and the beryl. Others are colored as well, and those (according to the diversity of their colors) are of seven sorts.*

1 *Bright and burning*: The Carbuncle <note>original: "Carbuncle"; a term used for deep-red gems, typically garnets or rubies cut into a rounded shape.</note>, the Chalcedony, and the Chrysolite.

2 *Yellow*: The Jacinth <note>An orange-red or yellow variety of zircon.</note> and Topaz.

3 *Green*: The Emerald and the Turquoise.

4 *Red or purple*: The Ruby and the Garnet <note>original: "Granate"</note>; though the Carnelian <note>original: "Carneolus"</note> and the Onyx are paler.

5 *Sky-colored*: The Sapphire and the Amethyst.

6 *Black*: The Morion <note>A dark brown to black variety of smoky quartz.</note>.

7 *Changeable*: Such as the Jasper, the Agate, and the Chrysoprase.

N. 1. Crystal is never found unless it is hexagonal <note>Six-sided.</note>, which is considered a miracle of nature. It has been observed at Hradec Králové <note>original: "Kings Itradeck"</note> in Bohemia in the year 1618 that crystal grows in arched cells underground—dry and sealed places where the wind has not entered for some years. Elegant crystals were found hanging from the stones of the arches like icicles in an exact hexagonal form; however, many more are found in the silver mines of Kutná Hora <note>original: "Catteberge"</note>. We have nothing more to say about other gems in particular.

N. 2. Stones that typically grow within living creatures are usually reckoned—

LXIV *The power <note>original: "virtue"</note> found in minerals is called their natural spirit. There are as many forms of this spirit as there are species of minerals.*

For there is one spirit of salt <note>hydrochloric acid</note>, another of vitriol <note>sulfuric acid</note>, another of loadstone <note>magnetic iron ore</note>, and iron, etc., which distillers know how to extract.

## CHAP. IX.
### Of Plants.

**S**o much for <term>concretes</term> <note>physical substances formed by the union of separate parts; here referring to inorganic matter</note>. Here follow plants, which, besides having a physical form, also possess life.

1 *A plant is a living <note>original: "vitall"</note> concrete object growing out of the earth, such as a tree or an herb.*

Some concretes (such as stars, meteors, and minerals) lack life and remain or stay where they were formed. But plants, endowed with an inward vigor, break out of the earth and spread themselves *across a flat surface* <note>original: "in plano"</note>. This is also why they were called plants <note>The author suggests an etymological link between "plants" and the Latin "planus," meaning flat or level</note>.

<summary>This page transitions from the study of minerals and organic stones to the definition of plants as "living concretes" that possess internal vigor. It explains that unlike minerals, plants grow and spread across the earth's surface.</summary>
<keywords>botany, minerals, bezoar, toadstone, vitriol, alchemy, plant biology, concretes</keywords>

II *Plants are created both to be an ornament*
*to the earth and to yield nourishment,*
*medicine, and other uses to living creatures.*

We have sufficient experience in winter of what a sad face the earth would have if it were not clothed every year with those diverse, colored tapestries of herbs. From where would living creatures get food, medicines, and pleasures if we were deprived of the roots, leaves, seeds, and fruits of plants? This is not even to mention the benefit <note>original: "commodity"</note> of shade and the infinite uses of wood.

III *The essential parts of a plant are the*
*root, the trunk (or stalk), and the branches*
*or leaves.*

<margin>Note well</margin> The elements, vapors, and solid <note>original: "concrete"</note> things consisted only of *similar* <term>similar parts: in traditional philosophy, "homogeneous" substances where every part is the same as the whole (e.g., every drop of water is still water).</term> parts; for every part and particle of water, earth, vapor, a cloud, iron, etc., is called—and is—water, earth, vapor, a cloud, iron, etc. But more perfect bodies (those of plants and living creatures) do consist of *dissimilar parts (that is, members)*, every one of which has both its own function <note>original: "office"</note> and its name, differing from the rest. For example: in a plant, *the root* is the part sticking in the ground and sucking in the juice of the earth; *the trunk* <note>The text ends mid-sentence as the "trunk" is introduced.</note>

<summary>This page explains the aesthetic and practical necessity of plants for life on Earth and introduces the biological distinction between "similar" elements and "dissimilar" organized bodies like plants and animals.</summary>
<keywords>botany, natural philosophy, similar parts, dissimilar parts, plant anatomy, nourishment, medicine, aesthetics of nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of plant anatomy and the biological functions of the "vegetable spirit," specifically focusing on nutrition and the "vegetative soul."</meta>

(or stalks) attracting the sap <note>original: "juice"</note>, processing <note>original: "concocting"</note> it, and sending it to the upper parts. The **boughs and branches** are twigs, distributing the sap—now even better processed—to create seed and fruit; **the leaves** are the coverings of the fruits and boughs.

IV. *The spirit of a plant is called a vegetable or vital spirit; it demonstrates its power <note>original: "virtue"</note> in three ways: through nutrition, growth <note>original: "augmentation"</note>, and reproduction <note>original: "generation"</note>.*

For here, that universal spirit (the spirit of life) begins to show its power more clearly, preparing a portion of matter so readily for its use that it becomes manageable <note>original: "tractable"</note> for performing the functions of life. It is therefore called "vital" in plants because of these more obvious signs and effects of life. They also call it the **vegetative soul**. <note>The "vegetative soul" was a concept in Aristotelian biology referring to the most basic level of life—shared by plants and animals—responsible for growth and reproduction.</note>

V. *Nutrition is an innate power <note>original: "inbred virtue"</note> in a plant, by which it sucks in suitable sap and changes it into its own substance.*

Because the surrounding air dries up every body, and the heat contained within a living body also consumes internal moisture, it would be impossible for a plant not to wither away immediately unless new matter and vigor were continually supplied with fresh...
